In the \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n problem, given a graph \n𝐺\n and an integer \n𝑘\n, the task is to count the number of \n𝑘\n-vertex induced subgraphs \n𝐺\n′\n with \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n′\n)\n=\n1\n. For example, this problem family includes counting \n𝑘\n-cliques or induced \n𝑘\n vertex subgraphs that are connected, among others. There has been extensive work on determining the parameterized complexity of \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n for various properties \nΦ\n. Very recently, Döring, Marx, and Wellnitz [STOC 2024] showed the general result that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard for every nontrivial edge-monotone property \nΦ\n and, assuming ETH, cannot be solved in time \n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n​\n𝑛\n𝑜\n⁡\n(\nlog\n⁡\n𝑘\n)\n.\n\n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n can be naturally generalized to graph parameters, that is, to functions \nΦ\n on graphs that do not necessarily map to \n{\n0\n,\n1\n}\n: now the task is to compute the sum \n∑\n𝐺\n′\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n′\n)\n taken over all \n𝑘\n-vertex induced subgraphs \n𝐺\n′\n. This problem setting can express a wider range of counting problems (for instance, counting \n𝑘\n-cycles or \n𝑘\n-matchings) and can model problems involving expected values (for instance, the expected number of components in a subgraph induced by \n𝑘\n random vertices). Our main results are lower bounds on \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n in this setting, which simplify, generalize, and tighten the lower bounds of Döring, Marx, and Wellnitz in various ways.\n\n1.\n\nWe show a lower bound for every nontrivial edge-monotone graph parameter \nΦ\n with finite codomain\n(not only for parameters that take value in \n{\n0\n,\n1\n}\n).\n\n2.\n\nThe lower bound is tight: we show that, assuming ETH, there is no \n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n​\n𝑛\n𝑜\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n time algorithm.\n\n3.\n\nThe lower bound applies also to the modular counting versions of the problem.\n\n4.\n\nThe lower bound applies also to the multicolored version of the problem.\n\nWe can extend the #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hardness result to the case when the codomain of \nΦ\n is not finite, but has size at most \n(\n1\n−\n𝜀\n)\n​\n𝑘\n on \n𝑘\n-vertex graphs. However, if there is no bound on the size of the codomain, the situation changes significantly: for example, there is a nontrivial edge-monotone function \nΦ\n where the size of the codomain is \n𝑘\n on \n𝑘\n-vertex graphs and \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is FPT.\n\n1Introduction\n\nMany scientific fields deal with data—very often in the form of networks or graphs. To understand said data, it is typically instructive to count how often certain substructures or patterns appear in a given input graph; for instance in the study of database systems [GSS01, FGRZ24], neural and social networks [MSOI+02, SJHS15], and computational biology [SS05, MT08], to name just a few examples. Unfortunately, this pattern analysis becomes an algorithmically challenging task whenever our graphs grow rapidly in size. Therefore, there is already a need for algorithms that are able to count just small patterns efficiently. Hence, a long line of research is devoted to efficiently count certain types of small subgraphs in large graphs [CDM17, CM14, Cur13, RS20, DRSW22, RSW21, RSW23, PRS+21, FR22, GR24, DMW23].\n\nFor example, if we wish to compute a function that depends somehow on patterns that have at most \n𝑘\n vertices in a graph \n𝐺\n with \n𝑛\n vertices, then one could do so by simply considering all induced subgraphs of size \n𝑘\n in \n𝐺\n. However, there are \n(\n𝑛\n𝑘\n)\n many induced subgraphs of size \n𝑘\n, meaning that enumerating all of them would require a running time of \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑛\n𝑘\n)\n which is unfeasible if the pattern size \n𝑘\n becomes too large. Instead, we require an algorithm whose complexity does not explode with our pattern size \n𝑘\n.\n\nWe use concepts from parameterized complexity theory to better understand the complexity of such problems. To be more precise, we would like to know whether our problems are fixed-parameter tractable (FPT), that is, if they can be solved in time \n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n​\n𝑛\n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n1\n)\n for some computable function \n𝑓\n. An algorithm with such a running time might be inefficient for large patterns. However, for small patterns (as they typically occur in practice), an FPT algorithm is efficient even for large input graphs.\n\nAlso, a polynomial running time is often not achievable since many counting problems are NP-complete and it is widely believed that many famous counting problems, such as #Clique are not FPT. The class of #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard problems is defined as the class of problems that are at least as hard as #Clique, meaning that it is very unlikely that such problems are FPT.\n\nA common formalism to unify different counting problem is the following [JM15b, JM15a, FR22, RSW23, DMW23, DRSW22]. Let \nΦ\n be a graph property such as bipartite, clique, or planar; formally, \nΦ\n is a function that maps every graph to \n{\n0\n,\n1\n}\n and closed under isomorphism. Then given a graph \n𝐺\n and integer \n𝑘\n, the task is to count the number of \n𝑘\n-vertex induced subgraphs that satisfy property \nΦ\n, or more formally, to compute the value\n\n\t\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n≔\n∑\n𝐴\n⊆\n(\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n𝑘\n)\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n)\n.\n\t\n\nFor a fixed graph property \nΦ\n, the problem \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is defined as the problem that gets as input an integer \n𝑘\n and a graph \n𝐺\n and computes #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n.\n\nTo express a wider range of problems, it is natural to generalize #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n from graph properties to graph parameters: that is, the codomain of \nΦ\n is not necessarily \n{\n0\n,\n1\n}\n. For example, when \nΦ\n is the number of perfect matchings or the number of Hamiltonian cycles, then #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n is the number of \n𝑘\n-matchings and the number of \n𝑘\n-cycles in \n𝐺\n, respectively. If \nΦ\n is, say, the chromatic number of the graph, then #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n can be interpreted (after dividing by \n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝑘\n)\n) as the expected chromatic number of a subgraph induced by a subset of \n𝑘\n random vertices. Thus the \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n problem allows us to formulate problems involving the expected value of graph parameters over random subsets of vertices.\n\nFor a fixed graph property \nΦ\n, Jerrum and Meeks [JM15a, JM15b] were the first who study the \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n problem. In later years, the \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n problem has attracted a lot of research and it is now known that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard for many classes of graph properties [JM17, CDM17, RS20, RSW23, DRSW22, FR22, DMW23].\n\nFor certain properties like being a complete graph it is easy to see that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard. On the other hand, there are trivial graph parameters for which \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is definitely FPT computable. We say that a graph property or a graph parameter \nΦ\n is trivial on \n𝑘\n if \nΦ\n is constant on \n𝑘\n-vertex graphs. It is obvious that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is easy if \nΦ\n is trivial on all \n𝑘\n.\n\nIt is known that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard for certain \nΦ\n and FPT computable for other \nΦ\n. An implicit result by Curticapean, Dell, and Marx (see [CDM17]) shows that for all graph properties \nΦ\n the problem \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is always either FPT or #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard. This result can easily extend to graph parameters. However, this result does not give any easy criteria to check which case holds. For many large classes of graph properties \nΦ\n it is known that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard (see Section 1.1) and current research suggests that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is always #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard whenever \nΦ\n is a nontrivial graph property (see [FR22, DMW23, Conjecture 1]).\n\nRecently, Döring, Marx and Wellnitz [DMW23] showed that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard for graph properties that are edge monotone (closed under deletion of edges). They also provided some quantitative lower bounds on the exponent of \n𝑛\n under the Exponential-Time Hypothesis (ETH) [IPZ01]. We extend their results in the following four ways.\n\n(1)\n\nWe show #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hardness for nontrivial edge-monotone graph parameters with finite codomain. Here, edge-monotone means that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n≥\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n whenever \n𝐻\n is an edge-subgraph of \n𝐺\n. This extends the main result of [DMW23] to the much larger space of graph parameters. However, we also show in Section 5 that there are nontrivial edge-monotone graph parameters with unbounded codomain that are FPT.\n\n(2)\n\nWe show that \n#\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n has tight lower bounds under ETH. In [DMW23], the authors archived tight lower bounds only for some special cases (like \nΦ\n is nontrivial on infinitely many prime powers \n𝑝\n𝑡\n).\n\n(3)\n\nWe show that \n#\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n stays hard under modulo counting.\n\n(4)\n\nWe show that (1) to (3) also hold if we consider the colored version \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n of \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n, where the graph \n𝐺\n is partitioned into \n𝑘\n classes in the input, and we are considering only induced \n𝑘\n-vertex subgraphs with exactly one vertex in each class.\n\nFinite Codomain.\n\nOur first main result generalizes and tightens the result of Döring, Marx and Wellnitz [DMW23] by considering graph parameters with arbitrary finite codomain (not only \n{\n0\n,\n1\n}\n) and making the lower bound tight in all cases.\n\n{restatable*}\n\nmtheoremthmedgemon Let \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℚ\n denote some edge-monotone graph parameter with finite codomain.\n\n• \n\nIf \nΦ\n is nontrivial, then \n#\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n (and \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n) is \n#\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard.\n\n• \n\nFurther, assuming ETH, if the codomain of \nΦ\n is equal to \n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑐\n}\n then there is a universal constant \n𝛿\n𝑐\n>\n0\n (that only depends on the size of the codomain \n𝑐\n) such that for any \n𝑘\n≥\n3\n on which \nΦ\n is nontrivial, no algorithm (that reads the whole input) computes for every graph \n𝐺\n the number #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n (or \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n) in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛿\n𝑐\n​\n𝑘\n)\n.\n\nAn important feature of edge-monotone graph parameters is that it is very well possible that the parameter is nontrivial only on graphs of certain sizes. This can be contrasted with the case of, say, hereditary graph classes, where if the class is nontrivial on sizes \n𝑘\n1\n and \n𝑘\n2\n, then it is nontrivial on all sizes in between.\n\nGiven an edge-monotone graph parameter \nΦ\n, let \n𝑀\nΦ\n contain every size \n𝑘\n such that \nΦ\n is nontrival on \n𝑘\n-vertex graphs. The proof of Döring, Marx and Wellnitz [DMW23] considers two main cases and handles them with very different techniques: (1) when \n𝑀\nΦ\n contains arbitrary large primes \n𝑝\n and (2) when \n𝑀\nΦ\n contains arbitrary large prime powers \n𝑝\n𝑡\n for some fixed prime \n𝑝\n. The proof is completed with a delicate reduction: if \n𝑘\n∈\n𝑀\nΦ\n and \n𝑝\n𝑡\n is the largest prime factor of \n𝑘\n, then a nontrivial edge-monotone problem on \n𝑝\n𝑡\n vertices can be reduced to computing #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n. While this proves #W[1]-hardness, the largest prime power \n𝑝\n𝑡\n dividing \n𝑘\n can be as small as logarithmic in \n𝑘\n, resulting in weak quantitative lower bounds.\n\nOur main technical contribution is a more refined reduction technique that allows reducing a problem on \n𝑝\n𝑡\n vertices to computing #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n, where \n𝑝\n𝑡\n is now the largest prime power not larger than \n𝑘\n (and not necessarily dividing \n𝑘\n). This allows us to completely bypass case (1) above an reuse (2) essentially verbatim, resulting in a much simpler proof.\n\nInfinite Codomain.\n\nThe behavior of the \n#\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n problem can change significantly if \nΦ\n has infinite domain. Apparently, the behavior of the problem is influenced by how large the codomain can be on \n𝑘\n-vertex graphs. We can extend the #W[1]-hardness of Section 1 to the more general case when the codomain of \nΦ\n on \n𝑘\n-vertex graphs is of size at most \n(\n1\n−\n𝜀\n)\n​\n𝑘\n. However, the quantitative lower bound on the exponent becomes weaker. Here, \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n𝑘\n→\n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n}\n means that the codomain of \nΦ\n is \n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n}\n when restricted to \n𝑘\n-vertex graphs.\n\n{restatable*}\n\nmtheoremthmedgemonDomain Let \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n𝑘\n→\n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n}\n be an edge-monotone graph parameter. Further, let \n𝛼\n∈\nNature\n with \n𝛼\n≥\n2\n and \n𝜀\n>\n0\n such that \n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n=\n⌊\n(\n1\n−\n𝜀\n)\n​\n𝑘\n1\n/\n𝛼\n⌋\n.\n\n• \n\nIf \nΦ\n is nontrivial then \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n and \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n are both \n#\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard.\n\n• \n\nThere exists a constant \n𝛿\n>\n0\n (that only depends on \n𝛼\n and \n𝜀\n) such that for all nontrivial \n𝑘\n≥\n3\n, no algorithm (that reads the whole input) computes the function #\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n (or \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n) in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛿\n​\n𝑘\n𝛽\n)\n, where \n𝛽\n≔\n1\n−\n1\n/\n𝛼\n.\n\nWhen the codomain of \nΦ\n on \n𝑘\n-vertex graphs is \n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑘\n}\n, then it is no longer true that \n#\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is hard for every edge-monotone graph parameter \nΦ\n. A simple example is when \nΦ\n is the number of isolated vertices, in which case an easy formula gives a formula for #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n. In contrast, we show that our techniques can be used to obtain hardness results for specific graph parameters with unbounded codomains. In Theorem 3.10, we show hardness for \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n for some common graph parameters like the number of connected components, chromatic number and maximal degree.\n\nBeyond the example of isolated vertices above, there seem to be many nontrivial graph parameters for which \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is FPT. For example, in Section 5 we show that, for every fixed \n𝑐\n≥\n1\n, \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n𝑐\n)\n is FPT, where \nΦ\n𝑐\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n𝑐\n. Thus a simple analog of Section 1 for edge-monotone \nΦ\n with infinite codomain is out of question. In Section 5, we present a large class of nontrivial graph parameters \nΦ\n for which \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is FPT. This class could be a conjectured candidate for the easy cases in an eventual dichotomy generalizing Section 1 to arbitrary edge-monotone \nΦ\n. We define this class to contain every \nΦ\n that is a a linear combination of subgraph counts of a set of graphs with bounded vertex cover number. The idea of using different bases to analyze counting problems was originally used in [CDM17].\n\nFinally, another interesting aspect of unbounded codomain is that it is no longer true that \n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n and \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n have the same complexity. As an example, let \nΦ\n be the number of perfect matchings. Then \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is the problem of counting \n𝑘\n-matchings, which is well known to be #W[1]-hard. On the other hand, given a graph \n𝐺\n partitioned into \n𝑘\n classes, counting \n𝑘\n-matchings where each of vertices contains exactly one endpoint of the matching can be done in FPT time, showing that \n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is FPT.\n\nModulo Counting.\n\nIn Section 6, we further extend the results of [DMW23] to modulo counting. For a fixed prime number \n𝑝\n and a counting problem \n#\n​\n𝐴\n, we define \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝐴\n as the problem that decides on input \n𝑥\n whether \n#\n​\n𝑃\n​\n(\n𝑥\n)\n is divisible by \n𝑝\n. Further, we define \n#\n​\n𝐴\n𝑝\n as the problem that computes \n𝐴\n⁡\n(\n𝑥\n)\nmod\n𝑝\n. If \n#\n​\n𝐴\n is parameterized by \n𝜅\n, then \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝐴\n and \n#\n​\n𝐴\n𝑝\n are both parameterized by \n𝜅\n.\n\nWe say that a problem \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝐴\n is \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard if there is a parameterized Turing reduction from \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nClique\n to \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝐴\n. Further, we say that a problem \n#\n​\n𝐴\n𝑝\n is \n#\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard if there is a parameterized Turing reduction from \n#\n​\nClique\n𝑝\n to \n#\n​\n𝐴\n𝑝\n. Observe that there is always an easy Turing reduction from \n#\n​\n𝐴\n𝑝\n to \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝐴\n (simply return true if the output of \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝐴\n​\n(\n𝑥\n)\n is zero).\n\nThe complexity of modulo counting gives us additional information about the hardness of our counting problem. For example, it is well-known that computing the permanent of a matrix is \n#\n​\n𝑃\n-complete (see [Val79]), thus there is probably no algorithm that can compute the permanent in polynomial time. However, observe that the permanent modulo \n2\n is equal to the determinant modulo \n2\n. Hence, this value can be computed in polynomial time. Hence, a counting problem that is \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard or \n#\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard for all prime numbers \n𝑝\n can therefore be regarded as a particularly hard counting problem. We show that this is the case for nontrivial edge-monotone graph parameters. Further, we show tight bounds assuming the randomized version of ETH (rETH).\n\n{restatable*}\n\nmtheoremthmedgemonMOD Let \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℤ\n denote an edge-monotone graph parameter with finite codomain and \n𝑝\n be a prime number.\n\n• \n\nIf \nΦ\n is nontrivial, then \n#\n​\nIndSub\n𝑝\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n and \n#\n​\ncp-IndSub\n𝑝\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n are both \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard and \n#\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard.\n\n• \n\nFurther, assuming rETH, if the codomain of \nΦ\n is equal to \n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑐\n}\n, with \n𝑐\n<\n𝑝\n, then there is a universal constant \n𝛿\n𝑐\n>\n0\n (that only depends on the size of the codomain \n𝑐\n) such that for any \n𝑘\n≥\n3\n on which \nΦ\n is nontrivial, no algorithm (that reads the whole input) solves for every graph \n𝐺\n the problem \n#\n​\nIndSub\n𝑝\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n (or \n#\n​\ncp-IndSub\n𝑝\n​\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n) in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛿\n𝑐\n​\n𝑘\n)\n.\n\nIf we apply Section 1 to a nontrivial edge-montone graph parameter \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\n{\n0\n,\n1\n}\n, then we obtain that the computation of \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n modulo any prime number \n𝑝\n is still hard.\n\n{corollaryq}\n\n[] Let \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\n{\n0\n,\n1\n}\n denote some edge-monotone graph property and \n𝑝\n be a prime number.\n\n• \n\nIf \nΦ\n is nontrivial, then \n#\n​\nIndSub\n𝑝\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n (and \n#\n​\ncp-IndSub\n𝑝\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n) is \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard and \n#\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard.\n\n• \n\nFurther, assuming rETH, universal constant \n𝛿\n>\n0\n (independent of \nΦ\n) such that for any prime number p and integer \n𝑘\n≥\n3\n, on which \nΦ\n is nontrivial, no algorithm (that reads the whole input) solves for every graph \n𝐺\n the problem \n#\n​\nIndSub\n𝑝\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n (and \n#\n​\ncp-IndSub\n𝑝\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n) in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛿\n​\n𝑘\n)\n.\n\nObserve that Section 1 does not follow directly from the results of [DMW23]. Imagine that we have an edge-monotone graph property \nΦ\n that is nontrivial only on prime numbers. Then for this case, the authors of [DMW23] rely on the hardness of \n#\nHom\n​\n(\nℋ\n)\n for a class of graphs \nℋ\n with unbounded treewidth (see [DMW23, Theorem 5.16]). However, observe that \n#\nHom\n​\n(\nℋ\n)\n might be trivial modulo \n𝑝\n.\n\nFor example, fix a prime number \n𝑝\n and let \nℋ\n≔\n{\n𝐾\n𝑛\n:\n𝑛\n≥\n2\n}\n. Now, for all \n𝑛\n≥\n𝑝\n, we have\n\n\t\n#\nHom\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑛\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n𝑛\n!\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑛\n→\n𝐺\n)\n≡\n𝑝\n0\n.\n\t\n\nThis implies that \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nHom\n​\n(\nℋ\n)\n is not \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard although \n#\nHom\n​\n(\nℋ\n)\n is \n#\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard. Hence, we cannot rely on the #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hardness of \n#\nHom\n​\n(\nℋ\n)\n to show the \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hardness of \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n. The parameterized complexity of \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nHom\n​\n(\nℋ\n)\n was analyzed in [PRS+21, Theorem 1.8].\n\n1.1Prior Work\n\nIn this section, we summarize recent results for the parameterized complexity of the \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n problem. We order the results by their date of first announcement.\n\n(a)\n\nThe problem \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n was first studied for some specific graph parameters \nΦ\n. It was shown that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard for\n\n(i)\n\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n1\n if and only if \n𝐺\n is connected [JM15a]\n\n(ii)\n\nΦ\n has a low edge densities [JM15b]\n\n(iii)\n\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n1\n if and only if \n|\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n is even, \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n1\n if and only if \n|\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n is odd (both in [JM17])\n\n(b)\n\nIn [DRSW22], Dörfler, Roth, Schmitt, and Wellnitz proved that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard if there are infinitely many prime powers \n𝑡\n such that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑡\n,\n𝑡\n)\n≠\nΦ\n⁡\n(\nIS\n2\n​\n𝑡\n)\n. We rely heavily on their techniques to prove our results.\n\n(c)\n\nIn [RSW23], Roth, Schmitt, and Wellnitz proved that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard if \nΦ\n is monotone, meaning closed under taking subgraphs.\n\n(d)\n\nIn [FR22], Focke and Roth proved that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard whenever \nΦ\n is a nontrivial and hereditary graph property. A say that \nΦ\n is hereditary if it is closed under vertex-deletion, meaning that if \n𝐺\n satisfies \nΦ\n, then each induced subgraph of \n𝐺\n also satisfies \nΦ\n.\n\n(e)\n\nLastly in [DMW23], Döring, Marx and Wellnitz showed that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard if \nΦ\n is nontrivial and edge-monotone. In this work, we refine their work by simplifying, improving, and extending their main results.\n\nFor modulo counting the following results are known.\n\n(a)\n\nBeigel, Gill, and Hertrampf were the first who considered the decision problem \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝐴\n in [BGH90] by introducing the complexity class \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝑃\n (the set of problems \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝐴\n that can be solved in polynomial time). The showed the surprising result that \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝑃\n=\nMOD\n𝑝\n𝑡\n​\n𝑃\n. There work was later extended by Hertrampf in [Her90].\n\n(b)\n\nFaben introduced in [Fab08] the idea of modulo counting by introducing \n#\n​\n𝐴\n𝑝\n. He used this class to give a dichotomy result for the Generalised Satisfiability Problem modulo \n𝑝\n.\n\n(c)\n\nIn [DRSW22], Dörfler, Roth, Schmitt, and Wellnitz proved that \n#\n​\nIndSub\n𝑝\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is \n#\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard if there are infinitely many prime powers \n𝑡\n such that \nΦ\n(\n𝐾\n𝑡\n,\n𝑡\n)\n≢\n𝑝\nΦ\n(\nIS\n2\n​\n𝑡\n)\n. Note that they used \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n to denote \n#\n​\nIndSub\n𝑝\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n.\n\n(d)\n\nIn [PRS+21], Peyerimhoff, Roth, Schmitt, Stix, and Vdovina analyzed the complexity of \n#\n​\nHom\n𝑝\n​\n(\nℋ\n)\n for recursively enumerable classes of graphs \nℋ\n. They showed that \n#\n​\nHom\n𝑝\n​\n(\nℋ\n)\n is \n#\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard if and only if the class \nℋ\n𝑝\n∗\n has unbounded treewidth. Otherwise, the problem is FPT solvable.\n\n(e)\n\nIn [BK22], Bulatov, and Kazeminia analyzed the complexity of the problem \n#\n​\nGraphHom\n𝑝\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n that for a fixed graph \n𝐻\n and an input graph \n𝐺\n asks to compute the number \n#\nHom\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n→\n𝐻\n)\nmod\n𝑝\n. They complete classified when \n#\n​\nGraphHom\n𝑝\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n is \n#\n​\n𝑃\n𝑝\n-complete for square-free graphs \n𝐻\n.\n\nBesides the aforementioned results, there is a lot of research specifically about the complexity class \nMOD\n2\n​\n𝑃\n which is also know as parity P and is usually denoted by \n⊕\n𝑃\n (see [PZ83, FGRZ21, GR24]).\n\n1.2Technical Overview\n\nThis section gives an overview of the techniques and ideas that we use to prove our main theorems. We start by discussing techniques that allow us to get hardness results and lower bounds under ETH.\n\nThe Alternating Enumerator and Reductions\n\nWe start by considering the Clique problem that is \n#\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard by definition and has tight lower bounds under ETH (see Section 3). Next, we construct a reduction from Clique to \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n that preserves the tight lower bounds. Later, we use a reduction from \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n to \n#\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n. Such reductions were first developed for graph properties in [DRSW22] and later refined in [DMW23]. We present the reduction for graph parameters in Section 3.\n\nOur reduction relies on the crucial observation that we can find, for each edge-monotone graph parameter \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℚ\n, graphs whose alternating enumerator is nonvanishing, where the alternating enumerator is defined as\n\n\t\nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n≔\n∑\n𝑆\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n{\n𝑆\n}\n)\n​\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝑆\n.\n\t\n\nWe say that a graph \n𝐺\n is nonvanishing if the alternating enumerator \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n is not zero. The graph parameter \nΦ\n is always clear from context. The alternating enumerator was originally introduced in [DRSW22] and later used in [DMW23].\n\nThe main observation of Section 3 is that we can solve the \nℓ\n-Clique problem by using an oracle for #\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n whenever we can find a nonvanishing graph \n𝐻\n that has \n𝑘\n vertices that contains \n𝐾\nℓ\n,\nℓ\n as a subgraph (see Theorem 3.5). Thus, to get tight bounds, we try to find nonvanishing graphs \n{\n𝐻\n𝑘\n}\n that contain bicliques of unbounded size. This leads to the main result of Section 3.\n\n{restatable*}\n\ncorollarycortightbounds Let \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℚ\n be a graph parameter and \n0\n<\n𝜓\n≤\n1\n. Assume that there is a set of graphs \n𝐴\n≔\n{\n𝐺\n𝑘\n}\n and a function \nℎ\n:\n𝐴\n→\nNature\n such that each \n𝐺\n𝑘\n has \n𝑘\n vertices, contains \n𝐾\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑘\n)\n,\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑘\n)\n as a subgraph and has a nonvanishing alternating enumerator.\n\n• \n\nIf \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n∈\n𝜔\n⁡\n(\n1\n)\n, then the problem \n#\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n (and \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n) is \n#\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard.\n\n• \n\nIf \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n∈\nΩ\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝜓\n)\n, then there is a global constant \n𝛾\n>\n0\n such that for each fixed \n𝑘\n=\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑗\n)\n|\n, no algorithm (that reads the whole input) that for every \n𝐺\n computes #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n (or \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n) in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛾\n​\n𝑘\n𝜓\n)\n, unless ETH fails. Here, the constant \n𝛾\n depends on \nℎ\n but is independent of \nΦ\n and \n𝑘\n.\n\n• \n\nIf \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n∈\nΩ\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝜓\n)\n, then there is no algorithm (that reads the whole input) that for every \n𝐺\n computes #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n (or \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n) in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝑜\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n𝜓\n)\n)\n for all computable functions \n𝑓\n unless ETH fails.\n\nComputing the Alternating Enumerator using Fixed Points\n\nWhen using the alternating enumerable, we have the problem that the computation of this alternating sum over all edge subgraphs is highly nontrivial. Nevertheless, there are some cases where the computation of \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n is straightforward. For example, assume that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n=\n𝑎\n and \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n′\n)\n=\n𝑏\n for all proper edge-subgraphs \n𝐻\n′\n of \n𝐻\n. Now, using the binomial theorem, it is easy to see that \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n=\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n​\n(\n𝑏\n−\n𝑎\n)\n. Of course, this observation is only useful in a few special cases.\n\nThus, we rely on techniques that were originally developed in [DRSW22] and later refined in [DMW23]. These techniques compute the alternating enumerator modulo a prime number \n𝑝\n by using the fixed points of a specific group action that is defined using \n𝑝\n-subgroup of the automorphism group of \n𝐾\n𝑛\n. A \n𝑝\n-group is a group whose order is a \n𝑝\n-power. Let \nΓ\n⊆\nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n be \n𝑝\n-subgroup of the automorphism group \nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n of \n𝐺\n, then we say that an edge-subgraph \n𝐴\n is an fixed point of \n𝐻\n with respect to \nΓ\n if \n𝑔\n​\n𝐴\n=\n𝐴\n for all \n𝑔\n∈\nΓ\n, where \n𝑔\n​\n𝐴\n is the graph with vertex set \n𝐴\n and edge set \n{\n{\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑢\n)\n,\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑣\n)\n}\n:\n{\n𝑢\n,\n𝑣\n}\n∈\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n}\n. We use \nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n to denote the set of all fixed points. If \n𝐴\n and \n𝐵\n are two fixed points with respect to the same group \nΓ\n and \n𝐵\n is an edge-subgraph of \n𝐴\n, then we say that \n𝐵\n is a sub-point of \n𝐴\n. This is sometimes denoted by \n𝐵\n⊆\n𝐴\n. Now, we exploit that these fixed points form a lattice structure that we can analyze. A complete introduction to this topic can be found in Section A.1.\n\nThe advantage of using fixed points is that they allow us to compute \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n modulo a prime number \n𝑝\n by considering only the fixed points of this specific group action (see Lemma A.2). Usually, the number fixed is much lower than the number subgraphs. Further, the set of fixed points has a lot of structure that helps us with our analyses. Now, similar to the example given at the beginning of this section, we can consider the case that we have a fixed point \n𝐴\n with \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n=\n𝑎\n such that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐵\n)\n=\n𝑏\n for all fixed points \n𝐵\n that lie in \n𝐴\n. As it turns out, we again get \nΦ\n^\n(\n𝐴\n)\n≡\n𝑝\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n(\n𝑏\n−\n𝑎\n)\n, which is enough to show that \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐴\n)\n is nonvanishing. This is our main tool to show that we have a nonvanishing alternating enumerator.\n\n{restatable*}\n\n[See [DMW23, Lemma 4.8]]lemmalemcompae Let \n𝐻\n denote a graph, let \nΓ\n⊆\nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n denote a \n𝑝\n-group, and let \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑐\n}\n denote a graph parameter with \n𝑐\n<\n𝑝\n. Further, let \n𝐴\n∈\nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n denote a fixed point with \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n=\n𝑎\n, such that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐵\n)\n=\n𝑏\n for all of the proper sub-points \n𝐵\n of \n𝐴\n. If \n𝑏\n≠\n𝑎\n, then \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐴\n)\n is nonvanishing and \nΦ\n^\n(\n𝐴\n)\n≢\n𝑝\n0\n.\n\nThe Main Steps of the Proof of Section 1\n\nNow, to prove Section 1, we need to find graphs that contain large bicliques and whose alternating enumerator is nonvanishing. However, we cannot always find such graphs. Thus, we rely on additional ideas. The proof works in multiple steps.\n\nThe Prime Power Case: Sylow Groups.\n\nIn Section 4.1, we show that Section 1 is true for the prime power case. Similar to [DMW23], we archive this result by proving that if \nΦ\n is nontrivial on a prime power \n𝑝\n𝑡\n then we can find a nonvanishing graph \n𝐻\n that contains \n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n−\n1\n,\n𝑝\n𝑡\n−\n1\n as a subgraph. This is enough due to Section 1.2. We find the graph \n𝐻\n by analyzing the fixpoints of the \n𝑝\n-sylow group of \nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n)\n (see Section A.2). In Section A.3 we show that these fixed points contain large bicliques.\n\nReducing to the Prime Power Case.\n\nIn order to make use of the prime power case, we have to find a way to go from a nonprime power \n𝑘\n to a prime power \n𝑝\n𝑡\n. This is archived by using inhabited graphs. Inhabited graphs allow us to take a graph \n𝐺\n, a graph \n𝐶\n, and graphs \n𝐻\n2\n,\n…\n​\n𝐻\n𝑠\n and combine them into a larger graph (see [DMW23, Definition 6.1]).\n\n{restatable*}\n\ndefinitiondefinhab For graphs \n𝐺\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐺\n𝑚\n and \n𝐶\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑚\n, we define the inhabited graph \n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐺\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐺\n𝑚\n⟩\n via\n\n\t\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐺\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐺\n𝑚\n⟩\n)\n\t\n≔\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n1\n)\n⊎\n⋯\n⊎\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑚\n)\n\t\n\t\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐺\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐺\n𝑚\n⟩\n)\n\t\n≔\n{\n{\n(\n𝑖\n,\n𝑣\n𝑖\n)\n,\n(\n𝑗\n,\n𝑢\n𝑗\n)\n}\n:\n{\n𝑖\n,\n𝑗\n}\n∈\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n)\n​\n or \n​\n(\n𝑖\n=\n𝑗\n​\n and \n​\n{\n𝑣\n𝑖\n,\n𝑢\n𝑖\n}\n∈\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑖\n)\n)\n}\n.\n\t\t\n\nAssume that a graph parameter \nΦ\n is nontrivial on \n𝑘\n≔\n𝑐\n+\n𝑝\n𝑡\n. Then, we could try to find a graph \n𝐶\n and graphs \n𝐻\n2\n,\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n with \n𝑐\n=\n∑\n𝑖\n=\n2\n𝑠\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n𝑖\n)\n|\n such that the graph parameter \nΦ\n~\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐺\n,\n𝐻\n2\n,\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n⟩\n)\n is nontrivial on \n𝑝\n𝑡\n. Here, we use the fact that the graph \n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐺\n,\n𝐻\n2\n,\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n⟩\n has \n𝑘\n vertices and that \nΦ\n is nontrivial on \n𝑘\n. Also \nΦ\n~\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n is still edge-monotone, and we get a reduction from \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\nΦ\n~\n,\n𝒢\n𝑝\n𝑡\n→\n⋆\n)\n to \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n→\n⋆\n)\n.\n\n{restatable*}\n\nlemmalemincexc Let \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℚ\n denote a graph parameter and suppose that there is an algorithm \n𝔸\n that computes for each graph \n𝐻\n and each \n𝐻\n-colored graph \n𝐺\n the value \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n,\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n)\n for some computable function \n𝑔\n that is monotonically increasing. Further, for a graph \n𝐶\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑠\n and graphs \n𝐻\n2\n,\n𝐻\n3\n,\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n, write \nΦ\n~\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐺\n,\n𝐻\n2\n​\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n⟩\n)\n for the graph parameter that inserts \n𝐺\n into \n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n⋅\n,\n𝐻\n2\n,\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n⟩\n and evaluates the result on \nΦ\n.\n\nThen, there is an algorithm \n𝔹\n with oracle access to \n𝔸\n that computes for each graph \n𝐻\n and each \n𝐻\n-colored graph \n𝐺\n the value \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n~\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑐\n+\n𝑘\n,\n𝑐\n+\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n)\n+\n(\n𝑐\n+\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n)\n2\n)\n, where \n𝑐\n≔\n∑\n𝑖\n=\n2\n𝑠\n#\n​\n𝑉\n​\n(\n𝐻\n𝑖\n)\n. The algorithm \n𝔹\n queries \n𝔸\n only graphs on \n𝐻\n with \n𝑐\n+\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n many vertices.\n\nConcentrated and Reducible Parameters.\n\nIn the following, let \n𝑝\n be a prime number and let \nΦ\n be nontrivial on \n𝑘\n. We now have two techniques to get hardness. Either, we can directly find a graph \n𝐻\n with \n𝑘\n vertices that contains a large enough biclique and has nonvanishing alternating enumerator; or we can find a graph \n𝐶\n and graphs \n𝐻\n2\n,\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n such that \nΦ\n~\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐺\n,\n𝐻\n2\n,\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n⟩\n)\n is nontrivial on \n𝑝\n𝑡\n, where \n𝑘\n=\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n𝑐\n and \n𝑐\n≔\n∑\n𝑖\n=\n2\n𝑠\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n𝑖\n)\n|\n. In order to obtain good lower bounds, we have to ensure that, for the first case, the graph \n𝐻\n contains a large enough biclique. For the second case, we have to ensure that the value \n𝑝\n𝑡\n is large enough (i.e. that we do not lose too much when doing the reduction). Otherwise, our reduction would not propagate the tight lower bounds under ETH. These two approaches motivate the following two definitions.\n\n{restatable*}\n\ndefinitiondefconsca Let \n𝑝\n be a prime number and let \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n𝑘\n→\n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n}\n denote a graph parameter. We say that\n\n• \n\nΦ\n is concentrated on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑝\n𝑡\n if there is a graph \n𝐻\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n with \nΦ\n^\n(\n𝐻\n)\n≢\n𝑝\n0\n and \n𝐻\n contains \n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n𝑝\n𝑡\n as a subgraph.\n\n• \n\nΦ\n is reducible on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑝\n𝑡\n if \n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n<\n𝑝\n and there is a graph \n𝐶\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑠\n and graphs \n𝐻\n2\n,\n…\n,\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n∈\n𝒢\n such that the graph parameter \nΦ\n~\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n≔\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐺\n,\n𝐻\n2\n​\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n⟩\n)\n is nontrivial on \n𝑝\n𝑡\n where \n𝑘\n=\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n∑\n𝑖\n=\n2\n𝑠\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n𝑖\n)\n|\n.\n\nTo obtain lower bounds that are as tight as possible, we need for all \n𝑘\n a prime power \n𝑝\n𝑡\n such that the quotient \n𝑘\n/\n𝑝\n𝑡\n is as small as possible. For this, we use the following lemma that ensures that this is always possible as long as we pick \n𝑝\n𝑡\n carefully.\n\n{restatable*}\n\nlemmalemlargebi Let \n𝑝\n be prime and let \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n𝑘\n→\n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑐\n}\n be an edge-monotone graph parameter with \n𝑐\n<\n𝑝\n. If \nΦ\n is nontrivial on \n𝑘\n and \n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n≤\n𝑚\n, then \nΦ\n is concentrated on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑝\n𝑡\n or \nΦ\n is reducible on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n.\n\nSection 1.2 is one of the main technical tools that we develop to prove our main theorems since this lemma allows us to use a win-win strategy where we either always simply use Section 1.2 when \nΦ\n is concentrated or a reduction to the prime power case when \nΦ\n is reducible.\n\nA similar strategy was used by the authors of [DMW23] to obtain their lower bounds. Their approach was to analyze the largest prime power inside a given number \n𝑘\n and to use their techniques on this specific prime power (see [DMW23, Definition 6.17]). However, observe that the largest prime power of \n𝑘\n could be logarithmically small which is why the authors of [DMW23] only archived a lower bound of \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛾\n​\nlog\n⁡\n𝑘\n/\nlog\n⁡\nlog\n​\n𝑘\n)\n. On the other side, our Section 1.2 is much stronger by allowing arbitrary prime powers as long as \n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n≤\n𝑘\n. It is easy to see that for a fixed prime number \n𝑝\n, the largest prime power \n𝑝\n𝑡\n with \n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n≤\n𝑘\n has to property that \n𝑝\n𝑡\n≥\n𝑘\n/\n𝑝\n meaning that \n𝑝\n𝑡\n grows linear with respect to \n𝑘\n. Thus, if \n𝑝\n is fixed then we can archive tight lower bounds since \n𝑘\n/\n𝑝\n𝑡\n is bounded by a constant.\n\nBringing the Parts Together.\n\nAssume that \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n𝑘\n→\n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n}\n is edge-monotone and nontrivial. Section 1.2 gives us the tools to prove Section 1 by using the following win-win approach. Let \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n be a function that returns on input \n𝑘\n a prime power \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n with base \n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n (i.e \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n=\n𝑔\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n𝑡\n for some \n𝑡\n) such that \n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n<\n𝑔\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n and \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n+\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≤\n𝑘\n. Now, Section 1.2 ensures that \nΦ\n is either concentrated on infinitely many \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n or \nΦ\n is reducible on infinitely \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n.\n\nIf \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n∈\nΩ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n𝜓\n)\n for a fixed \n0\n<\n𝜓\n≤\n1\n, then we can use Section 1.2 to show #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hardness for the concentrated case. Further, since each graph \n𝐻\n𝑘\n contains the graph \n𝐾\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n,\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n as a subgraph, we also get a lower bound of \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛿\n​\n𝑘\n𝜓\n)\n. Otherwise, we are in the reducible case. In this case, we can construct a graph property \nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n that is nontrivial on infinitely many primer powers \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n. We can use Theorem 4.1 to show that \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard and to get a lower bound of \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛿\n​\n𝑘\n𝜓\n)\n (see Lemma 4.8). Observe that we lose a prime factor during this process which is why we need that \nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n is nontrivial on \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n to mitigate this effect. Now, we can use the reduction from \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n)\n to \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n to show that \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is also #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard and has the same lower bounds as \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n)\n under ETH (see Theorem 4.10).\n\nIf \n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≡\n𝑐\n, then we can choose the smallest prime number \n𝑝\n with \n𝑐\n<\n𝑝\n and set \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≔\n𝑝\n𝑡\n−\n1\n and \n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≡\n𝑝\n. Here, \n𝑝\n𝑡\n is the largest prime power \n𝑝\n𝑡\n with \n𝑝\n𝑡\n≤\n𝑘\n. Now, we get that \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n∈\nΩ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n which is why we get tight lower bounds. This leads us to Section 1. On the other side, if \n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n=\n⌊\n(\n1\n−\n𝜀\n)\n​\n𝑘\n1\n/\n𝛼\n⌋\n, then we can choose \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n in way that \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n∈\nΩ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n1\n−\n1\n/\n𝛼\n)\n which leads us to Section 1.\n\nIn Section 6, we show that our hardness results remain true under modulo counting as long as \n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n is bounded by a constant. These results use the fact that our reduction from Section 3 also holds for modulo counting as long as the alternating enumerator does not vanish modulo \n𝑝\n, which leads to a reduction from \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nClique\n to \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n. Lastly, we show that \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n has tight bounds under rETH. For this, we rely on the fact that the problem \nUnique\n-\n​\n𝑘\n​\n-\nClique\n has tight bounds under rETH which is shown in Appendix B.\n\n2Preliminaries\n\nFor natural numbers \n𝑛\n, we write \n[\n𝑛\n]\n for the set \n{\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑛\n}\n. For natural numbers \n𝑎\n, \n𝑏\n and \n𝑚\n, we write \n𝑎\n≡\n𝑚\n𝑏\n as a shorthand for \n𝑎\n≡\n𝑏\nmod\n𝑚\n. Next, we write \n(\n𝑛\n𝑘\n)\n for the binomial coefficient. If \n𝐴\n is a set then \n(\n𝐴\n𝑘\n)\n is the set of all subsets \n𝐵\n⊆\n𝐴\n that have size \n𝑘\n. For a function \n𝑓\n:\n𝐴\n→\n𝐵\n and a set \n𝐶\n⊆\n𝐴\n, we write \n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n)\n for the set \n{\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑐\n)\n:\n𝑐\n∈\n𝐶\n}\n. We say that a set \n𝐴\n is computable if there exists an algorithm that decides on input \n𝑥\n if \n𝑥\n∈\n𝐴\n. We say that a function \n𝑓\n:\nNature\n→\nNature\n is unbounded if \nlim\n𝑛\n→\n∞\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑛\n)\n=\n∞\n. If \n𝐺\n is a graph and \nℎ\n:\nNature\n→\nNature\n is function, then \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n is defined as \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n)\n.\n\nGraphs\n\nFor the whole paper, we only consider simple graphs, meaning undirected graphs that have neither weights, loops, nor parallel edges. We denote with \n𝒢\n the set of all (simple) graphs, and use \n𝒢\n𝑛\n for the set of all (simple) graphs with vertex set \n[\n𝑛\n]\n. Further, we order the set \n𝒢\n lexicographically. This way, we construct the set \n𝒢\n∗\n that constant of the first graph, in each isomorphism class of graphs, that appears \n𝒢\n. We use \n𝒢\n𝑛\n∗\n to denote the set of graphs in \n𝒢\n∗\n with vertex set \n[\n𝑛\n]\n.\n\nFor a graph \n𝐺\n, we use \n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n to denote the set of vertices and we use \n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n to denote the set of edges. For a subset of edges \n𝐴\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n, we write \n𝐺\n​\n{\n𝐴\n}\n for the graph with vertex set \n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n​\n{\n𝐴\n}\n)\n≔\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n and edge set \n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n​\n{\n𝐴\n}\n)\n≔\n𝐴\n. We say that such a graph is an edge-subgraph of \n𝐺\n. For a set of vertices \n𝑆\n⊆\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n, we write \n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝑆\n]\n for the graph induced by \n𝑆\n. For a graph \n𝐻\n and a graph \n𝐺\n, we write \nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n for the set of all subgraphs of \n𝐺\n that are isomorphic to \n𝐻\n.\n\nWe write \nIS\n𝑘\n for the independent set that has \n𝑘\n vertices. Further, we use \n𝐾\n𝑛\n to denote the complete graph with vertex set \n[\n𝑛\n]\n, and we write \n𝐾\n𝑛\n,\n𝑚\n for the complete bipartite graph with vertex set \n[\n𝑛\n]\n×\n[\n𝑚\n]\n.\n\nGroup Theory and Morphisms between Graphs\n\nFor a finite set \n𝑋\n of size \n𝑛\n, the set of all bijections from \n𝑋\n to \n𝑋\n forms together with function composition a group that is usually called the symmetric group \n𝔖\n𝑛\n. We use the notation \n𝔖\n𝑋\n to emphasize the set \n𝑋\n.\n\nWe say that a group \nΓ\n is a permutation group of \n𝑋\n if \nΓ\n is a subgroup of the symmetric group \n𝔖\n𝑋\n for a finite set \n𝑋\n. This group is transitive if every \n𝑥\n∈\n𝑋\n can be mapped to any other element \n𝑦\n∈\n𝑋\n via a group element \n𝑔\n∈\nΓ\n. Finally, we say that \nΓ\n is a \n𝑝\n-group if its order is a power of \n𝑝\n (that is, the number of elements that appear in \nΓ\n is a power of \n𝑝\n).\n\nA graph homomorphism \nℎ\n:\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n from \n𝐻\n to \n𝐺\n is a function between the vertex sets of two graphs \n𝐻\n and \n𝐺\n that preserves adjacencies (but not necessarily non-adjacencies). This means that \nℎ\n maps the vertices of every edge \n{\n𝑢\n,\n𝑣\n}\n∈\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n to an edge \n{\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑢\n)\n,\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑣\n)\n}\n∈\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n. We denote the set of all homomorphisms from \n𝐻\n to \n𝐺\n by \nHom\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n. We say that a graph homomorphism \nℎ\n:\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n is surjective if all vertices and edges of \n𝐺\n are in the image of \nℎ\n. The spasm of \n𝐺\n is the set of all graphs \n𝐺\n such that there is a surjective homomorphism from \n𝐻\n to \n𝐺\n.\n\nA homomorphism \nℎ\n:\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n is called an isomorphism if \nℎ\n is a bijection on the vertex sets and \nℎ\n−\n1\n:\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n is a homomorphism, too. This definition is equivalent to saying that \n{\n𝑢\n,\n𝑣\n}\n∈\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n if and only if \n{\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑢\n)\n,\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑣\n)\n}\n∈\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n. We call two graphs \n𝐺\n and \n𝐻\n isomorphic, if there is an isomorphism between them, which is denoted by \n𝐺\n≅\n𝐻\n. An automorphism of \n𝐺\n is an isomorphism from \n𝐺\n to itself. The set of all automorphism of \n𝐺\n is denoted by \nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n.\n\nThe set of automorphism \nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n together with composition forms a group. Observe that the automorphism group of the clique \nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑛\n)\n is just the symmetric group \n𝔖\n𝑛\n.\n\nParameterized Problems and Parameterized Complexity\n\nA parameterized (counting) problem is defined via a function \n𝑃\n:\nΣ\n∗\n→\nNature\n and a computable parameterization \n𝜅\n:\nΣ\n∗\n→\nNature\n. To goal is to find an algorithm that computes in input \n𝑥\n∈\nΣ\n∗\n to value \n𝑃\n⁡\n(\n𝑥\n)\n. We say that a parameterized problem \n(\n𝑃\n,\n𝜅\n)\n is fixed-parameter tractable (FPT) if there is a computable function \n𝑓\n and a deterministic algorithm \n𝔸\n such that the algorithm \n𝔸\n computes the value \n𝑃\n⁡\n(\n𝑥\n)\n in time \n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝜅\n⁡\n(\n𝑥\n)\n)\n​\n|\n𝑥\n|\n𝑐\n, for a fixed constant \n𝑐\n, for all \n𝑥\n∈\nΣ\n∗\n.\n\nA parameterized Turing reduction from \n(\n𝑃\n,\n𝜅\n)\n to \n(\n𝑃\n′\n,\n𝜅\n′\n)\n is a deterministic FPT algorithm with oracle access to \n𝑃\n′\n that computes \n𝑃\n⁡\n(\n𝑥\n)\n such that there is a computable function \n𝑔\n with the property that \n𝜅\n′\n​\n(\n𝑦\n)\n≤\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝜅\n⁡\n(\n𝑦\n)\n)\n holds for each oracle access to \n𝑃\n′\n. We write \n𝐴\n≤\n𝑇\nfpt\n𝐵\n to denote that there is a parameterized Turing reduction from \n𝐴\n to \n𝐵\n.\n\nThe counting version \n#\n​\n𝑃\n of a decision problem \n𝑃\n is the problem to compute the number of valid solutions for a given input \n𝑥\n. One very important example of such a counting problem is the #Cliqueproblem. The input is a graph \n𝐺\n and a natural number \n𝑘\n and the output is the number of induced subgraphs of \n𝐺\n of size \n𝑘\n which form a \n𝑘\n-clique. We parameterize #Clique by \n𝜅\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n,\n𝑘\n)\n≔\n𝑘\n. Much research has been focused on understanding the complexity of #Clique and it is widely believed that no FPT algorithm exists [FG04, CCF+05]. We say therefore that a problem \n(\n𝑃\n′\n,\n𝜅\n′\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard if there is parameterized Turing reduction from \n(\n#\nClique\n,\n𝜅\n)\n to \n(\n𝑃\n′\n,\n𝜅\n′\n)\n, which intuitively means that \n(\n𝑃\n′\n,\n𝜅\n′\n)\n is at least as hard as #Clique. Thus, #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hardness rules out FPT algorithms of \n(\n𝑃\n′\n,\n𝜅\n′\n)\n based on said beliefs.\n\nIn the following, we further define parameterized counting problems. For a recursively enumerable class of graphs \nℋ\n, a problem instance of \n#\nHom\n​\n(\nℋ\n)\n is a graph \n𝐻\n∈\nℋ\n and a graph \n𝐺\n∈\n𝒢\n. The output is the number of homomorphisms from \n𝐻\n to \n𝐺\n (that is, #\nHom\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n); the parameterization is given by \n𝜅\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n,\n𝐺\n)\n≔\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n. In [DJ04], Dalmau and Jonsson proved that \n#\nHom\n​\n(\nℋ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard if and only if the treewidth of the set \nℋ\n is unbounded (that is, there is no constant \n𝑐\n such that the treewidth of all elements in \nℋ\n is below \n𝑐\n).\n\nNext, we define the colored version of \n#\nHom\n​\n(\nℋ\n)\n. Here, we get two graphs \n𝐺\n and \n𝐻\n, with a coloring \n𝑐\n:\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n→\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n. Here, \n𝑐\n is simply a homomorphism from \n𝐺\n to \n𝐻\n. A color-prescribed homomorphism \nℎ\n from \n𝐻\n to \n𝐺\n is a homomorphism from \n𝐻\n to \n𝐺\n such that \n𝑐\n⁡\n(\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑣\n)\n)\n=\n𝑣\n for all \n𝑣\n∈\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n.\n\nWe write \ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n for the set of all color-prescribed homomorphism from \n𝐻\n to a graph \n𝐺\n that is \n𝐻\n-colored via \n𝑐\n. Observe that \ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n might change if we use a different coloring. For a recursively enumerable class of graphs \nℋ\n, the problem \n#\ncp-Hom\n​\n(\nℋ\n)\n gets as input a graph \n𝐻\n∈\nℋ\n and a graph \n𝐺\n that is \n𝐻\n-colored via \n𝑐\n, the task is to compute the value \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n. We parameterize \n#\ncp-Hom\n​\n(\nℋ\n)\n by \n𝜅\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n,\n𝐺\n)\n≔\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n.\n\nGraph Parameters and the \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n problem\n\nGraph parameters (also known as graph invariants) are functions \nΦ\n that assign a value to a given graph and are invariant under graph isomorphisms that is \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n whenever \n𝐺\n≅\n𝐻\n. In this paper, we consider only graph parameters that map graphs to rational numbers. Further, we exclusively consider computable graph parameters. A graph parameter \nΦ\n is considered computable if there is an algorithm that gets as input a graph \n𝐺\n and computes the value \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n. We say that \nΦ\n is nontrivial on \n𝑛\n if \nΦ\n is not constant on the set of \n𝑛\n-vertex graphs, and we say that \nΦ\n is nontrivial if it is nontrivial on infinitely many values \n𝑛\n. Graph properties are graph parameters with codomain \n{\n0\n,\n1\n}\n.\n\nWe sometimes write \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n𝑘\n→\n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n}\n where \n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n:\nNature\n→\nNature\n is some computable function. This notation means that the graph parameter \nΦ\n has codomain \n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n}\n when restricted to \n𝑘\n-vertex graphs. For instance, if \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n, then we could use \n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n=\n𝑘\n2\n.\n\nNext, we say that a graph parameter \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℚ\n is edge-monotone if we have \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n≤\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n whenever \n𝐻\n is an edge-subgraph of \n𝐺\n. Note that the definition of edge-monotone graph parameter coincides with the definition of edge-monotonicity for graph properties given in [FR22, DMW23], meaning that we can view each edge-monotone graph property \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\n{\n0\n,\n1\n}\n as an edge-monotone graph parameter. Observe that if \nΦ\n is edge-monotone and nontrivial on \n𝑘\n, then \nΦ\n⁡\n(\nIS\n𝑘\n)\n>\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑘\n)\n. For a fixed graph parameter \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℚ\n, we define the following parameterized problem.\n\n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\nInput: A natural number \n𝑘\n and a graph \n𝐺\n.\nOutput: The value\n\t\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n≔\n∑\n𝐴\n∈\n(\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n𝑘\n)\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n)\n.\n\t\nParameter: The problem is parameterized by the size of the induced subgraphs \n𝑘\n.\n\nObserve that for a graph property \nΦ\n the definition of \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n considers with the definition of the counting problem \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n given in [RS20, DRSW22, DMW23]. As with #Hom, we also need a colored variant of \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n. Imagine that the vertices of a given input graph \n𝐺\n are partitioned into \n𝑘\n color classes and that we would like to compute the sum \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n)\n that goes over all \n𝑘\n-vertex induced subgraphs \n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n of \n𝐺\n such that \n𝐴\n contains exactly one vertex from each color class (this is also known as colorful). Observe that the sum depends on the coloring of \n𝐺\n, and remember that we can describe a coloring using a homomorphism \n𝑐\n:\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n→\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n. Now, a graph parameter \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℚ\n and for a recursively enumerable class of graphs \nℋ\n, we define the following problem.\n\n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n,\nℋ\n)\nInput: A a graph \n𝐻\n∈\nℋ\n and a \n𝐻\n-graph colored \n𝐺\n∈\n𝒢\n.\nOutput: The value\n\t\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n≔\n∑\n𝐴\n∈\n(\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n)\n\n\n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n​\n is colorful\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n)\n.\n\t\nParameter: The problem is parameterized by \n𝜅\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n,\n𝐺\n)\n≔\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n.\n\nWe write \n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n instead of \n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n)\n, meaning that we can also all graphs for coloring. We use \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n to denote the problem that get as input a \n𝑘\n-vertex graph \n𝐻\n and a graph \n𝐺\n and is asked to compute \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n.\n\nBasic Properties of Graph Parameters, \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n, and \n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n,\nℋ\n)\n\nNext, we collect basic properties of graph parameters, \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n, and \n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n,\nℋ\n)\n. We start by constructing a graph property given a graph parameter.\n\nDefinition 2.1.\n\nFor all graph parameters \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℚ\n and numbers \n𝑘\n, we define the following objects.\n\n• \n\nThe set \nIm\nΦ\n𝑘\n is the image of \nΦ\n on the set \n𝒢\n𝑘\n (this is the set of graphs with \n𝑘\n vertices). Further, we write \nIm\nΦ\n for the image of \nΦ\n.\n\n• \n\nFor all \n𝑏\n∈\nℚ\n, we define \nΦ\n𝑏\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n1\n if \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n𝑏\n and \nΦ\n𝑏\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n0\n otherwise.\n\nObserve that \nIm\nΦ\n𝑘\n is always a finite set. It is not hard to see that we can write each \nΦ\n as a linear combination of \nΦ\n𝑏\n where \n𝑏\n∈\nIm\nΦ\n𝑘\n. This allows us to also view \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n and \n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n,\nℋ\n)\n as a linear combination of \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n𝑏\n)\n and \n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n𝑏\n,\nℋ\n)\n respectively. We use this to prove statements about \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n and \n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n,\nℋ\n)\n by first looking at graph properties.\n\nRemark 2.2.\n\nFor all graph parameters \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℚ\n, non negative integers \n𝑘\n∈\nNature\n, graphs \n𝐺\n and \n𝐻\n, values \n𝑏\n∈\nℚ\n, sets \n𝐴\n⊇\nIm\nΦ\n#\n​\n𝑉\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n, \n𝐵\n⊇\nIm\nΦ\n𝑘\n, and \n𝐶\n⊇\nIm\nΦ\n#\n​\n𝑉\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n, we obtain\n\n\t\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n\t\n=\n∑\n𝑏\n∈\n𝐴\n𝑏\n⋅\nΦ\n𝑏\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n\t\t\n(1)\n\n\t#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n\t\n=\n∑\n𝑏\n∈\n𝐵\n𝑏\n⋅\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n𝑏\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n\t\t\n(2)\n\n\t\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n\t\n=\n∑\n𝑏\n∈\n𝐶\n𝑏\n⋅\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n𝑏\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n\t\t\n(3)\n\nFor the last equality, we assume that \n𝐺\n is \n𝐻\n-colored.\n\nLastly, we show that we can transform each edge-monotone graph parameter \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\n𝐷\n with finite codomain \n𝐷\n⊆\nℚ\n into an edge-monotone graph parameter \nΦ\n′\n:\n𝒢\n→\n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑐\n}\n such that we can compute \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n using \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n′\n)\n. This allows us to use \n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑐\n}\n as our codomain for most theorems.\n\nLemma 2.3.\n\nFor graph parameter \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\n𝐷\n with finite codomain \n𝐷\n⊆\nℚ\n, there is a constant \n𝑐\n and a graph parameter \nΦ\n′\n:\n𝒢\n→\n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑐\n}\n such that\n\n\t\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n,\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n,\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n′\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n)\nfor some function \n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n,\n𝑛\n,\n𝑚\n)\n.\n\t\n\nThe function \n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n,\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n,\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n′\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n)\n can be computed in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n)\n. Further, if \nΦ\n is edge-monotone then \nΦ\n′\n can be chosen to be edge-monotone.\n\nProof 2.4.\n\nLet \n𝑛\n≔\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n. Since \n𝐷\n=\n{\n𝑑\n1\n,\n…\n​\n𝑑\n𝑚\n}\n is finite, there exists a constant \n𝑑\n>\n0\n such that \n𝑑\n⋅\n𝑑\n𝑖\n∈\nℤ\n for all \n𝑑\n𝑖\n∈\n𝐷\n. Further, since the set \n{\n𝑑\n⋅\n𝑑\n𝑖\n:\n𝑑\n𝑖\n∈\nℚ\n}\n is finite, there is a constant \n𝑠\n and \n𝑐\n∈\nNature\n such that \n𝑠\n+\n𝑑\n⋅\n𝑑\n𝑖\n∈\n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑐\n}\n. We define the graph parameter \nΦ\n′\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n≔\n𝑠\n+\n𝑑\n⋅\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n. Observe that \nΦ\n′\n is still computable and that we have \n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n′\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n𝑠\n⋅\n(\n𝑛\n𝑘\n)\n+\n𝑑\n⋅\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n. Hence, by setting\n\n\t\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n,\n𝑛\n,\n𝑚\n)\n≔\n𝑚\n−\n𝑠\n⋅\n(\n𝑛\n𝑘\n)\n𝑑\n,\n\t\n\nwe obtain \n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n,\n𝑛\n,\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n′\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n)\n. Next, note that we can assume \n𝑘\n≤\n𝑛\n and \n|\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n|\n≤\n2\n𝑛\n⋅\nmax\n𝑑\n𝑖\n∈\n𝐷\n⁡\n|\n𝑑\n𝑖\n|\n. Thus, the function \n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n,\n𝑛\n,\n|\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n|\n)\n can be computed in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑛\n)\n. Lastly, if \nΦ\n is edge-monotone, then \nΦ\n′\n is also edge-monotone since \n𝑑\n>\n0\n.\n\n3Reduction from #Clique to \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n\nIn this section, we show a reduction from #Clique to \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n which is an important step for showing Section 1. We use the following chain of reductions which is a modification of [DRSW22].\n\n\t#Clique\t\n≤\n𝑇\nfpt\nSection 3\n​\n#\ncp-Hom\n​\n(\nℋ\n)\n\t\t\n(4)\n\n\t\t\n≤\n𝑇\nfpt\n[DRSW22, Lemmas 7 and 8]\n​\n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n,\nℋ\n)\n​\n≤\n𝑇\nfpt\n[DRSW22, Lemma 10]\n​\n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n;\n\t\n\nThis chain starts with the \n#\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard problem #Clique. Further, it is well-known that this problem has tight lower bounds under ETH. The proof of the following statement can be found in [DMW23, Lemma B.2].\n\n{lemmaq}\n\n[Modification of 14.21 in [CFK+15]] Assuming ETH, there is a constant \n𝛼\n>\n0\n such that for \n𝑘\n≥\n3\n, no algorithm (that reads the whole input) solves \n𝑘\n-Clique on graph \n𝐺\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛼\n​\n𝑘\n)\n.\n\nThe first step of (4) is a reduction from #Clique to \n#\ncp-Hom\n​\n(\nℋ\n)\n. To that end, we use the following statement that allows us to count \n𝑘\n-cliques by using an oracle for \n#\ncp\n-\nHom\n(\n𝐹\n→\n⋆\n)\n whenever \n𝐹\n contains \n𝐾\n𝑘\n,\n𝑘\n as a subgraph.\n\n{lemmaq}\n\n[Modification of [DRSW22, Lemma 11]] There is an algorithm that given a positive integer \nℓ\n>\n1\n, a graph \n𝐹\n (that contains \n𝐾\nℓ\n,\nℓ\n as assuming subgraph), and a graph \n𝐺\n; computes an \n𝐹\n-colored graph \n𝐺\n′\n with \n2\n​\nℓ\n​\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n+\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n)\n|\n−\n2\n​\nℓ\n)\n vertices. Further, the number of cliques of size \nℓ\n in \n𝐺\n equals \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐹\n→\n𝐺\n′\n)\n. The running time of the algorithm is \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n)\n|\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n)\n|\n+\n2\n+\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n)\n|\n2\n​\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n2\n)\n.\n\nSection 3 allows us to focus on a reduction from \n#\ncp-Hom\n​\n(\nℋ\n)\n to \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n. To that end we use the following key lemma from [DRSW22] that we adopt for graph parameters.\n\nLemma 3.1 (Modification of [DRSW22, Lemma 18]).\n\nLet \n𝐻\n denote a graph, let \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℚ\n denote a graph parameter, and let \n𝐺\n denote an \n𝐻\n-colored graph. Then, we have\n\n\t\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n\t\n=\n∑\n𝑆\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n⁡\n{\n𝑆\n}\n)\n​\n∑\n𝐽\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n∖\n𝑆\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐽\n⋅\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n⁡\n{\n𝑆\n∪\n𝐽\n}\n→\n𝐺\n)\n\t\n\t\t\n=\n∑\n𝐴\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐴\n⋅\nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n⁡\n{\n𝐴\n}\n)\n⋅\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n⁡\n{\n𝐴\n}\n→\n𝐺\n)\n.\n\t\n\nFurther, the absolute values of \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n and of the coefficient of \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n are equal.\n\nProof 3.2.\n\nWe define \n𝑘\n≔\n#\n​\n𝑉\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n and use Remark 2.2 to rewrite the equation from [DRSW22, Lemma 18] that was derived for graph properties. We obtain\n\n\t\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n\t\n=\n(\n3\n)\n∑\n𝑏\n∈\nIm\nΦ\n𝑘\n𝑏\n⋅\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n𝑏\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n\t\n\t\t\n=\n(\nLemma 18\n)\n​\n∑\n𝑏\n∈\nIm\nΦ\n𝑘\n𝑏\n​\n∑\n𝑆\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\nΦ\n𝑏\n​\n(\n𝐻\n⁡\n{\n𝑆\n}\n)\n​\n∑\n𝐽\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n∖\n𝑆\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐽\n⋅\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n⁡\n{\n𝑆\n∪\n𝐽\n}\n→\n𝐺\n)\n\t\n\t\t\n=\n∑\n𝑆\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n∑\n𝐽\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n∖\n𝑆\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐽\n⋅\n#\ncp\n-\nHom\n(\n𝐻\n{\n𝑆\n∪\n𝐽\n}\n→\n𝐺\n)\n∑\n𝑏\n∈\nIm\nΦ\n𝑘\n𝑏\n⋅\nΦ\n𝑏\n(\n𝐻\n{\n𝑆\n}\n)\n\t\n\t\t\n=\n(\n1\n)\n∑\n𝑆\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n∑\n𝐽\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n∖\n𝑆\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐽\n⋅\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n⁡\n{\n𝑆\n∪\n𝐽\n}\n→\n𝐺\n)\n⋅\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n⁡\n{\n𝑆\n}\n)\n\t\n\t\t\n=\n∑\n𝑆\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n⁡\n{\n𝑆\n}\n)\n​\n∑\n𝐽\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n∖\n𝑆\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐽\n⋅\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n⁡\n{\n𝑆\n∪\n𝐽\n}\n→\n𝐺\n)\n\t\n\nThus \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n is equal to a linear combination of \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n​\n{\n𝐴\n}\n→\n𝐺\n)\n for \n𝐴\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n. By collecting terms, we obtain\n\n\t\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n∑\n𝐴\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n(\n∑\n𝑆\n⊆\n𝐴\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n⁡\n{\n𝑆\n}\n)\n​\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐴\n−\n#\n​\n𝑆\n)\n⋅\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n⁡\n{\n𝐴\n}\n→\n𝐺\n)\n.\n\t\n\nBy using the definition of the alternating enumerator, we can further rewrite this into\n\n\t\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n∑\n𝐴\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐴\n⋅\nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n⁡\n{\n𝐴\n}\n)\n⋅\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n⁡\n{\n𝐴\n}\n→\n𝐺\n)\n,\n\t\n\nwhich shows that the absolute values of \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n and of the coefficient of \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n are equal.\n\nIn particular, the second part of Lemma 3.1 yields that the term \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n appears in the of Lemma 3.1 if and only if \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n≠\n0\n. Observe that Lemma 3.1 itself does not suffice to obtain the claimed reduction \n#\ncp-Hom\n(\nℋ\n)\n≤\n𝑇\nfpt\n#\ncp-IndSub\n(\nΦ\n)\n: the oracle for \n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n computes only a sum in which \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n occurs as some term—we still need to extract the value \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n out of the result of the oracle. Fortunately for us, [DRSW22, Lemma 7] does exactly that by showing a generalization of the Complexity Monotonicity of [CDM17]. This means that the computation of a finite sum \n∑\n𝐻\n∈\n𝒢\n𝛼\n(\n𝐻\n)\n⋅\ncp\n-\nHom\n(\n𝐻\n→\n⋆\n)\n is precisely as hard as the computation of the hardest term with nonvanishing coefficient. This result is summarized in the following lemma.\n\nLemma 3.3 (Modification of [DMW23, Lemma A.7]).\n\nLet \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℚ\n denote a graph parameter and let \n𝐻\n be a \n𝑘\n-vertex graph with \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n≠\n0\n. Any algorithm that computes for each \n𝐻\n-colored \n𝐺\n′\n the number \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n′\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n′\n)\n|\n𝛽\n)\n implies an algorithm that for each graph \n𝐺\n\n• \n\ncomputes the number \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛽\n+\n1\n)\n for some computable function \nℎ\n,\n\n• \n\nand our algorithm queries \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n′\n)\n only on \n𝐻\n-colored graphs \n𝐺\n′\n with \n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n′\n)\n≤\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n, for some computable function \n𝑓\n.\n\nProof 3.4.\n\nWrite \n𝑘\n≔\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n. Suppose that we are given a graph \n𝐺\n that is \n𝐻\n-colored via \n𝑐\n. We wish to compute the number \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n.\n\n(1)\n\nFirst, we use Lemma 3.1 to write \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n as\n\n\t\n∑\n𝐴\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐴\n⋅\nΦ\n^\n(\n𝐻\n{\n𝐴\n}\n)\n⋅\n#\ncp\n-\nHom\n(\n𝐻\n{\n𝐴\n}\n→\n⋆\n)\n,\n\t\n\nwhere the term \n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n⋅\nΦ\n^\n(\n𝐻\n)\n⋅\n#\ncp\n-\nHom\n(\n𝐻\n→\n⋆\n)\n appears with non-zero coefficient since \n𝐻\n is nonvanishing. According to [DRSW22, Lemmas 7], we can compute \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n)\n using an oracle for \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n. Here \n𝑓\n is a computable function. In said reduction, each graph \n𝐺\n′\n that is used inside an oracle call satisfies \n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n′\n)\n|\n≤\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n.\n\n(2)\n\nSecond, we compute for each \n𝐺\n′\n the number \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n′\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n′\n)\n|\n𝛽\n)\n using the algorithm which we assumed to exist.\n\nWe combine the two steps to obtain an algorithm that computes \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n⋅\n(\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n)\n𝛽\n)\n, which can be rewritten into \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛽\n+\n1\n)\n for some computable function \nℎ\n. Finally, observe that we query our algorithm only on graphs \n𝐺\n′\n with \n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n′\n)\n≤\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n.\n\nLet us quickly recap what we have done. We first showed that we can count the number of \n𝑘\n-cliques in a graph \n𝐺\n by finding a graph \n𝐻\n that contains \n𝐾\n𝑘\n,\n𝑘\n and computing \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n′\n)\n for some auxiliary graph \n𝐺\n. Further, we showed that we can compute \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n′\n)\n using a oracle for \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n whenever the alternating enumerator of \n𝐻\n is nonvanishing. That is, we can count cliques by finding graphs that contain a biclique and have a nonvanishing alternating enumerator. Since there are tight bounds for counting cliques assuming ETH, we obtain the following result.\n\nTheorem 3.5 (Modification of [DRSW22]).\n\nAssuming ETH, there is a global constant \n𝛽\n>\n0\n and a positive integer \n𝑁\n such that for all graph parameters \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℚ\n, functions \nℎ\n, numbers \n𝑘\n with\n\n• \n\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≥\n𝑁\n\n• \n\nthere is a graph \n𝐹\n with \n𝑘\n vertices and \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐹\n)\n≠\n0\n,\n\n• \n\nand \n𝐹\n contains \n𝐾\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n,\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n as a subgraph\n\nthere is no algorithm (that reads the whole input) that for every \n𝐺\n computes \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛽\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n. Here, input consists of a \n𝑘\n-vertex graph \n𝐻\n and \n𝐻\n-colored graph \n𝐺\n.\n\nProof 3.6.\n\nWe show how to use \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n to solve \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n-Clique.\n\nClaim 1.\n\nFor a fixed \n𝑘\n such that there is a graph \n𝐹\n with \n𝑘\n vertices, \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐹\n)\n≠\n0\n, and \n𝐹\n contains \n𝐾\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n,\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n as a subgraph; if there is an algorithm that computes for each \n𝐹\n-colored graph \n𝐺\n′\n the value \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐹\n)\n→\n𝐺\n′\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n′\n)\n|\n𝛾\n)\n, then \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n-Clique can be computed for each graph \n𝐺\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛾\n+\n1\n)\n.\n\n{claimproof}\n\nWe construct an algorithm that solves \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n-Clique using an oracle for \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐹\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n. Since \n𝐹\n is fixed, we can assume that our algorithm knows \n𝐹\n. Fix a graph \n𝐺\n, we use the algorithm from Section 3 to construct a graph \n𝐺\n′\n such that \n𝐺\n contains a \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n-clique if and only if \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐹\n→\n𝐺\n′\n)\n is not zero. The running time of this construction is in \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n2\n)\n since \n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n)\n|\n=\n𝑘\n is constant. Further, we obtain \n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n′\n)\n|\n≤\n2\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n​\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n+\n𝑘\n=\n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n)\n.\n\nIf we can compute \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐹\n)\n→\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n′\n)\n|\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n′\n)\n|\n𝛾\n)\n, then we can use Lemma 3.3 to compute \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐹\n→\n𝐺\n′\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n(\n2\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n​\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n+\n𝑘\n)\n𝛾\n+\n1\n)\n. Thus, we can solve \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n-Clique in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n2\n+\n(\n2\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n​\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n+\n𝑘\n)\n𝛾\n+\n1\n)\n which is in \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛾\n+\n1\n)\n since \n𝑘\n is fixed. Note, that we can assume that \n𝛾\n≥\n1\n since otherwise the algorithm for \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐹\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n would be sublinear. By Section 3, there is a constant \n𝛼\n>\n0\n such that no algorithm solves \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n-Clique in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛼\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n for a fixed \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≥\n3\n unless ETH fails. Set \n𝛽\n≔\n𝛼\n/\n2\n and \n𝑁\n≔\nmax\n⁡\n(\n3\n,\n2\n/\n𝛼\n)\n. If there is a \n𝑘\n with \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≥\n𝑁\n≥\n3\n, a graph \n𝐹\n with \n𝑘\n vertices such that \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐹\n)\n≠\n0\n, and \n𝐹\n contains \n𝐾\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n,\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n as a subgraph, and an algorithm that solves #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n′\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n′\n)\n|\n𝛽\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n, then we can use Claim 1 to solve \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n-Clique in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛽\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n+\n1\n)\n. Observe that \n𝛽\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n+\n1\n≤\n𝛼\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n for \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≥\n2\n/\n𝛼\n. Thus, we can use solve \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n-Clique in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑛\n𝛼\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n. Hence, ETH fails.\n\nThe last results show how to use an algorithm for \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n to solve #-Clique. Now, the last step of our reduction chain (4) is to prove a reduction form \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n to \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n.\n\nLemma 3.7 (Modification of [DRSW22, Lemma 10]).\n\nGiven a graph parameter \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℚ\n and let \n𝐻\n be a \n𝑘\n-vertex graph. Assume that there is an algorithm \n𝔸\n that computes on input \n(\n𝐺\n,\n𝑘\n)\n the number #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛽\n. Then, there is an algorithm \n𝔹\n that given an \n𝐻\n-colored graph \n𝐺\n as input, computes \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n2\n𝑘\n⋅\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛽\n+\n2\n)\n.\n\nProof 3.8.\n\nLet \n𝐻\n∈\nℋ\n be a graph and let \n𝐺\n be a \n𝐻\n-colored graph with coloring \n𝑐\n. Using [DRSW22, equation (19)], we can rewrite each \n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n𝑏\n,\nℋ\n)\n as a sum of #\nIndSub\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n→\n𝐺\n𝐽\n)\n terms. We combine this with (3) to obtain\n\n\t\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n\t\n=\n(\n3\n)\n∑\n𝑏\n∈\nIm\nΦ\n𝑘\n𝑏\n⋅\n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\nΦ\n𝑏\n,\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n\t\n\t\t\n=\n(\n19\n)\n∑\n𝑏\n∈\nIm\nΦ\n𝑘\n𝑏\n​\n∑\n𝐽\n⊆\n[\n𝑘\n]\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐽\n⋅\n#\nIndSub\n(\nΦ\n𝑏\n,\n𝑘\n→\n𝐺\n𝐽\n)\n\t\n\t\t\n=\n∑\n𝐽\n⊆\n[\n𝑘\n]\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐽\n​\n∑\n𝑏\n∈\nIm\nΦ\n𝑘\n𝑏\n⋅\n#\nIndSub\n(\nΦ\n𝑏\n,\n𝑘\n→\n𝐺\n𝐽\n)\n\t\n\t\t\n=\n(\n2\n)\n∑\n𝐽\n⊆\n[\n𝑘\n]\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐽\n​\n#\nIndSub\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n→\n𝐺\n𝐽\n)\n,\n\t\n\nwhere \n𝐺\n𝐽\n is the graph the is obtained from \n𝐺\n by deleting all vertices whose color is in \n𝐽\n. Hence, \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n can be computed by using algorithm \n𝔸\n at most \n2\n𝑘\n times. Each graph \n𝐺\n𝐽\n can be computed in time \n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n2\n and we can compute #\nIndSub\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n→\n𝐺\n𝐽\n)\n in \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛽\n)\n.\n\nLastly, we combine the results of this section to obtain a corollary that we can use for proving hardness and to get tight lower bounds under ETH. In the following, we write \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑘\n)\n instead of \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑘\n)\n|\n)\n whenever \nℎ\n:\nNature\n→\nNature\n is a function that maps natural numbers to natural numbers.\n\n\\cortightbounds\nProof 3.9.\n• \n\nFirst, we show \n#\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hardness by constructing a parameterized Turing reduction from #Clique to \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n. Given a graph \n𝐺\n and a number \n𝑘\n. We know that there exists a nonvanishing graph \n𝐹\n that contains \n𝐾\n𝑘\n,\n𝑘\n is a biclique since there exists a graph \n𝐺\n𝑗\n∈\n𝐴\n with \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑗\n)\n≥\n𝑘\n. We can find \n𝐹\n by simply iterating over all possible graphs (ordered by the number of vertices) and search for the first one that has these properties. The running time of this is in \n𝑂\n​\n(\nℎ\n1\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n for some computable function \nℎ\n1\n. Further, we can use Section 3 to compute a \n𝐹\n-colored graph \n𝐺\n′\n with \n2\n​\n𝑘\n​\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n+\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n)\n|\n−\n2\n​\n𝑘\n)\n many vertices in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\nℎ\n2\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n2\n)\n such that the number of \n𝑘\n-clique in \n𝐺\n is equal to \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐹\n→\n𝐺\n′\n)\n. Lemma 3.3 ensures that \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐹\n→\n𝐺\n′\n)\n can be computed by an algorithm that can queries \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n only on \n𝐹\n and \n𝐹\n-colored graphs with size at most \nℎ\n3\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n, for some computable function \nℎ\n3\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n. This shows that there is a parameterized Turing reduction from #Clique to \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n, thus \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is \n#\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard. Lastly, we use Lemma 3.7 to get a parameterized Turing reduction from \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n to \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n which shows that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is \n#\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard, too.\n\n• \n\nLet \n𝑁\n and \n𝛽\n be the constants from Theorem 3.5. Since \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n∈\nΩ\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝜓\n)\n, there is a constant \n𝑐\n and integers \n𝑀\n such that \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n≥\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝜓\n/\n𝑐\n for all \n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n≥\n𝑀\n. We define \n𝛾\n′\n≔\nmin\n⁡\n(\n𝛽\n/\n𝑐\n,\n𝛽\n/\n2\n)\n. Now, let \n𝑘\n be some value such that \n𝑘\n=\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑗\n)\n|\n for some \n𝑗\n. If \n𝑘\n≥\n𝐶\n, where \n𝐶\n≔\nmax\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝑐\n​\n𝑁\n)\n1\n/\n𝜓\n,\n𝑁\n,\n𝑀\n,\n4\n/\n𝛽\n)\n, then we can use Theorem 3.5 to show that no algorithm that computes for each \n𝑘\n-vertex graph \n𝐻\n and \n𝐻\n-colored graph \n𝐺\n the value \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛽\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n unless ETH fails. This means that no algorithm solves the problem in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛾\n′\n​\n𝑘\n𝜓\n)\n since \n𝛾\n′\n​\n𝑘\n𝜓\n≤\n𝛽\n​\n𝑘\n𝜓\n/\n𝑐\n≤\n𝛽\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n.\n\n \n\nTo obtain our lower bound also for \n𝑘\n<\n𝐶\n, we set \n𝛾\n≔\nmin\n⁡\n(\n𝛾\n′\n,\n1\n/\n(\n𝐶\n+\n1\n)\n)\n. Observe that for \n𝑘\n<\n𝐶\n, we obtain\n\n\t\n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛾\n​\n𝑘\n𝜓\n)\n=\n𝑜\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n)\n.\n\t\n\nsince \n𝑘\n𝜓\n≤\n𝑘\n. Now, this running time is unconditionally unachievable for an algorithm that reads the whole input.\n\nLastly, assume that we could compute the value #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛾\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n. If \n𝑘\n≥\n𝐶\n we can use Lemma 3.7 to get an algorithm that computes \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛾\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n+\n2\n)\n. However, now we obtain that \n𝛾\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n+\n2\n≤\n𝛽\n/\n2\n⋅\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n+\n2\n≤\n𝛽\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n for \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≥\n𝐶\n≥\n4\n/\n𝛽\n. Otherwise \n𝑘\n<\n𝐶\n and we would obtain a sublinear algorithm for #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n which is unconditionally unachievable for any algorithm that reads the whole input.\n\n• \n\nThis part is a direct application of the previous part.\n\nOur Section 1 only covers nontrivial edge-monotone graph parameters with finite codomain. However, there are also many interesting graph parameters with unbounded codomain.\n\nTheorem 3.10.\n\nFor the following three graph parameter the problem \nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n𝑗\n)\n and \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n𝑗\n)\n are both #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard and there exists a constant \n𝛼\n𝑗\n such that for all \n𝑘\n≥\n3\n no algorithm (that reads the whole input) computes for all \n𝐺\n the value \nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n𝑗\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n (or \ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n𝑗\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n) in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛼\n𝑗\n​\n𝑘\n)\n.\n\n1.\n\nΦ\n1\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\nΔ\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n, where \nΔ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n is the maximal degree of \n𝐺\n\n2.\n\nΦ\n2\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n number of connected components of \n𝐺\n\n3.\n\nΦ\n3\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n𝜒\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n, where \n𝜒\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n is the chromatic number of \n𝐺\n\nProof 3.11.\n\nLet us first consider \nΦ\n1\n. For this let \n𝑝\n be an odd prime number, we consider the fixed points \nFP\n⁡\n(\nSyl\n𝑝\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n)\n. By Section A.2, we know that these fixed points are precisely the difference graphs \n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n for \n𝐴\n⊆\n[\n𝑠\n]\n, where \n𝑠\n≔\n(\n𝑝\n−\n1\n)\n/\n2\n. Observe that \nΦ\n1\n​\n(\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n)\n=\n2\n​\n|\n𝐴\n|\n. Thus\n\n\t\nΦ\n1\n^\n(\n𝐾\n𝑝\n)\n=\nΦ\n1\n^\n(\n𝐶\n𝑝\n[\n𝑠\n]\n)\n≡\n𝑝\n∑\n𝑘\n=\n0\n𝑠\n2\n𝑘\n(\n𝑠\n𝑘\n)\n≡\n𝑝\n𝑠\n2\n𝑠\n,\n\t\n\nwhere the last equation follows from a simple induction. Observe that \n𝑠\n2\n𝑠\n≢\n𝑝\n0\n since \n2\n≢\n𝑝\n0\n and \n𝑠\n≢\n𝑝\n0\n. Hence, for each prime \n𝑝\n, we get that \n𝐾\n𝑝\n is nonvanishing and it is easy to see that \n𝐾\n𝑝\n contains \n𝐾\n𝑠\n,\n𝑠\n as a subgraph. This means that we can use Section 1.2 to show that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n1\n)\n and \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n1\n)\n are both #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard and have tight bounds under ETH.\n\nNext, for all natrual numbers \n𝑡\n, we consider the fixed points \nFP\n⁡\n(\nSyl\n2\n𝑡\n,\n𝐾\n2\n𝑡\n)\n. By Section A.2, we know that these fixed points are precisely the \n𝑡\n-lexicographic product of difference graphs \n𝐶\n2\n𝐴\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n2\n𝐴\n𝑡\n. Let \n𝑠\n=\n2\n𝑡\n−\n1\n, and observe that \n𝐾\n𝑠\n,\n𝑠\n=\n𝐶\n2\n{\n1\n}\n∘\n𝐶\n2\n∅\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n2\n∅\n and \nIS\n2\n𝑡\n=\n𝐶\n2\n∅\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n2\n∅\n. Thus \nΦ\n𝑗\n^\n(\n𝐾\n𝑠\n,\n𝑠\n)\n≡\n2\nΦ\n𝑗\n(\n𝐾\n𝑠\n,\n𝑠\n)\n+\nΦ\n𝑗\n(\nIS\n2\n𝑡\n)\n. It is now easy to verify that \nΦ\n2\n^\n(\n𝐾\n𝑠\n,\n𝑠\n)\n≡\n2\n1\n+\n2\n𝑡\n≡\n2\n1\n and \nΦ\n3\n^\n(\n𝐾\n𝑠\n,\n𝑠\n)\n≡\n2\n2\n+\n1\n≡\n2\n1\n. Hence, we can use Section 1.2 to show that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n𝑗\n)\n and \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n𝑗\n)\n are both #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard and have tight bounds under ETH.\n\n4Hardness for edge-monotone graph parameters with finite codomain\n\nTo goal of this section is to show that for all edge-monotone nontrivial graph parameters with finite codomain the problems \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n and \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n are both \n#\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard and have tight bounds assuming ETH.\n\n\\thmedgemon\n\n*\n\n4.1Tight bounds for edge-monotone graph parameters with finite codomain on prime powers\n\nThe goal of this section is to prove \n#\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hardness and tight bounds for \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n and \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n when \nΦ\n is nontrivial on prime powers. We archive this by finding nonvanishing graphs \n𝐻\n that contain large bicliques. To find these graphs we analyze the fixed points of the \n𝑝\n-Sylow group of \nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n)\n (see Section A.2). We exploit the fact that these fixed points contain large bicliques whenever they have a small empty-prefix (see Section A.3).
