Thus, our goal is to find a nonvanishing fixed point \n𝐻\n=\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n𝑡\n with empty-prefix \n𝜀\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐴\n𝑡\n)\n=\n0\n, which is done using the following approach.\n\nSet \n𝑧\n≔\nΦ\n⁡\n(\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n)\n, then we search for the smallest \nℓ\n such that there is a fixed point \n𝐻\n of level \nℓ\n with \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n<\n𝑧\n. Next, we show that \n𝜀\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐴\n𝑡\n)\n=\n0\n which is done by using that graphs with a high empty-prefix are edge-subgraphs of graphs with small empty-prefix. Lastly, we use Section 1.2 to get that \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n is nonvanishing. The proof is similar to the proof of Theorem 7.18 given in [DMW23].\n\nTheorem 4.1 (Modification of [DMW23, Theorem 7.18]).\n\nLet \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\n{\n0\n,\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑐\n}\n denote an edge-monotone graph parameter that is nontrivial on a prime power \n𝑝\n𝑡\n with \n𝑐\n<\n𝑝\n, then there is a nonvanishing fixed point \n𝐻\n of \nSyl\n𝑝\n𝑡\n in \n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n that contains \n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n−\n1\n,\n𝑝\n𝑡\n−\n1\n as a subgraph. In particular, \nΦ\n^\n(\n𝐻\n)\n≢\n𝑝\n0\n.\n\nProof 4.2.\n\nSet \n𝑧\n≔\nΦ\n⁡\n(\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑚\n)\n. Our goal is to show that there is a fixed point \n𝐻\n with the following properties\n\n• \n\n𝐻\n=\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n𝑡\n for \n𝐴\n𝑖\n⊆\n𝔽\n𝑝\n+\n,\n\n• \n\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n<\n𝑧\n,\n\n• \n\n𝜀\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐴\n𝑡\n)\n=\n0\n, and\n\n• \n\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n~\n)\n=\n𝑧\n for all proper sub-points \n𝐻\n~\n of \n𝐻\n.\n\nAs \nΦ\n is nontrivial on \n𝑝\n𝑡\n, we have \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n)\n<\n𝑧\n. Further, we have and \nΦ\n(\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n)\n=\nΦ\n(\n𝐶\n𝑝\n∅\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n∅\n)\n=\n𝑧\n. Now, let \n𝑖\n denote the smallest value such that there is a fixed point \n𝐻\n of level \n𝑖\n with \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n<\n𝑧\n, but all fixed points of level smaller than \n𝑖\n have value \n𝑧\n. As \nΦ\n⁡\n(\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n)\n=\n𝑧\n, we have \n𝑖\n>\n0\n.\n\nClaim 2.\n\nThere is a fixed point \n𝐻\n of level \n𝑖\n with \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n<\n𝑧\n and whose empty-prefix is zero.\n\n{claimproof}\n\nToward an indirect proof, assume that all fixed points \n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n𝑡\n of level \n𝑖\n with \n𝜀\n⁡\n(\nA\n1\n,\n…\n,\nA\nt\n)\n=\n0\n have value \n𝑧\n. We show that now, all fixed points of level \n𝑖\n have value \n𝑧\n, which is a contradiction to our choice of \n𝑖\n.\n\nTo that end, fix an \n𝐹\n≔\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n𝑡\n. If \n𝜀\n⁡\n(\nA\n1\n,\n…\n,\nA\nt\n)\n=\n0\n, then \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n)\n=\n𝑧\n due to our assumption. Otherwise, \n𝜀\n⁡\n(\nA\n1\n,\n…\n,\nA\nt\n)\n>\n0\n, which means that \n𝐹\n has the form \n𝐹\n=\n𝐶\n𝑝\n∅\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n∅\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n𝑡\n−\n𝑗\n and is thus, by Lemma A.9, isomorphic to an edge-subgraph of\n\n\t\n𝐹\n′\n≔\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐹\n𝑝\n𝐴\n𝑡\n−\n𝑗\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n∅\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n∅\n.\n\t\n\nBy Section A.2, the level of \n𝐹\n is equal to the level of \n𝐹\n′\n. Thus by assumption, \n𝐹\n′\n has value \n𝑧\n, hence the value of \n𝐹\n is at least as large as \n𝑧\n since \nΦ\n is edge-monotone. This implies that \n𝐹\n has value \n𝑧\n.\n\nBy Claim 2, we may assume that there is a fixed point \n𝐻\n≔\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n𝑡\n of level \n𝑖\n with \n𝜀\n⁡\n(\nA\n1\n,\n…\n,\nA\nt\n)\n=\n0\n with value less than \n𝑧\n. Now, as the empty-prefix of \n𝐻\n is zero, Section A.3 yields that \n𝐻\n contains \n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n−\n1\n,\n𝑝\n𝑡\n−\n1\n as a subgraph. Finally, the fact that \n𝑐\n<\n𝑝\n allows us to apply Section 1.2 which shows that \nΦ\n^\n(\n𝐻\n)\n≢\n𝑝\n0\n. This completes the proof.\n\n4.2Reduction to Smaller Size Cases\n\nIn this section, we describe a reduction from \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n~\n,\n𝒢\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n𝑐\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n to \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑝\n𝑡\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n, where \nΦ\n~\n is a specific graph parameter that is defined using \nΦ\n and inhabited graphs. We use said reduction to get from a value \n𝑘\n=\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n𝑐\n to a prime power \n𝑝\n𝑡\n.\n\n\\lemincexc\nProof 4.3.\n\nLet \n𝐻\n be a graph and \n𝐺\n be a \n𝐻\n-colored graph. We define a graph \n𝐻\n~\n with \n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n~\n)\n≔\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n⊎\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n2\n)\n⊎\n⋯\n⊎\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n𝑠\n)\n and the edge set of \n𝐻\n~\n consists of all edges in \n𝐻\n. Further, all vertices \n𝑥\n of \n𝐻\n~\n with \n𝑥\n∉\n𝐻\n are connected to all other vertices (including the vertices in \n𝐻\n). Next, we define \n𝐺\n~\n≔\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐺\n,\n𝐻\n2\n​\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n⟩\n. We rename the vertices in \n𝐺\n~\n such that \n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n~\n)\n=\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n⊎\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n2\n)\n⊎\n⋯\n⊎\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n𝑠\n)\n. Since \n𝐺\n is a \n𝐻\n-colored graph we get a homomorphism \n𝑐\n:\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n→\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n as part of our input. We extend \n𝑐\n by defining the following homomorphism\n\n\t\n𝑐\n~\n:\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n~\n)\n→\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n~\n)\n,\n𝑥\n↦\n{\n𝑐\n⁡\n(\n𝑥\n)\n,\nif \n𝑥\n∈\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n\t\n\n𝑥\n,\notherwise\n\t\n.\n\t\n\nThis means that \n𝐺\n~\n is a \n𝐻\n~\n-colored graph. Observe that we can compute \n𝐻\n~\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝑐\n+\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n)\n2\n)\n, \n𝐺\n~\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝑐\n+\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n)\n2\n)\n and \n𝑐\n~\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝑐\n+\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n)\n2\n)\n. Further, without loss of generality, we can assume that \n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n≤\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n.\n\nLastly, we claim that \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n~\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n~\n)\n→\n𝐺\n~\n)\n, thus we can use algorithm \n𝔸\n to compute \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n~\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑐\n+\n𝑘\n,\n𝑐\n+\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n)\n+\n(\n𝑐\n+\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n)\n2\n)\n. To prove our claim, we start with \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n~\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n and unfold the definition of \nΦ\n~\n. Observe that an induced subgraph \n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n is colorful with respect to \n𝑐\n if \n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n hits all color classes meaning that \n𝑐\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n)\n=\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n.\n\n\t\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n~\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n∑\n𝐴\n∈\n(\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n)\n\n\n𝑐\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n)\n=\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n,\n𝐻\n2\n,\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n⟩\n)\n\t\n\nNext, we define \n𝑋\n≔\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n2\n)\n⊎\n⋯\n⊎\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n𝑠\n)\n and observe that \n𝑐\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n)\n=\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n is equivalent to \n𝑐\n~\n​\n(\n𝐺\n~\n​\n[\n𝐴\n⊎\n𝑋\n]\n)\n=\n𝑉\n​\n(\n𝐻\n~\n)\n since \n𝑐\n~\n​\n(\n𝐺\n~\n​\n[\n𝐴\n⊎\n𝐵\n]\n)\n=\n𝑐\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n)\n⊎\n𝐵\n, for all \n𝐵\n⊆\n𝑋\n. Lastly, observe that \n𝐺\n~\n​\n[\n𝐴\n⊎\n𝑋\n]\n=\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n,\n𝐻\n2\n,\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n⟩\n. Thus we can continue our computation to obtain\n\n\t\n∑\n𝐴\n∈\n(\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n)\n\n\n𝑐\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n)\n=\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n,\n𝐻\n2\n,\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n⟩\n)\n=\n∑\n𝐴\n∈\n(\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n)\n\n\n𝑐\n~\n​\n(\n𝐺\n~\n​\n[\n𝐴\n⊎\n𝑋\n]\n)\n=\n𝑉\n​\n(\n𝐻\n~\n)\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n~\n​\n[\n𝐴\n⊎\n𝑋\n]\n)\n=\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n~\n)\n→\n𝐺\n~\n)\n.\n\t\n4.3Finding Large Bicliques in Graphs\n\nFollowing [DMW23, Section 6.4], we introduce the following definition:\n\n\\defconsca\n\nWe show that each edge-monotone graph parameter \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n𝑘\n→\n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑐\n}\n, with \n𝑐\n<\n𝑝\n, that is nontrivial on \n𝑘\n≥\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n+\n𝑝\n𝑡\n is always concentrated on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑝\n𝑡\n or reducible on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n.\n\n\\lemlargebi\nProof 4.4.\n\nFirst, we define \n𝑧\n≔\nΦ\n⁡\n(\nIS\n𝑘\n)\n. Let \n∑\n𝑖\n=\n0\n𝑑\n𝑠\n𝑖\n⋅\n𝑝\n𝑖\n be the base \n𝑝\n representation of \n(\n𝑘\n−\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n)\n, that is \n𝑠\n𝑖\n∈\n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑝\n−\n1\n}\n and \n𝑠\n𝑑\n≠\n0\n.1\n1\nFor example, the base \n3\n representation of 34 is \n1\n⋅\n3\n0\n+\n2\n⋅\n3\n1\n+\n0\n⋅\n3\n2\n+\n1\n⋅\n3\n3\n.\n Observe that \n𝑝\n𝑑\n≥\n𝑝\n𝑡\n. We define \n𝑠\n≔\n∑\n𝑗\n=\n𝑡\n𝑑\n𝑠\n𝑗\n⋅\n𝑝\n𝑗\n−\n𝑡\n and \n𝑟\n≔\n(\n𝑘\n−\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n)\n−\n𝑠\n⋅\n𝑝\n𝑡\n. Observe that \n𝑠\n≥\n1\n, \n𝑘\n=\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n+\n𝑠\n⋅\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n𝑟\n and \n𝑟\n<\n𝑝\n𝑡\n. The idea is that \n𝑠\n represents the digits of \n(\n𝑘\n−\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n)\n after the \n𝑡\n position. Our goal is to prove that \nΦ\n is concentrated on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑝\n𝑡\n or reducible on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n. For this, we assume that \nΦ\n satisfies neither of these properties and show that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑘\n)\n=\n𝑧\n. This would imply that \nΦ\n is trivial on \n𝑘\n.\n\nNext, we consider the \n𝑝\n-group2\n2\nSyl\n1\n is simply the trivial group containing one element. The group is isomorphic to \nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n1\n)\n=\nAut\n⁡\n(\nIS\n1\n)\n=\n𝔖\n1\n.\n\n\t\nΓ\n≔\nSyl\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n×\n(\nSyl\n𝑝\n𝑡\n×\n⋯\n×\nSyl\n𝑝\n𝑡\n)\n⏟\n𝑠\n−\ntimes\n×\n(\nSyl\n1\n×\n⋯\n×\nSyl\n1\n)\n⏟\n𝑟\n−\ntimes\n\t\n\ntogether with the fixed points \nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐾\n𝑘\n)\n. By Section A.4, we get that the set of fixed points is equal to\n\n\t\nFP\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐾\n𝑘\n)\n=\n{\n𝐶\n⟨\n𝐴\n1\n,\n𝐴\n2\n…\n,\n𝐴\n1\n+\n𝑠\n,\nIS\n1\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n1\n⟩\n:\n𝐶\n∈\n𝒢\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n,\n𝐴\n1\n∈\nFP\n(\nSyl\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n)\n,\n𝐴\n𝑖\n∈\nFP\n(\nSyl\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n)\n}\n.\n\t\n\nObserve that the last \n𝑟\n elements of each fixed point are always independent sets of size \n1\n. Hence, we write \n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐴\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐴\n1\n+\n𝑠\n⟩\n instead of \n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐴\n1\n​\n…\n,\n𝐴\n1\n+\n𝑠\n,\nIS\n1\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n1\n⟩\n from now on.\n\nIn our analysis, we mainly consider fixed points where each \n𝐴\n𝑖\n is either an independent set or a clique. We write \n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n⊎\nIS\n𝑟\n for the graph with \n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n vertices and edge set \n{\n{\n𝑖\n,\n𝑗\n}\n:\n1\n≤\n𝑖\n<\n𝑗\n≤\n1\n+\n𝑠\n}\n. Observe that a graph \n𝐺\n=\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐴\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐴\n1\n+\n𝑠\n⟩\n contains \n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n𝑝\n𝑡\n as a subgraph whenever \n𝐶\n contains an edge \n{\n𝑢\n,\n𝑣\n}\n for \n1\n≤\n𝑢\n,\n𝑣\n≤\n1\n+\n𝑠\n. This is because \n𝐺\n contains two components of size \n𝑝\n𝑡\n (or \n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n) that are completely connected to each other.\n\nClaim 3.\n\nIt holds \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n⊎\nIS\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n.\n\n{claimproof}\n\nAssume that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n⊎\nIS\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n<\n𝑧\n. Then, there is a graph \n𝐶\n∈\n𝒢\n1\n+\n𝑠\n with \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝐶\n⊎\nIS\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n<\n𝑧\n and \n𝐶\n has minimal number of edges amongst all graphs \n𝐶\n′\n with \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝐶\n′\n⊎\nIS\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n<\n𝑧\n. Observe that \n𝐶\n contains at least one edge since \nΦ\n⁡\n(\nIS\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n and \n1\n+\n𝑠\n≥\n2\n. Further, observe that all proper sub-points of \n(\n𝐶\n⊎\nIS\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n are fixed points of the form \n(\n𝐶\n′\n⊎\nIS\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n, where \n𝐶\n′\n is an proper edge-subgraph of \n𝐶\n (see Remark A.12). This means that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝐶\n′\n⊎\nIS\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n for all proper edge-subgraphs. This allows us to apply Section 1.2 to show that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝐶\n⊎\nIS\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n is nonvanishing mod \n𝑝\n. Lastly, observe that \n(\n𝐶\n⊎\nIS\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n contains \n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n𝑝\n𝑡\n as a subgraph, meaning that \nΦ\n is concentrated on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑝\n𝑡\n. This implies that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n⊎\nIS\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n since we assume that \nΦ\n is not concentrated on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑝\n𝑡\n.\n\nNext, we use the following result to strengthen Claim 3.\n\nClaim 4.\n\nIf \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n; then either \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⁡\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n; or \nΦ\n is reducible on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n.\n\n{claimproof}\n\nAssume otherwise, then there exists an \n𝐴\n∈\nFP\n⁡\n(\nSyl\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n)\n such that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐴\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n<\n𝑧\n. We define \nΦ\n~\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n≔\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐺\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n. Observe that \nΦ\n~\n​\n(\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n)\n=\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n and \nΦ\n~\n​\n(\n𝐴\n)\n=\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐴\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n<\n𝑧\n. Thus \nΦ\n~\n is nontrivial on \n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n which shows that \nΦ\n is reducible on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n.\n\nClaim 4 shows \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n⊎\nIS\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n. Let \n𝐷\n∈\n𝒢\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n be a graph such that each vertex is connected to each other vertex expect for vertex \n2\n which is isolated.\n\nClaim 5.\n\nIf \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n⊎\nIS\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n then \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐷\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⁡\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n\n{claimproof}\n\nFirst, observe that the graph \n𝐺\n≔\nIS\n𝑟\n⊎\n(\n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n​\n⟨\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n is isomorphic to the graph \n𝐺\n′\n≔\n(\n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n⊎\nIS\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n. Second, observe that \n𝐹\n≔\n𝐷\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⁡\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n is isomorphic to a subgraph of \n𝐹\n′\n≔\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⊎\n(\n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n𝐾\n𝑟\n⟩\n)\n. Lastly, observe that \n𝐹\n′\n is a subgraph of \n𝐺\n since \n𝑝\n𝑡\n>\n𝑟\n. This implies \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n)\n≥\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n′\n)\n≥\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n′\n)\n=\n𝑧\n which proves the claim.\n\nClaim 5 is the base case of the proof of Claim 6 which we prove next.\n\nClaim 6.\n\nIf \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n⊎\nIS\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⁡\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n then \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n.\n\n{claimproof}\n\nRecall that the vertices of a graph \n𝐺\n∈\n𝒢\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n are labeled from 1 to \n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n. For a graph \n𝐺\n∈\n𝒢\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n, we write \ndeg\n𝐺\n⁡\n(\n2\n)\n for the degree of the second vertex in \n𝐺\n. We show the statement via induction by using the following induction hypothesis. For all \n𝑛\n∈\n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n}\n we get \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n for all graphs \n𝐺\n∈\n𝒢\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n with \ndeg\n𝐺\n⁡\n(\n2\n)\n≤\n𝑛\n,\n\nThe base case of the induction is already shown by Claim 5 since for each graph \n𝐺\n with \ndeg\n𝐺\n⁡\n(\n2\n)\n=\n0\n the graph \n𝐺\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n is a subgraph of \n𝐷\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⁡\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n.\n\nFor the induction step, we can assume \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n for all graphs \n𝐺\n∈\n𝒢\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n with \ndeg\n𝐺\n⁡\n(\n2\n)\n≤\n𝑛\n. Our goal is to show the statement for all graphs \n𝐺\n with \ndeg\n𝐺\n⁡\n(\n2\n)\n=\n𝑛\n+\n1\n. {citemize}\n\nIn the first step, we consider the set \nℱ\n of all graphs in \n𝒢\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n with \ndeg\n𝐺\n⁡\n(\n2\n)\n=\n𝑛\n+\n1\n that contain at least one edge of the form \n{\n2\n,\n𝑔\n}\n for some \n𝑔\n≤\n1\n+\n𝑠\n. We show that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n for all graphs \n𝐹\n∈\nℱ\n. Assume otherwise and \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n<\n𝑧\n for some graph \n𝐹\n∈\nℱ\n, then we can find a graph \n𝐹\n∈\nℱ\n with minimal number of edges and \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n<\n𝑧\n. We show that all proper sub-points of \n𝐹\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n evaluate to \n𝑧\n on \nΦ\n. According to Remark A.12, these proper sub-points have the form \n𝐹\n′\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n, where \n𝐹\n′\n is a proper edge-subgraph of \n𝐹\n.\n\nFirst, let \n𝐹\n′\n be a proper edge-subgraph with \ndeg\n𝐹\n′\n⁡\n(\n2\n)\n=\n𝑛\n. In this case, our induction hypothesis ensures that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n′\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n. Otherwise, \ndeg\n𝐹\n′\n⁡\n(\n2\n)\n=\n𝑛\n+\n1\n which implies that \n𝐹\n′\n still contains the edge \n{\n2\n,\n𝑔\n}\n, thus \n𝐹\n′\n∈\nℱ\n. Because \n𝐹\n′\n has strictly fewer edges than \n𝐹\n, we obtain \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n′\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n.\n\nThis implies that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n′\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n for all proper sub-points, thus Section 1.2 shows that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n is nonvanishing. Lastly, Observe that \n𝐹\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n contains \n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n𝑝\n𝑡\n as a subgraph since \n𝐹\n contains the edge \n{\n2\n,\n𝑔\n}\n for \n𝑔\n≤\n1\n+\n𝑠\n. This means that \nΦ\n is concentrated on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑝\n𝑡\n. However, this cannot happen since we assume that \nΦ\n is not concentrated on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑝\n𝑡\n, hence \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n for all graphs \n𝐹\n∈\nℱ\n. Further, we get \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n for all \n𝐹\n∈\nℱ\n. Otherwise, \nΦ\n is reducible on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n due to Claim 4.\n\nDue to the first case, we assume that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⁡\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n for all \n𝐹\n∈\nℱ\n. In the following, we consider a graph \n𝐺\n with \ndeg\n𝐺\n⁡\n(\n2\n)\n=\n𝑛\n+\n1\n that is not in \nℱ\n and show that \n𝐺\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n is isomorphic to a subgraph of \n𝐹\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⁡\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n for some \n𝐹\n∈\nℱ\n. This implies \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n as \nΦ\n is edge-monotone.\n\nFirst, observe that we can find an edge \n{\n2\n,\n𝑔\n}\n with \n𝑠\n+\n2\n≤\n𝑔\n because vertex \n2\n has at least one adjacent edge in \n𝐺\n. Next, we construct a graph \n𝐹\n∈\n𝒢\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n with\n\n\t\n𝐸\n(\n𝐹\n)\n:=\n{\n{\n1\n,\n2\n}\n}\n∪\n{\n{\n2\n,\n𝑢\n}\n:\n{\n2\n,\n𝑢\n}\n∈\n𝐸\n(\n𝐺\n)\n}\n∖\n{\n{\n2\n,\n𝑔\n}\n}\n∪\n{\n{\n𝑢\n,\n𝑣\n}\n:\n𝑢\n≠\n2\n≠\n𝑣\n,\n𝑢\n≠\n𝑣\n}\n.\n\t\n\nWe obtain \n𝐹\n by adding the edge \n{\n1\n,\n2\n}\n, removing the edge \n{\n2\n,\n𝑔\n}\n and adding all possible edges \n{\n𝑢\n,\n𝑣\n}\n with \n𝑢\n≠\n1\n≠\n𝑣\n. Observe that \ndeg\n𝐹\n⁡\n(\n2\n)\n=\n𝑛\n+\n1\n and that \n𝐹\n∈\nℱ\n. We show that there is a bijective function \n𝜋\n:\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n→\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n such that \n𝜋\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n is a subgraph of \n𝐹\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n. Note that the vertex set of \n𝐺\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n is equal to \n{\n1\n}\n×\n{\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n}\n∪\n{\n2\n,\n…\n,\n1\n+\n𝑠\n}\n×\n{\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑝\n𝑡\n}\n∪\n{\n2\n+\n𝑠\n,\n…\n,\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n}\n×\n{\n1\n}\n. Next, we define the permutation\n\n\t\n𝜋\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝑥\n,\n𝑦\n)\n)\n≔\n{\n(\n1\n,\n𝑥\n−\n𝑠\n−\n1\n)\n\t\nif \n𝑥\n≥\n𝑠\n+\n2\n,\n\n\n(\n𝑦\n+\n𝑠\n+\n1\n,\n1\n)\n\t\nif \n𝑥\n=\n1\n and \n𝑦\n≤\n𝑟\n,\n\n\n(\n𝑥\n,\n𝑦\n)\n\t\notherwise\n.\n\t\n\nObserve that this mapping is possible since \n𝑟\n<\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n. Consult Figure 1 for an illustration of a demonstration of \n𝜋\n. We show that \n𝜋\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n is a subgraph of \n𝐹\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n by considering the following case distinction. We consider a case distinction\n\n– \n\nCase \n𝑎\n≠\n1\n or \n𝑥\n≠\n1\n: With lose of generality, we assume that \n𝑥\n≠\n1\n. This implies \n𝜋\n⁡\n(\n(\n,\n,\n,\n)\n)\n=\n(\n𝑥\n,\n𝑦\n)\n. Since \n{\n𝑏\n+\n𝑠\n+\n1\n,\n𝑦\n+\n𝑠\n+\n1\n}\n∈\n𝐹\n, we get that \n𝜋\n⁡\n(\n(\n,\n,\n,\n)\n)\n and \n𝜋\n⁡\n(\n(\n,\n,\n,\n)\n)\n are adjacent in \n𝐹\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n.\n\n• \n\nCase \n𝑎\n≤\n1\n+\n𝑠\n and \n𝑥\n≥\n2\n+\n𝑠\n: We consider another case distinction:\n\n– \n\nCase \n𝑎\n≠\n1\n: We get \n𝜋\n⁡\n(\n(\n,\n,\n,\n)\n)\n=\n(\n𝑎\n,\n𝑏\n)\n and \n𝜋\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝑥\n,\n𝑦\n)\n)\n=\n(\n1\n,\n𝑥\n−\n𝑠\n−\n1\n)\n. Observe that \n{\n𝑎\n,\n1\n}\n∈\n𝐹\n since \n{\n1\n,\n𝑔\n}\n∈\n𝐹\n, for all \n2\n≤\n𝑔\n≤\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n. Again, observe that \n{\n𝑏\n+\n𝑠\n+\n1\n,\n1\n}\n∈\n𝐹\n since \n{\n1\n,\n𝑔\n}\n∈\n𝐹\n, for all \n2\n≤\n𝑔\n≤\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n. This implies that \n𝜋\n⁡\n(\n(\n,\n,\n,\n)\n)\n and \n𝜋\n⁡\n(\n(\n,\n,\n,\n)\n)\n are adjacent in \n𝐹\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n\n• \n\nCase \n𝑎\n≥\n2\n+\n𝑠\n and \n𝑥\n≤\n2\n+\n𝑠\n: Analog to the last case.\n\nFigure 1:Graphs \n𝐺\n and \n𝐹\n that are transformed into the inhabited graphs \n𝐺\n′\n≔\n𝐺\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n27\n,\nIS\n9\n,\nIS\n1\n,\nIS\n1\n,\nIS\n1\n,\nIS\n1\n⟩\n that is not in \nℱ\n and a graph \n𝐹\n′\n≔\n𝐹\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐾\n27\n,\nIS\n9\n,\nIS\n1\n,\nIS\n1\n,\nIS\n1\n,\nIS\n1\n⟩\n that is in \nℱ\n. The graph \n𝐺\n′\n is isomorphic to a subgraph of \n𝐹\n′\n; the isomorphism \n𝜋\n maps vertices of \n𝐺\n′\n to vertices of \n𝐹\n′\n that have a common color; the colors of the edges demonstrate that \n𝜋\n is indeed an isomorphism onto a subgraph of \n𝐹\n′\n. Gray edges of \n𝐹\n′\n symbolize extra edges in \n𝐹\n′\n that do not have corresponding edges in \n𝐺\n′\n. Note that the two orange graphs actually have 27 nodes.\n\nHence, \n𝐺\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n is isomorphic to a subgraph of \n𝐹\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⁡\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n. Since \nΦ\n is edge-monotone, we get that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n which ends our induction step. \\claimqedhere\n\nThus, we get \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n. We show that this is enough to prove \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑘\n)\n=\n𝑧\n.\n\nClaim 7.\n\nIf \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n then \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝐾\n𝑘\n=\n𝑧\n.\n\n{claimproof}\n\nFirst, we apply Claim 4 to our assumption\n\n\t\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n\t\n\nand obtain that\n\n\t\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n.\n\t\n\nNext, assume that the statement is false then there are fixed points \n𝐴\n2\n,\n…\n,\n𝐴\n𝑠\n+\n1\n∈\nFP\n⁡\n(\nSyl\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n)\n such that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n)\n<\n𝑧\n, where \n𝐹\n≔\n(\n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\n𝐴\n2\n,\n…\n,\n𝐴\n𝑠\n+\n1\n⟩\n. Here, \n𝐴\n2\n,\n…\n,\n𝐴\n𝑠\n+\n1\n are chosen minimally. We get that there is at least one fixed point \n𝐴\n𝑗\n with \n𝐴\n𝑗\n≠\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n. After reordering, we may assume \n𝑗\n=\n2\n.\n\nNext, we define the graph parameter\n\n\t\nΦ\n~\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n​\n⟨\n𝐺\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n𝐴\n3\n,\n…\n,\n𝐴\n𝑠\n+\n1\n⟩\n)\n.\n\t\n\nIt is now easy to see that \nΦ\n~\n​\n(\n𝐾\n𝑝\n​\n⟨\n𝐴\n2\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n is isomorphic to \n𝐹\n. However, this implies that\n\n\t\nΦ\n~\n​\n(\n𝐾\n𝑝\n​\n⟨\n𝐴\n2\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n<\n𝑧\n.\n\t\n\nFurther, we have \nΦ\n~\n​\n(\n𝐾\n𝑝\n​\n⟨\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n since \n𝐴\n2\n,\n…\n,\n𝐴\n𝑠\n+\n1\n were chosen minimally and \n𝐴\n2\n≠\nIS\n𝑝\n𝑡\n. This shows that \nΦ\n~\n is nontrivial on \n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n which means that \nΦ\n is reducible on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n. However, we assume that this is not the case, thus \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝐾\n1\n+\n𝑠\n+\n𝑟\n)\n​\n⟨\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n,\n…\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑡\n⟩\n)\n=\n𝑧\n.\n\nThis shows that \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑘\n)\n=\n𝑧\n which implies that \nΦ\n is trivial on \n𝑘\n. Thus we get that if \nΦ\n is nontrivial on \n𝑘\n then \nΦ\n is either concentrated on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑝\n𝑡\n or reducible on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n.\n\n4.4Hardness and Tight Bounds for the General Case\n\nIn this section, we prove Section 1. The main idea is that if \nΦ\n is nontrivial on \n𝑘\n, Section 1.2 ensures that either \nΦ\n is concentrated or reducible on \n𝑘\n with respect to some prime power \n𝑝\n𝑡\n or \n𝑝\n𝑡\n+\n1\n. If \nΦ\n is concentrated, we use Section 1.2 and we are done. Otherwise, we use the reduction shown in Section 1.2 which allows us to use Theorem 4.1. The reduction constructs a graph parameter \nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n that is nontrivial on large prime powers.\n\nDefinition 4.5.\n\nLet \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℚ\n, denote an edge-monotone graph parameter and \n𝑞\n:\nNature\n→\nNature\n a computable function and write \nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n for the set of all integers \n𝑘\n such that \nΦ\n is reducible on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n.\n\nThe set \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n)\n≔\n{\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n:\n𝑘\n∈\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n}\n is the set of all values on which \nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n is nontrivial. However, we need that the set \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n)\n is computable.\n\nLemma 4.6.\n\nLet \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℚ\n be a graph parameter and \n𝑞\n:\nNature\n→\nNature\n be a computable unbounded monotonically increasing function. Then the sets \nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n and \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n)\n are computable. Further, there is an algorithm that for \n𝑥\n∈\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n)\n computes \n𝐶\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑠\n and \n𝐻\n2\n,\n…\n,\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n∈\n𝒢\n such that the graph parameter \nΦ\n~\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n≔\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐺\n,\n𝐻\n2\n​\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n⟩\n)\n is nontrivial on \n𝑥\n with \n𝑘\n≔\n(\n𝑥\n+\n∑\n𝑖\n=\n2\n𝑠\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n𝑖\n)\n|\n)\n∈\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n and \n𝑘\n is minimal under all values \n𝑗\n∈\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n with \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑗\n)\n=\n𝑥\n.\n\nProof 4.7.\n\nFirst, we construct an algorithm \n𝔸\n to check if \n𝑘\n∈\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n. For this, we compute the value \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n and check if it is a prime power. We now iterate over all \n𝑠\n∈\n[\n𝑘\n]\n, all graphs \n𝐶\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑠\n and graphs \n𝐻\n2\n,\n…\n,\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n∈\n𝒢\n with \n𝑘\n=\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n+\n∑\n𝑖\n=\n2\n𝑠\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n𝑖\n)\n|\n. Observe that only finitely many graphs with this property exist. For each set of graphs, we check whether the graph parameter \nΦ\n~\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n≔\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐺\n,\n𝐻\n2\n​\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n⟩\n)\n is nontrivial on \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n and stop if this the case. In this case, we return the graphs and say that \n𝑘\n∈\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n. Otherwise, we return that \n𝑘\n∉\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n.\n\nTo check \n𝑥\n∈\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n)\n, we check for \n𝑘\n=\n2\n, \n𝑘\n=\n3\n, \n…\n if \n𝑘\n∈\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n and \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n=\n𝑥\n. We stop if we find an \n𝑘\n with \n𝑘\n∈\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n and \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n=\n𝑥\n and return that \n𝑥\n is in \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n)\n. Further, we use algorithm \n𝔸\n to compute the graph \n𝐶\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑠\n and graphs \n𝐻\n2\n,\n…\n,\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n∈\n𝒢\n. Alternatively, we stop if we find the first value \n𝑘\n with \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n>\n𝑥\n and return that \n𝑥\n is not in \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n)\n. Observe that one of these two cases will always occur after a finite amount of steps since \n𝑞\n is an unbounded monotonically increasing function.\n\nNext, we show how the graph parameter \nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑝\n is constructed and that there is a reduction from \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑝\n)\n to \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n.\n\nLemma 4.8.\n\nLet \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℚ\n be an edge monotone graph parameter and let \n𝑞\n:\nNature\n→\nNature\n be a computable unbounded monotonically increasing function. Then there is a computable graph parameter \nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n with:\n\n• \n\nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n is nontrivial exactly on \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n)\n.\n\n• \n\nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n is edge-monotone.\n\n• \n\nThere is parameterized Turing reduction from \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n)\n to \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n.\n\nProof 4.9.\n\nLet \n𝐺\n be a graph. We set \n𝑘\n≔\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n. According to Lemma 4.6 we can check if \n𝑘\n∈\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n)\n and compute a graph \n𝐶\n𝑘\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑠\n𝑘\n and graphs \n𝐻\n2\n𝑘\n,\n…\n,\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n𝑘\n𝑘\n∈\n𝒢\n such that the graph parameter \nΦ\n~\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n≔\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n𝑘\n​\n⟨\n𝐺\n,\n𝐻\n2\n𝑘\n​\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n𝑘\n𝑘\n⟩\n)\n is nontrivial on \n𝑘\n. We use this to define the following graph parameter:\n\n\t\nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n{\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n𝑘\n​\n⟨\n𝐺\n,\n𝐻\n2\n𝑘\n,\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n𝑘\n𝑘\n⟩\n)\n\t\n if \n𝑘\n≔\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n∈\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n)\n\n\n 0\n\t\n otherwise\n.\n\t\n\nFirst, observe that \nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n is a computable graph parameter. Further, it is easy to check that \nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n is edge-monotone, whenever \nΦ\n is. Lastly, \nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n is nontrivial exactly on \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n)\n by construction.\n\nNext, we prove the reduction. For a \n𝑘\n-vertex graph \n𝐻\n and a \n𝐻\n-colored graph \n𝐺\n, we want to compute \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n. First, check if \n𝑘\n∉\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n)\n. If this is the case then \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n0\n since \nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n is trivial on \n𝑘\n. Since \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n)\n is computable, this can be done in time \nℎ\n1\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n for some computable function \nℎ\n. We again use Lemma 4.6 to compute the graph \n𝐶\n𝑘\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑠\n𝑘\n and graphs \n𝐻\n2\n𝑘\n,\n…\n,\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n𝑘\n𝑘\n∈\n𝒢\n in time \nℎ\n1\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n. Observe that \n𝑐\n𝑘\n=\n∑\n𝑖\n=\n2\n𝑠\n𝑘\n#\n​\n𝑉\n​\n(\n𝐻\n𝑖\n𝑘\n)\n only depends on \n𝑘\n. Now, Section 1.2 allows us to compute \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n using an oracle for \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n+\n𝑐\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n. This shows that there is a parameterized Turing reduction from \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n)\n to \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n.\n\nWe define the function \n𝑝\n−\n:\nNature\n→\nNature\n that returns for each prime power \n𝑝\n𝑡\n to value \n𝑝\n𝑡\n−\n1\n. Further \n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n=\n1\n if \n𝑘\n is not a prime power. Now, because of Theorem 4.1, we can find for all \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n∈\nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n a nonvanishing graph \n𝐹\n that contain \n𝐾\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n,\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n as a subgraph. Thus we can use Theorem 3.5 to get lower bounds for \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n)\n. We show that these lower bounds propagate to \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n and \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n due to Lemma 4.8.\n\nTheorem 4.10.\n\nLet \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n𝑘\n→\n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n be an edge monotone graph parameter and let \n𝑞\n:\nNature\n→\nNature\n be a computable unbounded monotonically increasing function. Let \n𝑞\n′\n:\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n→\nNature\n be the restriction of \n𝑞\n to \nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n.\n\n• \n\nIf \n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n′\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n∈\n𝜔\n⁡\n(\n1\n)\n then \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n and \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n are both #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard.\n\n• \n\nThere is a constant \n𝛾\n>\n0\n such that for all \n𝑘\n∈\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n, there is no algorithm (that reads the whole input) that for every \n𝐺\n computes #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n (or \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n) in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛾\n​\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n)\n unless ETH fails. Here \n𝛾\n depends on \n𝑞\n but is independent of \nΦ\n and \n𝑘\n.\n\nProof 4.11.\n\nWe use Lemma 4.8 to construct the graph parameter \nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n. Let \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n∈\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n)\n, then according to Theorem 4.1 there exists a nonvanishing graph \n𝐻\n that contains \n𝐾\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n,\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n as a subgraph. Now, if \n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n′\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n∈\n𝜔\n⁡\n(\n1\n)\n then we can apply Section 1.2 to \nℎ\n:\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n→\nNature\n,\n𝑘\n↦\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n′\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n which proves that \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard. Lemma 4.8 ensures that \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard, too. Lastly, Lemma 3.7 shows that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard.\n\nFurther, assuming ETH, we can use Theorem 3.5 to get an integer \n𝑁\n and a constant \n𝛽\n such that for all \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≥\n𝑁\n with \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n∈\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n)\n, there is no algorithm (that reads the whole input) that for every \n𝐺\n computes \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n,\n𝒢\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛽\n​\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n)\n. Since \n𝑞\n is monotonically increasing and unbounded, we can find a value \n𝑁\n′\n such that \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≥\n𝑁\n if and only if \n𝑘\n≥\n𝑁\n′\n. Next, let \n𝑘\n∈\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n such that \n𝑘\n≥\n𝑁\n′\n. Assume that there is an algorithm that solves \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛽\n​\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n)\n. Now using Lemma 4.6, we can compute a graph \n𝐶\n𝑘\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑠\n𝑘\n and graphs \n𝐻\n2\n𝑘\n,\n…\n,\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n𝑘\n𝑘\n∈\n𝒢\n such that the graph parameter \nΦ\n~\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n≔\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n𝑘\n​\n⟨\n𝐺\n,\n𝐻\n2\n𝑘\n​\n…\n,\n𝐻\n𝑠\n𝑘\n𝑘\n⟩\n)\n coincides with \nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n on \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n-vertex graphs. Now, let \n𝑐\n𝑘\n≔\n𝑘\n−\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n. According to Section 1.2, we can use our algorithm for \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n to solve \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n,\n𝒢\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time\n\n\t\n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n+\n𝑐\n𝑘\n)\n𝛽\n​\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n+\n𝑐\n𝑘\n)\n)\n)\n=\n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛽\n​\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n)\n,\n\t\n\nwhich is not possible unless ETH fails. Next, set \n𝛽\n′\n≔\nmin\n⁡\n(\n𝛿\n𝑠\n,\n𝑝\n′\n,\n1\n/\n(\n𝑁\n′\n+\n1\n)\n)\n. Now for all \n𝑘\n<\n𝑁\n′\n, we obtain\n\n\t\n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛽\n′\n​\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n)\n=\n𝑜\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n)\n.\n\t\n\nsince \n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n≤\n𝑘\n. Such a running time is unconditionally unachievable for any algorithm that reads the whole input. Thus for all \n𝑘\n∈\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n, there is no algorithm (that reads the whole input) that for every \n𝐺\n computes \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛽\n′\n​\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n)\n unless ETH fails.\n\nLastly, we define \n𝐶\n≔\n4\n/\n𝛽\n′\n and \n𝛾\n≔\nmin\n⁡\n(\n𝛽\n′\n/\n2\n,\n1\n/\n(\n𝐶\n+\n1\n)\n)\n. Assume that we can compute on input \n𝐺\n the value #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛾\n​\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n)\n. If \n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n≥\n𝐶\n, then we can use Lemma 3.7 to get an algorithm that computes \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛾\n​\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n+\n2\n)\n. Now, we obtain that \n𝛾\n​\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n+\n2\n≤\n𝛽\n′\n/\n2\n⋅\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n+\n2\n≤\n𝛽\n′\n​\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n for \n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n≥\n𝐶\n=\n4\n/\n𝛽\n′\n. This implies that we can compute \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛽\n​\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n)\n which is not possible unless ETH fails. Otherwise \n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n<\n𝐶\n and we would obtain a sublinear algorithm for #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n which is unconditionally unachievable for any algorithm that reads the whole input.\n\n\\thmedgemon\nProof 4.12.\n\nWithout loss of generality, we assume that the codomain of \nΦ\n is equal to \n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑐\n}\n. Otherwise, we use Lemma 2.3. Let \n𝑝\n be the first prime number such that \n𝑐\n<\n𝑝\n and write \n𝑀\nΦ\n for the set of values \n𝑘\n on which \nΦ\n is nontrivial. Let \n𝑞\n𝑝\n be the function that on input \n𝑘\n returns the largest prime power \n𝑝\n𝑡\n such that \n𝑝\n𝑡\n≤\n𝑘\n. We define the function \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≔\n𝑞\n𝑝\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n/\n𝑝\n and \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≔\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n/\n𝑝\n=\n𝑝\n−\n​\n(\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n. Observe that \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≥\n𝑘\n/\n𝑝\n2\n thus \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n,\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n∈\nΩ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n. Further, \n𝑞\n is a computable unbounded monotonically increasing function with \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n/\n𝑝\n+\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≤\n𝑘\n. This means that we can apply Section 1.2 on all \n𝑘\n∈\n𝑀\nΦ\n to get that \nΦ\n is concentrated on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n/\n𝑝\n=\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n or \nΦ\n is reducible on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n.\n\nWrite \nCo\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n for the set of all \n𝑘\n∈\n𝑀\nΦ\n such that \nΦ\n is concentrated on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n/\n𝑝\n. Further, write \nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n for the set of all \n𝑘\n∈\n𝑀\nΦ\n such that \nΦ\n is reducible on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n. Now, Section 1.2 implies that \nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n∪\nCo\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n=\n𝑀\nΦ\n.\n\nIf we assume that \n𝑀\nΦ\n is infinite then at least one of the sets \nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n or \nCo\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n has to be infinite as well. If \nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n is infinite, then we use Theorem 4.10 to show that both \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n and \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n are #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard. If \nCo\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n is infinite, then we can use Section 1.2 to show that both \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n and \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n are #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard.\n\nIf we assume ETH, then we can use Theorem 4.10 to get a constant \n𝛿\n𝑠\n,\n𝑐\n such that for all \n𝑘\n∈\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n no algorithm computes #\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n (or \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n) in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛿\n𝑠\n,\n𝑐\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n. On the other side, we can use Section 1.2 to get a constant \n𝛿\n𝑐\n,\n𝑐\n such that for all \n𝑘\n∈\nCo\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n no algorithm computes #\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n (or \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n) in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛿\n𝑐\n,\n𝑐\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n. We obtain our tight bounds under ETH by simply picking \n𝛿\n𝑐\n=\nmin\n⁡\n(\n𝛿\n𝑠\n,\n𝑐\n,\n𝛿\n𝑐\n,\n𝑐\n)\n/\n𝑝\n.\n\n4.5Hardness for unbounded codomain\n\nIn this section, we show that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n and \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n are still #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard when our codomain is smaller than \n⌊\n(\n1\n−\n𝜀\n)\n​\n𝑘\n1\n/\n𝛼\n⌋\n. We also get lower bounds which are not tight anymore.\n\n\\thmedgemonDomain\nProof 4.13.\n\nFix \n𝛽\n≔\n1\n−\n1\n/\n𝛼\n, and \n𝛿\n>\n0\n such that \n(\n1\n+\n𝛿\n)\n𝛼\n​\n(\n1\n−\n𝜀\n)\n𝛼\n<\n1\n. According to [HW08, (22.19.2)], there exists a number \n𝑁\n𝛿\n such that for all \n𝑘\n≥\n𝑁\n𝛿\n there exists a prime number \n𝑝\n with \n𝑘\n<\n𝑝\n<\n(\n1\n+\n𝛿\n)\n​\n𝑘\n. Let \n𝑁\n𝛿\n′\n be a number such that \n𝑘\n≥\n𝑁\n𝛿\n′\n implies \n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≥\n𝑁\n𝛿\n.\n\nFor all \n𝑘\n, we write \n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n for the smallest prime number \n𝑝\n with \n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n<\n𝑝\n=\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n. Note that [HW08, (22.19.2)] implies that for all \n𝑘\n≥\n𝑁\n𝛿\n′\n we have \n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n<\n(\n1\n+\n𝛿\n)\n​\n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n. Further, we define \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≔\n𝑔\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n𝛼\n and \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≔\n𝑔\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n𝛼\n−\n1\n. Observe that \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n is a computable unbounded monotonically increasing function.\n\nFurther, for \n𝑘\n≥\n𝑁\n𝛿\n′\n we get\n\n\t\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n+\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n=\n(\n1\n+\n1\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n​\n𝑞\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≤\n(\n1\n+\n𝛿\n)\n𝛼\n​\n(\n1\n−\n𝜀\n)\n𝛼\n​\n(\n1\n+\n1\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n​\n𝑘\n.\n\t\n\nFor \n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n large enough, we get that\n\n\t\n(\n1\n+\n𝛿\n)\n𝛼\n​\n(\n1\n−\n𝜀\n)\n𝛼\n⏟\n<\n1\n⋅\n(\n1\n+\n1\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n<\n1\n\t\n\nimplying \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n/\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n+\n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≤\n𝑘\n.\n\nSince the function \n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n is unbounded and monotonically increasing, there is a constant \n𝑁\n such that, for all \n𝑘\n≥\n𝑁\n, we can use Section 1.2. This means that for all \n𝑘\n∈\n𝑀\nΦ\n with \n𝑘\n≥\n𝑁\n we get that \nΦ\n is concentrated on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n/\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n=\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n or \nΦ\n is reducible on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n.\n\nWrite \nCo\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n for the set of all \n𝑘\n∈\n𝑀\nΦ\n such that \nΦ\n is concentrated on \n𝑘\n with respect to \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n. Further, write \nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n for the set of all \n𝑘\n∈\n𝑀\nΦ\n such that \nΦ\n is reducible on \n𝑘\n with respect to \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n.\n\nIf we assume that \n𝑀\nΦ\n is infinite then at least one of the sets \nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n or \nCo\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n has to be infinite as well. If \nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n is infinite, then we use Theorem 4.10 to show that both \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n and \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n are #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard. If \nCo\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n is infinite, then we can use Section 1.2 to show that both \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n and \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n are #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard.\n\nIf we assume ETH, then we can use Theorem 4.10 to get a constant \n𝛿\n𝑠\n,\n𝑐\n such that for all \n𝑘\n∈\nSc\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n with \n𝑘\n≥\n𝑁\n no algorithm computes #\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n (or \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n) in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛿\n𝑠\n,\n𝑐\n​\n𝑞\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n/\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n=\n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛿\n𝑠\n,\n𝑐\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n.\n\nObserve that \nℎ\n∈\nΩ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n𝛽\n)\n since \nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n1\n/\n𝛽\n=\n𝑔\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n𝛼\n≥\n(\n1\n−\n𝜀\n)\n𝛼\n​\n𝑘\n. This allows us to use Section 1.2 to get a constant \n𝛿\n𝑐\n,\n𝑐\n such that for all \n𝑘\n∈\nCo\nΦ\n,\n𝑞\n with \n𝑘\n>\n𝑁\n no algorithm computes #\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n (or \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n) in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛿\n𝑐\n,\n𝑐\n​\nℎ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n. Next, we set \n𝛿\n=\n(\n1\n−\n𝜀\n)\n𝛼\n−\n1\n​\nmin\n⁡\n(\n𝛿\n𝑠\n,\n𝑐\n,\n𝛿\n𝑐\n,\n𝑐\n,\n1\n/\n(\n𝑁\n+\n1\n)\n)\n. Observe that for \n𝑘\n≥\n𝑁\n we obtain\n\n\t\nmin\n⁡\n(\n𝛿\n𝑠\n,\n𝑐\n,\n𝛿\n𝑐\n,\n𝑐\n)\n⋅\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≥\nmin\n⁡\n(\n𝛿\n𝑠\n,\n𝑐\n,\n𝛿\n𝑐\n,\n𝑐\n)\n⋅\n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n𝛼\n−\n1\n≥\nmin\n⁡\n(\n𝛿\n𝑠\n,\n𝑐\n,\n𝛿\n𝑐\n,\n𝑐\n)\n⋅\n(\n1\n−\n𝜀\n)\n𝛼\n−\n1\n​\n𝑘\n𝛼\n−\n1\n𝛼\n≥\n𝛿\n​\n𝑘\n𝛽\n,\n\t\n\nwhere we used \n𝑔\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n>\n𝑓\nΦ\n​\n(\n𝑘\n)\n. Thus, for all \n𝑘\n∈\n𝑀\nΦ\n with \n𝑘\n≥\n𝑁\n, no algorithm computes for every graph \n𝐺\n the number #\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n (or \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n) in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛿\n​\n𝑘\n𝛽\n)\n. For \n𝑘\n<\n𝑁\n, we obtain\n\n\t\n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛿\n​\n𝑘\n𝛽\n)\n=\n𝑜\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n)\n.\n\t\n\nsince \n𝑘\n𝛽\n≤\n𝑘\n. Such a running time is unconditionally unachievable for any algorithm that reads the whole input. Thus for all nontrivial \n𝑘\n≥\n3\n, no algorithm (that reads the whole input) computes the function #\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n (or \n#\ncp\n-\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n) in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛿\n​\n𝑘\n𝛽\n)\n.\n\n5The Sub-Basis Representation of \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n\nMany counting problems \n#\n​\n𝑃\n in graphs can be expressed as a linear combination of graph homomorphisms counts. This means that we can find a finite number of non-isomorphic graphs \n𝐻\n and coefficients \n𝛼\n𝐻\n such that for all graphs \n𝐺\n, we have\n\n\t\n#\n​\n𝑃\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n∑\n𝐻\n𝛼\n𝐻\n⋅\n#\nHom\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n.\n\t\t\n(5)\n\nWe say that (5) is the hom-basis representation of \n#\n​\n𝑃\n.\n\nIn [CDM17], the authors used that the set \n{\n#\nHom\n(\n𝐻\n→\n⋆\n)\n:\n𝐻\n∈\n𝒢\n}\n forms a basis of an infinite dimension vector space which allowed them to rewrite sums of homomorphisms using different basis. One basis they used was the sub-basis \n{\n#\nSub\n(\n𝐻\n→\n⋆\n)\n:\n𝐻\n∈\n𝒢\n}\n where #\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n is the number of subgraphs in \n𝐺\n that are isomorphic to \n𝐻\n. This means that we can do a change of basis and rewrite (5) using the sub-basis representation.\n\nIn this section we analyze the sub-basis representation of \nΦ\n and \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n and see that the coefficients the sub-basis are in direct relationship to the alternating enumerator. This enables us to find nontrivial graph parameters \nΦ\n such that the \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n becomes easy to compute. Lastly, in Section 5.3, we compare the sub-basis representation of \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n with the \ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n-basis representation of \n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n which helps us to understand why there are graph parameters \nΦ\n such that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard while \n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is FPT solvable.\n\n5.1Using the Sub-Basis for Graph Parameters\nTheorem 5.1.\n\nLet \nΦ\n𝑘\n:\n𝒢\n𝑘\n→\nℚ\n be any graph parameter. Then, there are coefficients \n𝛼\n𝐻\n𝑘\n such that\n\n\t\nΦ\n𝑘\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n∑\n𝐻\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n𝛼\n𝐻\n𝑘\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n.\n\t\n\nFurther, we have \n𝛼\n𝐻\n𝑘\n=\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n⋅\nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n.\n\nProof 5.2.\n\nWe first prove that we can write \nΦ\n𝑘\n as a linear combination of subgraph counts. For this, we define for all \n𝐻\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n the values\n\n\t\n𝛼\n𝐻\n𝑘\n≔\nΦ\n𝑘\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n−\n∑\n𝐹\n≠\n𝐻\n\n\n𝐹\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n𝛼\n𝐹\n𝑘\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n→\n𝐻\n)\n.\n\t\t\n(6)\n\nObserve that #\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n→\n𝐻\n)\n is only nonvanishing if \n𝐹\n is isomorphic to a subgraph of \n𝐻\n. Thus, Equation 6 is a well-defined recursive definition. Further, we obtain\n\n\t\nΦ\n𝑘\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n=\n𝛼\n𝐻\n𝑘\n+\n∑\n𝐹\n≠\n𝐻\n\n\n𝐹\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n𝛼\n𝐹\n𝑘\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n→\n𝐻\n)\n=\n∑\n𝐹\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n𝛼\n𝐹\n𝑘\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n→\n𝐻\n)\n,\n\t\n\nwhere the last equation is justified since \n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐻\n)\n=\n1\n. Lastly, we show that for each \n𝑘\n-vertex graph \n𝐻\n, we obtain \n𝛼\n𝐻\n𝑘\n=\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n⋅\nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n. For this, observe that \n𝐻\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n, we obtain\n\n\t\nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n=\n∑\n𝑆\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n⁡\n{\n𝑆\n}\n)\n​\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝑆\n\t\n=\n∑\n𝐹\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐹\n)\n⋅\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n)\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n→\n𝐻\n)\n\t\n\t\t\n=\n∑\n𝐹\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n∑\n𝐹\n′\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐹\n)\n⋅\n𝛼\n𝐹\n′\n𝑘\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n′\n→\n𝐹\n)\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n→\n𝐻\n)\n\t\n\t\t\n=\n∑\n𝐹\n′\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n𝛼\n𝐹\n′\n𝑘\n⋅\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐹\n′\n)\n∑\n𝐹\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐹\n)\n−\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐹\n′\n)\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n′\n→\n𝐹\n)\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n→\n𝐻\n)\n,\n\t\n\nwhere the last step is archived by swapping the summation. Next, we use that for all graphs \n𝐹\n′\n,\n𝐻\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n\n\t\n∑\n𝐹\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐹\n)\n−\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐹\n′\n)\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n′\n→\n𝐹\n)\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n→\n𝐻\n)\n=\n{\n 1\n\t\nif \n𝐹\n′\n=\n𝐻\n,\n\n\n 0\n\t\notherwise.\n\t\n\nThis equation is justified since the sum of Proof 5.2 is the same as the matrix product of \nExt\n−\n1\n and \nExt\n evaluated at position \n(\n𝐹\n′\n,\n𝐻\n)\n (see [CDM17, Section 3.1, (9)] for the definition of \nExt\n).3\n3\nExt\n is an infinite matrix whose rows and columns are enumerated by \n𝒢\n∗\n. For a graph \n𝐻\n and a graph \n𝐺\n we define \nExt\n(\n𝐻\n,\n𝐺\n)\n=\n[\n𝑉\n(\n𝐻\n)\n=\n𝑉\n(\n𝐺\n)\n]\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n, where \n[\n𝑉\n(\n𝐻\n)\n=\n𝑉\n(\n𝐺\n)\n]\n is one if \n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n=\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n and zero otherwise.\n Since \nExt\n−\n1\n⋅\nExt\n is the identity matrix, we obtain that the left side of Proof 5.2 is equal to \n1\n if \n𝐹\n′\n=\n𝐻\n and is equal to zero otherwise. Using Proof 5.2, we continue our computation and obtain \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n=\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n​\n𝛼\n𝐻\n𝑘\n.\n\nNow Theorem 5.1 allows us to rewrite \nΦ\n𝑘\n as a linear combination of subgraph counts. This in turn allows us to write #\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n𝑘\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n as a linear linear combination of subgraph counts. We use this fact later to show that #\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n𝑘\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n is easy to compute for certain graph parameters.\n\nTheorem 5.3.\n\nFor each \nΦ\n𝑘\n:\n𝒢\n𝑘\n→\nℚ\n with \nΦ\n𝑘\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n∑\n𝐻\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n𝛼\n𝐻\n𝑘\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n, we have\n\n\t\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n𝑘\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n∑\n𝐻\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n𝛼\n𝐻\n𝑘\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n∑\n𝐻\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n⋅\nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n,\n\t\n\nfor all \n𝐺\n∈\n𝒢\n.\n\nProof 5.4.\n\nWe first show the statement for \nΦ\n𝑘\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n where \n𝐻\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n is a fixed graph. For each graph \n𝐺\n, we obtain\n\n\t\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n𝑘\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n∑\n𝐴\n∈\n(\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n𝑘\n)\nΦ\n𝑘\n​\n(\n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n)\n=\n∑\n𝐴\n∈\n(\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n𝑘\n)\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n)\n.\n\t\n\nNext, we show that #\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n is equal to \n∑\n𝐴\n∈\n(\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n𝑘\n)\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n)\n. Note that #\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n counts the number of subgraphs \n𝐻\n′\n in \n𝐻\n that are isomorphic to \n𝐺\n and each \n𝐻\n′\n is in the set \nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n)\n if and only if \n𝐴\n=\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n′\n)\n. Thus \nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n⁡\n[\n𝐴\n]\n)\n forms a partition of the set \nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n. This shows \n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n𝑘\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n.\n\nFor the general case, we use\n\n\t\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\n𝛼\n​\nΦ\n𝑘\n+\n𝛽\n​\nΨ\n𝑘\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n𝛼\n​\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n𝑘\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n+\n𝛽\n​\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΨ\n𝑘\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n.\n\t\n\nWe obtain\n\n\t\n∑\n𝐻\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n𝛼\n𝐻\n𝑘\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n∑\n𝐻\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n⋅\nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n\t\n\ndue to Theorem 5.1.\n\n5.2\n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is Easy for Bounded Vertex Cover Size\n\nIn this section, we show that there are nontrivial graph parameters for which we can compute \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n easily. For this, the vertex-cover size of a graph \n𝐻\n plays an important role (that is, the size of the smallest vertex-cover of \n𝐻\n). We write \nVC\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n for the vertex-cover size of \n𝐻\n. Recall that the vertex-cover size is an upper bound for the treewidth of \n𝐻\n.\n\nBy [CDM17, Theorem 1.1], we can compute #\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n)\n​\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝑡\n+\n1\n)\n, where \n𝑡\n is the maximum treewidth in the spasm of \n𝐻\n. However, this value is upper bounded by the vertex-cover size of \n𝐻\n (see [CDM17, Fact 3.4]). This in turn means that we can compute #\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n)\n​\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\nVC\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n+\n1\n)\n.\n\nTheorem 5.5.\n\nIf \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℚ\n is a graph parameter such that there is a constant \n𝜏\n with \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n0\n for all graphs \n𝐺\n with \nVC\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n>\n𝜏\n, then \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is FPT and there is an algorithm that computes #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝜏\n+\n1\n)\n for some computable function \n𝑓\n.\n\nProof 5.6.\n\nFor each \n𝑘\n∈\nNature\n, we use \nΦ\n𝑘\n:\n𝒢\n𝑘\n→\nℚ\n to denote the restriction of \nΦ\n to \n𝑘\n-vertex graphs. Observe that \n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n𝑘\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n. From Theorem 5.3, we obtain\n\n\t\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n𝑘\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n∑\n𝐻\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n⋅\nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\nfor all \n𝐺\n∈\n𝒢\n.\n\t\n\nFor each graph \n𝐻\n with \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n≠\n0\n, we can compute #\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n​\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\nVC\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n+\n1\n)\n. Thus, we can compute #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n𝑘\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝜏\n+\n1\n)\n.\n\nCorollary 5.7.\n\nFor each \n𝑐\n∈\nNature\n, let \nΦ\n𝑐\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n≔\n(\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n)\n𝑐\n be a graph parameter. There is an algorithm that computes #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n𝑐\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝑐\n+\n1\n)\n for some computable function \n𝑓\n.\n\nProof 5.8.\n\nFor each fixed \n𝑐\n, we compute alternating enumerator of \nΦ\n𝑐\n which yields\n\n\t\nΦ\n𝑐\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n=\n∑\n𝑆\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\nΦ\n𝑐\n​\n(\n𝐻\n⁡\n{\n𝑆\n}\n)\n​\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝑆\n=\n∑\n𝑘\n=\n0\n𝑘\n(\n−\n1\n)\n𝑘\n​\n(\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n𝑘\n)\n​\n𝑘\n𝑐\n=\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n​\n∑\n𝑘\n=\n0\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n−\n𝑘\n​\n(\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n𝑘\n)\n​\n𝑘\n𝑐\n,\n\t\t\n(9)\n\nwhere we used that we can group together all \n(\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n𝑘\n)\n many graphs subgraphs of \n𝐻\n that contain \n𝑘\n edges. By [Aig07, Chapter 2.4, (9)], the sum (9) is zero whenever \n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n>\n𝑐\n. Meaning, that \nΦ\n^\n𝑐\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n=\n0\n for all graphs that contain more than \n𝑐\n edges, which implies that \nΦ\n^\n𝑐\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n≠\n0\n is only possible if \nVC\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n≤\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n≤\n𝑐\n. Now, Theorem 5.5 shows that we can compute #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n𝑐\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝑐\n+\n1\n)\n for some computable function \n𝑓\n.\n\nIn the following, we write \n𝑆\n𝑛\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑛\n for the star graph with \n𝑛\n vertices. The edge set \n𝑆\n𝑛\n is equal to \n{\n{\n1\n,\n𝑗\n}\n:\n2\n≤\n𝑗\n≤\n𝑛\n}\n. Next, we show that there are graph parameters whose codomain on \n𝑘\n-vertex graphs is equal to \n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑘\n}\n but \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is easy to compute.\n\nCorollary 5.9.\n\nLet \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝑆\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n→\n𝐺\n)\n denote the graph parameter that counts the number of universal vertices, then \nΦ\n𝑘\n:\n𝒢\n𝑘\n→\n{\n0\n,\n…\n​\n𝑘\n}\n and \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n can be computed in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n2\n)\n.\n\nProof 5.10.\n\nIt is easy to see that \nΦ\n𝑘\n maximal for \n𝐾\n𝑘\n and that \nΦ\n𝑘\n​\n(\n𝐾\n𝑘\n)\n=\n𝑘\n. This immediately implies that the codomain of \nΦ\n𝑘\n is equal to \n{\n0\n,\n…\n​\n𝑘\n}\n. Further, due to Theorem 5.3 we get \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n≠\n0\n if and only if \n𝐺\n=\n𝑆\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n. This allows to use Theorem 5.5 which shows that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n can be computed in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n2\n)\n since \nVC\n⁡\n(\n𝑆\n𝑛\n)\n=\n1\n.\n\n5.3\n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is Harder than \n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n\nWe write \n𝑃\n𝑛\n for the path graph that contains \n𝑛\n vertices.\n\nLemma 5.11.\n\nLet \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝑃\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n→\n𝐺\n)\n denote the graph parameter that counts the number of Hamiltonian paths, then\n\n• \n\n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard.\n\n• \n\n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n,\nℋ\n)\n is FPT for any recursively enumerable class of graphs \nℋ\n. Further, there is an algorithm that computes \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n2\n)\n for some computable function \n𝑓\n.\n\nProof 5.12.\n• \n\nObserve that the vertex-cover size of the \n𝑘\n-vertex path \n𝑃\n𝑘\n is equal to \n⌈\n𝑘\n/\n2\n⌉\n. Let \n𝒫\n=\n{\n𝑃\n𝑘\n:\n𝑘\n∈\nNature\n}\n be the set of all paths. Then, \n#\nSub\n​\n(\n𝒫\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard due to [CM14, Theorem 1.1]. Further, for each \n𝑘\n∈\nNature\n, we use \nΦ\n𝑘\n:\n𝒢\n𝑘\n→\nℚ\n to denote the restriction of \nΦ\n on \n𝑘\n-vertex graphs, thus \nΦ\n𝑘\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝑃\n𝑘\n→\n𝐺\n)\n. We now apply Theorem 5.3 to each \nΦ\n𝑘\n and obtain\n\n\t\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n𝑘\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝑃\n𝑘\n→\n𝐺\n)\nfor all \n𝐺\n∈\n𝒢\n.\n\t\n\nThis implies that \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is also #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard.\n\n• \n\nFor each \n𝐻\n∈\nℋ\n and graph \n𝐺\n, we use Lemma 3.1 and obtain\n\n\t\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n∑\n𝐴\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐴\n⋅\nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n⁡\n{\n𝐴\n}\n)\n⋅\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n⁡\n{\n𝐴\n}\n→\n𝐺\n)\n.\n\t\n\nNext, we use \nΦ\n𝑘\n​\n(\n𝐺\n)\n=\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝑃\n𝑘\n→\n𝐺\n)\n and obtain \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n​\n{\n𝐴\n}\n)\n≠\n0\n if and only if \n𝐻\n​\n{\n𝐴\n}\n≅\n𝑃\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n due to Theorem 5.1. This means that\n\n\t\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n=\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝑃\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n→\n𝐻\n)\n⋅\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝑃\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n→\n𝐺\n)\n.\n\t\n\nThus, in order to compute \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n, we first compute #\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝑃\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n→\n𝐻\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n​\n(\n𝑓\n1\n​\n(\n|\n𝑉\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n)\n)\n for some computable function \n𝑓\n1\n. Afterward, we compute \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝑃\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n→\n𝐺\n)\n, which can be done in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑓\n2\n​\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n2\n)\n since the treewidth of \n𝑃\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n is equal to \n1\n (see [Rot19, Theorem 2.35]. Note that the DP algorithm has to be modified to work with \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n). Thus, we can compute \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n|\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n2\n)\n for some computable function \n𝑓\n.\n\nRemark 5.13.\n\nThe difference of \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n and \n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n,\nℋ\n)\n can be best understood by choosing \nℋ\n=\n{\n𝐾\n𝑘\n:\n𝑘\n∈\nNature\n}\n. Now, according to Lemma 3.1 and Theorem 5.3 we obtain\n\n\t#\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n\t\n=\n∑\n𝐻\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n⋅\nΦ\n^\n(\n𝐻\n)\n⋅\n#\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\nand\n\t\t\n(10)\n\n\t\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐾\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n\t\n=\n∑\n𝐻\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑘\n∗\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n⋅\n𝑘\n!\n#\n​\nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n⋅\nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n⋅\n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n.\n\t\t\n(11)\n\nThis means that #\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n can be understood as a sum of #\nSub\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n where a term appears whenever \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n≠\n0\n and \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐾\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n can be understood as a sum of \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n​\n(\n𝐻\n→\n𝐺\n)\n where a term appears whenever \nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n≠\n0\n. Now, the problem \n#\nSub\n​\n(\nℱ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard if and only if the class \nℱ\n has unbounded vertex cover size. However, \n#\ncp-Hom\n​\n(\nℱ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard if and only if the class \nℱ\n has unbounded treewidth. Note, that the treewidth is a lower bound for the vertex cover size and that there are classes \nℱ\n of graphs with unbounded vertex cover size but bounded treewidth. This explains why \n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n,\nℋ\n)\n could be FPT in cases where \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard. Still, there is a parameterized Turing reduction from \n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n,\nℋ\n)\n to \n#\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n (see [DRSW22, Lemma 10]).\n\nNevertheless, it is often much simpler to use \n#\ncp-IndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n,\nℋ\n)\n since we can apply complexity monotonicity on the \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n-terms (see [DRSW22, Lemma 7]). Hence, the hardness of \n#\n​\ncp\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝐾\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n is governed by the term with highest treewidth that appears in the sum of Equation 11. However, it might be that nontrivial cancellations occur in the sum of Equation 10, which is why this sum is much harder to analyze. See [CDM17, Example 1.12] for an example of nontrivial cancelations.\n\n6Hardness Results for Modular Counting\n\nIn this section, we show that our hardness results from Sections 3 and 4 stay true when considering modulo counting. To that end, we introduce the following two classes of problems.\n\nDefinition 6.1.\n\nFor each prime number \n𝑝\n, and parameterized counting problem \n(\n#\n​\n𝐴\n,\n𝜅\n)\n, we define the following two problems:\n\n• \n\nThe parameterized decision problem \n(\nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝐴\n,\n𝜅\n)\n decides on input \n𝑥\n if \n#\n​\n𝐴\n​\n(\n𝑥\n)\n is divisible by \n𝑥\n.\n\n• \n\nThe parameterized problem \n(\n#\n​\n𝐴\n𝑝\n,\n𝜅\n)\n computes on input \n𝑥\n the values \n#\n​\n𝐴\n​\n(\n𝑥\n)\nmod\n𝑝\n.\n\nThe definition of \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝐴\n was introduced by Beigel, Gill, and Hertramp in [BGH90] and is a generalization of \nMOD\n2\n​\n𝐴\n=\n⊕\n𝐴\n which was already studied by Papadimitriou and Zachos back in the 1980s (see [PZ82]). However, Faben [Fab08] realized that the definition of \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝐴\n does not completely capture the complexity of modulo counting which is why he introduced the definition \n#\n​\n𝐴\n𝑝\n.\n\nIt is easy to see that an FPT algorithm for \n(\n#\n​\n𝐴\n𝑝\n,\n𝜅\n)\n yields an FPT algorithm for \n(\nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝐴\n,\n𝜅\n)\n. However, already in [Fab08, p. 3] Faben mentioned that one can design artificial problems for which \n#\n​\n𝐴\n𝑝\n is substantially harder than \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝐴\n, even though he was not able to find natural examples for this phenomenon. Observe that for \n𝑝\n=\n2\n, the problems \n(\n#\n​\n𝐴\n2\n,\n𝜅\n)\n and \n(\nMOD\n2\n​\n𝐴\n,\n𝜅\n)\n are equivalent. A detailed discussion of the differences between the complexity classes \n#\n​\n𝑃\n𝑝\n and \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝑃\n can be found in Faben’s PhD thesis [Fab12, Section 3.1.2].\n\nDue to the differences between \n#\n​\n𝐴\n𝑝\n and \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝐴\n, we introduce two different complexity classes for which we rely on the #Clique problem.\n\nDefinition 6.2.\n\nFor each prime number \n𝑝\n, we define the following classes of problems.\n\n• \n\nThe class of \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard problems consists of all problems \n(\nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝐴\n,\n𝜅\n)\n that have a parameterized Turing reduction from \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nClique\n to \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝑃\n.\n\n• \n\nThe class of \n#\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard problems consists of all problems \n(\n#\n​\n𝐴\n𝑝\n,\n𝜅\n)\n that have a parameterized Turing reduction from \n#\n​\nClique\n𝑝\n to \n#\n​\n𝑃\n𝑝\n.\n\nIn Lemma B.7, we show that the problem \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nClique\n and \n#\n​\nClique\n𝑝\n are equivant to each other under parameterized Turing reduction. However, this does not imply that the class of \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard problems is equivalent to the class of \n#\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard problems. Further, assuming rETH, we show in Appendix B that there is a constant \n𝛿\n, such that for all prime numbers \n𝑝\n, no algorithm decides \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nClique\n on input \n(\n𝐺\n,\n𝑘\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛼\n​\n𝑘\n)\n.\n\n{restatable*}\n\ncorollarycorcliquemodeth Assuming rETH, there is a constant \n𝛾\n>\n0\n such that for all prime numbers \n𝑝\n, no algorithm (that reads the whole input) solves \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nClique\n on input \n(\n𝐺\n,\n𝑘\n)\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛼\n​\n𝑘\n)\n.\n\nTo show the hardness of \n#\n​\nIndSub\n𝑝\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n, we use the following corollary which is a variation of Section 1.2.\n\nCorollary 6.3.\n\nLet \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\n{\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝑐\n}\n be a graph parameter, \n0\n<\n𝜓\n≤\n1\n and \n𝑝\n be a prime number. Assume that there set of graphs \n𝐴\n≔\n{\n𝐺\n𝑘\n}\n and a function \nℎ\n:\n𝐴\n→\nNature\n such that each \n𝐺\n𝑘\n contains \n𝐾\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑘\n)\n,\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑘\n)\n as a subgraph and \nΦ\n^\n(\n𝐺\n𝑘\n)\n≢\n𝑝\n0\n.\n\n• \n\nIf \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n∈\n𝜔\n⁡\n(\n1\n)\n, then the problem \n#\n​\nIndSub\n𝑝\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n (and \n#\n​\ncp-IndSub\n𝑝\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n) is \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard and \n#\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard.\n\n• \n\nIf \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n∈\nΩ\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝜓\n)\n, then there is a global constant \n𝛾\n>\n0\n such that for each fixed \n𝑘\n=\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑗\n)\n|\n, there is no algorithm (that reads the whole input) that for every \n𝐺\n computes the number \n#\n​\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n𝑝\n (or \n#\n​\ncp\n𝑝\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n) in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛾\n​\n𝑘\n)\n unless rETH fails. Here, the constant \n𝛾\n depends on \nℎ\n but is independent of \nΦ\n and \n𝑘\n.\n\n• \n\nIf \nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n∈\nΩ\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝜓\n)\n, then there is no algorithm (that reads the whole input) that for every \n𝐺\n computes \n#\n​\nIndSub\n⁡\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n𝑝\n (or \n#\n​\ncp\n𝑝\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n→\n𝐺\n)\n) in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n⋅\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝑜\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n)\n for all computable functions \n𝑓\n unless rETH fails.\n\nProof 6.4.\n\nWe show that an algorithm for \n#\n​\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n𝑝\n (or \n#\ncp\n𝑝\n-\nIndSub\n(\n(\nΦ\n,\n𝒢\n𝑘\n)\n→\n⋆\n)\n) can be used to compute \n#\n​\nClique\n𝑝\n. This is enough since \n#\n​\nClique\n𝑝\n and \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nClique\n are equivalent.\n\nWe emulate the proof Section 1.2. For this, observe that the chain of reductions (4) still holds when taking all computations modulo \n𝑝\n. This was already shown in [DRSW22, Appendix A]. In particular, we observe that we can still write \n#\n​\ncp\n𝑝\n​\n-\n​\nIndSub\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n as a linear combination of \n#\n​\ncp\n𝑝\n​\n-\n​\nHom\n-terms over the finite field \n𝔽\n𝑝\n (see Lemma 3.1). This means that our reduction is possible if we can find graphs with large bicliques whose alternating enumerator is nonvanishing mod \n𝑝\n. This is exactly the case when there is a graph \n𝐺\n𝑘\n that contains \n𝐾\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑘\n)\n,\nℎ\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑘\n)\n as a subgraph and \nΦ\n^\n(\n𝐺\n𝑘\n)\n≢\n𝑝\n0\n. We obtain the tight bounds under rETH by replacing Section 3 with Section 6 in the proof of Theorem 3.5.\n\n\\thmedgemonMOD\nProof 6.5.\n\nThe proof of this theorem works exactly like the proof of Section 1 with the only difference that we replace Section 1.2 with Section 6 and we observe that the reduction from Section 1.2 still works for modulo counting. Hereby it is important to point out that in the proof of Section 1, we picked a prime \n𝑝\n>\n𝑐\n and only considered prime powers of the form \n𝑞\n⁡\n(\n𝑘\n)\n≔\n𝑝\n𝑡\n. This allows us to again use Theorem 4.1 to get our hardness results for the prime power case. Note, that Theorem 4.1 ensures that we can find graphs that contain large bicliques and whose alternating enumerator is nonvanishing modulo \n𝑝\n (see definition of concentrated); or we can find a reduction to \nΦ\nSc\n,\n𝑞\n which is nontrivial on prime powers of the form \n𝑝\n𝑡\n.\n\nLastly, observe that each graph property \nΦ\n can be viewed as a graph parameter with codomain \n{\n0\n,\n1\n}\n. Thus, we can apply Section 1 to obtain that \n#\n​\nIndSub\n𝑝\n​\n(\nΦ\n)\n is \n#\n𝑝\n​\n𝖶\n​\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hard for all prime numbers \n𝑝\n.\n\nSee 1\n\nReferences\n[Aig07]\nMartin Aigner.\nA Course in Enumeration.\nSpringer Berlin, 1. edition, 2007.\ndoi:10.1007/978-3-540-39035-0.\n[BGH90]\nRichard Beigel, John Gill, and Ulrich Hertrampf.\nCounting classes: Thresholds, parity, mods, and fewness.\nIn Christian Choffrut and Thomas Lengauer, editors, STACS 90, pages 49–57, Berlin, Heidelberg, 1990.
