Valiant.\nThe complexity of computing the permanent.\nTheoretical Computer Science, 8(2):189–201, 1979.\ndoi:10.1016/0304-3975(79)90044-6.\nAppendix AThe Fixed Points of Automorphism Groups\n\nThe following explanations, definitions, and statements are taken from [DMW23]. However, the idea of using fixed points to analyze the alternating enumerator was originally developed in [DRSW22].\n\nA.1The Fixed Points of Graph Automorphisms\n\nFor each graph \n𝐺\n, the set of automorphisms \nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n forms a group with composition as the group operation. Observe that the automorphism group of a clique \nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑛\n)\n is just the symmetric group \n𝔖\n𝑛\n.\n\nLet \n𝐺\n denote a graph and consider a subgroup \nΓ\n⊆\nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n. Any \n𝑔\n∈\n𝐺\n is a bijection on \n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n, which we may interpret as \n𝑔\n permuting the vertices of \n𝐺\n. We may also interpret \n𝑔\n as an operation on the edges of \n𝐺\n. However, as we wish to use results from the literature (such as the Orbit-stabilizer Theorem), we need to describe this operation on the edges of \n𝐺\n using the language of group actions.\n\nFormally, we can turn the operation on vertices into a group action \n⋅\n:\nΓ\n×\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n→\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n that tells us how each member of \nΓ\n moves the edges of \n𝐺\n. Specifically, we define \n𝑔\n⋅\n{\n𝑢\n,\n𝑣\n}\n≔\n{\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑢\n)\n,\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑣\n)\n}\n. We interpret said group action \n⋅\n multiplicatively and typically write just \n𝑔\n​\n{\n𝑢\n,\n𝑣\n}\n.\n\nExtending the previous group action, \nΓ\n also acts on edge-subgraphs of \n𝐺\n via\n\n\t\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝑔\n⋅\n𝐴\n)\n\t\n≔\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n,\nand\n\t\n\t\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝑔\n⋅\n𝐴\n)\n\t\n≔\n{\n{\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑢\n)\n,\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑣\n)\n}\n:\n{\n𝑢\n,\n𝑣\n}\n∈\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n}\n,\n\t\n\nwhere \n𝑔\n∈\nΓ\n and \n𝐴\n is an edge-subgraphs of \n𝐺\n. Again, we interpret said group actions \n⋅\n multiplicatively and typically write just \n𝑔\n​\n𝐴\n.\n\nFor each \n{\n𝑢\n,\n𝑣\n}\n∈\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n the set \nΓ\n⋅\n{\n𝑢\n,\n𝑣\n}\n≔\n{\n𝑔\n⋅\n{\n𝑢\n,\n𝑣\n}\n:\n𝑔\n∈\nΓ\n}\n is the orbit of \n{\n𝑢\n,\n𝑣\n}\n. Two different edges either have disjoint orbits or equal orbits. We write \n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n/\nΓ\n to denote the set of all orbits of \n⋅\n. Recall that \n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n/\nΓ\n forms a partition of \n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n. In a slight abuse of notation, for an orbit \n𝑂\n, we also write \n𝑂\n for the edge-subgraph \n𝐺\n​\n{\n𝑂\n}\n; similarly, we use \n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n/\nΓ\n to denote the set of all such edge-subgraphs.\n\nWe say that an edge-subgraph \n𝐴\n is a fixed point of \nΓ\n in \n𝐺\n if \n𝑔\n​\n𝐴\n=\n𝐴\n for all \n𝑔\n∈\nΓ\n. We write \nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐺\n)\n for the set of all fixed points of \nΓ\n in \n𝐺\n. As it turns out, a graph \n𝐴\n being a fixed point of \nΓ\n in \n𝐺\n is a strong property that is very useful to us. For now and as a first useful observation, we see that \n𝐻\n inherits key properties from \n𝐺\n.\n\n{lemmaq}\n\n[See [DMW23, Lemma 3.1]] Let \n𝐺\n denote a graph and let \nΓ\n⊆\nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n denote a group. For any \n𝐻\n∈\nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐺\n)\n all of the following hold.\n\n(1)\n\nWe have \nΓ\n⊆\nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n.\n\n(2)\n\nAny fixed point of \nΓ\n in \n𝐻\n is a fixed point of \nΓ\n in \n𝐺\n and \nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n=\n{\n𝐴\n∈\nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐺\n)\n:\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n⊆\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n}\n.\n\nIntuitively, Section A.1 implies that fixed points of fixed points are again fixed points, which allows us to define a structure on the set of fixed points. Now, the following lemma shows that this structure of fixed points is made up of orbits, meaning that orbits are the basic building blocks of \nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n.\n\n{lemmaq}\n\n[See [DMW23, Lemma 4.1]] Let \n𝐻\n denote a graph and let \nΓ\n⊆\nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n denote a group. Further, let \nΓ\n act on \n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n and write \n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n/\nΓ\n for the set of all resulting orbits. Finally, let \nΓ\n act on edge-subgraphs of \n𝐻\n and write \nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n for the set of all resulting fixed points of \nΓ\n in \n𝐻\n.\n\nThen, the edge set of each fixed point \n𝐹\n∈\nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n is the (possible empty) disjoint union of orbits \n𝑂\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑂\n𝑠\n, where \n𝑂\n𝑖\n∈\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n/\nΓ\n and each disjoint union of orbits yields a fixed point. The partition into orbits is unique.\n\nSection A.1 induce an order of the fixed points in \nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n: we say a fixed point \n𝐹\n is a sub-point of another fixed point \n𝐺\n if we can obtain \n𝐺\n from the union of \n𝐹\n and (potentially multiple) orbits in \n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n/\nΓ\n. This allows us to define fixed points as the union of orbits.\n\nDefinition A.1 (See [DMW23, Definition 4.5]).\n\nLet \n𝐻\n denote a graph and let \nΓ\n⊆\nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n denote a group. Further, let \nΓ\n act on \n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n and write \n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n/\nΓ\n for the set of all resulting orbits. Finally, let \nΓ\n act on edge-subgraphs of \n𝐻\n and write \nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n for the set of all resulting fixed points of \nΓ\n in \n𝐻\n.\n\nFor a fixed point \n𝐹\n∈\nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n, its orbit factorization \n𝕆\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n)\n is the unique subset of \n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n/\nΓ\n whose union is \n𝐹\n:\n\n\t\n𝐹\n=\n⋃\n𝕆\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n)\n.\n\t\n\nThe level of \n𝐹\n, denoted by \nℓ\n⁡\n(\nF\n)\n, is the size of the orbit factorization of \n𝐹\n\n\t\nℓ\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n)\n≔\n|\n𝕆\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n)\n|\n.\n\t\n\nFinally, for two fixed points \n𝐹\n1\n,\n𝐹\n2\n∈\nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n, we say that \n𝐹\n2\n is a sub-point of \n𝐹\n1\n, denoted by \n𝐹\n2\n⊆\n𝐹\n1\n, if the orbit factorization of \n𝐹\n2\n is a subset of the orbit factorization of \n𝐹\n1\n. If the inclusion is strict, we say that \n𝐹\n2\n is a proper sub-point of \n𝐹\n1\n.\n\nThe next lemma makes it possible to group the sub-points of a fixed point according to their level. It can be proven by observing that each orbit divides the group order \n|\nΓ\n|\n, which is a \n𝑝\n power, due to the orbit-stabilizer-theorem.\n\n{lemmaq}\n\n[See [DMW23, Lemma 4.6]] Let \n𝐻\n denote a graph and let \nΓ\n⊆\nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n denote a \n𝑝\n-group.\n\nFor any fixed point \n𝐹\n∈\nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n, we have\n\n\t\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐹\n)\n≡\n𝑝\n(\n−\n1\n)\nℓ\n⁡\n(\n𝐹\n)\n.\n\t\n\nFinally, we are at the point where we can use fixed points to compute the alternating enumerator modulo a prime number. For this, we use the following lemma which was originally proven in [DRSW22].\n\nLemma A.2 ([DRSW22]).\n\nLet \n𝐻\n denote a graph, let \nΓ\n⊆\nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐻\n)\n denote a \n𝑝\n-group, and let \nΦ\n:\n𝒢\n→\nℤ\n denote a graph parameter, then\n\n\t\nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐻\n)\n≡\n∑\n𝐴\n∈\nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐻\n)\n𝑝\n⁡\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n​\n(\n−\n1\n)\n#\n​\n𝐸\n​\n(\n𝐴\n)\n≡\n∑\n𝐴\n⊆\n𝐻\n𝑝\n⁡\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n​\n(\n−\n1\n)\nℓ\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n.\n\t\t\nProof A.3.\n\nThe proof originates from the proof of [DRSW22, Lemma 1]. The main idea is that we can group together edge-subgraphs of \n𝐻\n that are in the same orbit of the group action \n⋅\n that takes as input a group element \n𝑔\n∈\nΓ\n and an edge-subgraph \n𝐴\n of \n𝐻\n, and outputs \n𝑔\n⋅\n𝐴\n. Observe that graphs from the same orbit are always isomorphic to each other, thus \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n=\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝑔\n⋅\n𝐴\n)\n, which allows us to group them together in the sum. Due to the orbit-stabilizer-theorem, the size of each orbit divides the group order of \nΓ\n, which is a \n𝑝\n power. Thus, if we compute the alternating enumerate mod \n𝑝\n, then only the orbits of size \n1\n (that is, fixed points) remain.\n\nWe mainly use Lemma A.2 to show that the alternating enumerator is non-vanishing in some special cases.\n\n\\lemcompae\nProof A.4.\n\nWe show that \nΦ\n^\n(\n𝐴\n)\n≡\n𝑝\n(\n−\n1\n)\nℓ\n⁡\n(\nA\n)\n+\n1\n(\n𝑏\n−\n𝑎\n)\n which is sufficient since \n𝑏\n−\n𝑎\n≢\n𝑝\n0\n. By definition, the fixed point \n𝐴\n has exactly \n(\nℓ\n⁡\n(\nA\n)\n𝑖\n)\n sub-points that have a level of exactly \n𝑖\n. Starting from Lemma A.2, we rewrite the alternating enumerator and obtain\n\n\t\nΦ\n^\n​\n(\n𝐴\n)\n\t\n≡\n∑\n𝐵\n⊆\n𝐴\n𝑝\n⁡\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐵\n)\n​\n(\n−\n1\n)\nℓ\n⁡\n(\n𝐵\n)\n\t\n\t\t\n≡\n𝑝\n−\n(\n−\n1\n)\nℓ\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n(\n𝑏\n−\n𝑎\n)\n+\n∑\n𝐵\n⊆\n𝐴\n𝑏\n(\n−\n1\n)\nℓ\n⁡\n(\n𝐵\n)\n\t(\nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n=\n𝑎\n and \nΦ\n⁡\n(\n𝐵\n)\n=\n𝑏\n for all \n𝐵\n⊊\n𝐴\n)\t\n\t\t\n≡\n𝑝\n−\n(\n−\n1\n)\nℓ\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n(\n𝑏\n−\n𝑎\n)\n+\n𝑏\n∑\n𝑖\n=\n0\nℓ\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n(\nℓ\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n𝑖\n)\n(\n−\n1\n)\n𝑖\n\t(group by level)\t\n\t\t\n≡\n𝑝\n−\n(\n−\n1\n)\nℓ\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n(\n𝑏\n−\n𝑎\n)\n+\n𝑏\n(\n−\n1\n+\n1\n)\nℓ\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n\t(Binomial Theorem)\t\n\t\t\n≡\n𝑝\n(\n−\n1\n)\nℓ\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n+\n1\n(\n𝑏\n−\n𝑎\n)\n.\n\t\n\nIn total, this yields the claim.\n\nA.2The \n𝑝\n-Sylow groups of \nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑚\n)\n and its Fixed Point Structure\n\nWe intend to construct a special subgroup of \nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑚\n)\n that we call the \n𝑝\n-Sylow groups of \nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑚\n)\n. To make things simpler, we identify the vertex set of \n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑚\n with \n[\n0\n.\n.\n𝑝\n)\n𝑚\n. We start by defining special types of bijections on \n[\n0\n.\n.\n𝑝\n)\n𝑚\n.\n\nDefinition A.5 (See [DMW23, Definition 7.1]).\n\nConsider a prime \n𝑝\n and a positive integer \n𝑚\n. For each \n𝑗\n∈\n[\n0\n.\n.\n𝑚\n)\n, write \n𝜑\n𝑖\n for a function \n[\n0\n.\n.\n𝑝\n)\n𝑗\n→\n[\n0\n.\n.\n𝑝\n)\n and set \n𝜑\n≔\n(\n𝜑\n0\n,\n…\n,\n𝜑\n𝑚\n−\n1\n)\n. We define the function \n𝜑\n¯\n:\n[\n0\n.\n.\np\n)\nm\n→\n[\n0\n.\n.\np\n)\nm\n via4\n4\nWe write \n𝜑\n0\n for \n𝜑\n0\n​\n(\n(\n)\n)\n since \n𝜑\n0\n is a function that is defined on a single element\n\n\t\n𝜑\n¯\n⁡\n(\n𝑥\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑥\n𝑚\n)\n≔\n(\n𝑥\n1\n+\n𝜑\n0\n,\n𝑥\n2\n+\n𝜑\n1\n​\n(\n𝑥\n1\n)\n,\n𝑥\n3\n+\n𝜑\n2\n​\n(\n𝑥\n1\n,\n𝑥\n2\n)\n,\n…\n,\n𝑥\n𝑚\n+\n𝜑\n𝑚\n−\n1\n​\n(\n𝑥\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑥\n𝑚\n−\n1\n)\n)\n,\n\t\n\nwhere all computations are done modulo \n𝑝\n.\n\nWe write \n𝑝\n¯\n𝑚\n for the set of all functions \n𝜑\n¯\n:\n[\n0\n.\n.\np\n)\nm\n→\n[\n0\n.\n.\np\n)\nm\n that are obtained in this fashion, that is,\n\n\t\n𝑝\n¯\n𝑚\n≔\n{\n𝜑\n¯\n:\n𝜑\n𝑖\n∈\n[\n0\n.\n.\n𝑝\n)\n𝑗\n→\n[\n0\n.\n.\n𝑝\n)\n}\n.\n\t\n\nNow, it is easy to check that, together with composition \n∘\n as a group operation, these specific bijections form a transitive \n𝑝\n-group.\n\n{lemmaq}\n\nThe pair \nSyl\n𝑝\n𝑚\n≔\n(\n𝑝\n¯\n𝑚\n,\n∘\n)\n is a transitive \n𝑝\n-group on the set \n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑚\n)\n.\n\nWe see that the fixed point structure of \nFP\n⁡\n(\nSyl\n𝑝\n𝑚\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑚\n)\n has some very nice properties (e.g. containing large bicliques). However, we need the lexicographic product of graphs in order to describe the fixed points of \nSyl\n𝑝\n𝑚\n in \n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑚\n.\n\nDefinition A.6 (See [DMW23, Definition 7.8]).\n\nFor graphs \n𝐺\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐺\n𝑚\n, we define their lexicographic product \n𝐺\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐺\n𝑚\n via\n\n\t\n𝑉\n(\n𝐺\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐺\n𝑚\n)\n\t\n≔\n𝑉\n(\n𝐺\n1\n)\n×\n⋯\n×\n𝑉\n(\n𝐺\n𝑚\n)\nand\n\t\n\t\n𝐸\n(\n𝐺\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐺\n𝑚\n)\n\t\n≔\n{\n{\n(\n𝑢\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑢\n𝑚\n)\n,\n(\n𝑣\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑣\n𝑚\n)\n}\n\t\n\t\t\n:\nthere is an \n𝑖\n∈\n[\n𝑚\n]\n with \n𝑢\n𝑗\n=\n𝑣\n𝑗\n for all \n𝑗\n<\n𝑖\n and \n{\n𝑢\n𝑖\n,\n𝑣\n𝑖\n}\n∈\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑖\n)\n}\n.\n\t\t\n\nAs an easy example, observe that we have \n𝐺\n1\n∘\n𝐺\n2\n≅\n𝐺\n1\n​\n⟨\n𝐺\n2\n,\n…\n,\n𝐺\n2\n⟩\n and \n𝐺\n1\n∘\n𝐺\n2\n∘\n𝐺\n3\n≅\n𝐺\n1\n​\n⟨\n𝐺\n2\n∘\n𝐺\n3\n,\n…\n,\n𝐺\n2\n∘\n𝐺\n3\n⟩\n. Now, it turns out that the fixed points of \nFP\n⁡\n(\nSyl\n𝑝\n𝑚\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑚\n)\n are the lexicographic product of so-called difference graphs.\n\nDefinition A.7 (See [DMW23, Definition 2.4]).\n\nFor a prime \n𝑝\n, an integer \n𝑚\n>\n0\n, and a set \n𝐴\n⊆\n𝔽\n𝑝\n𝑚\n+\n, we define the difference graph \n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝑚\n𝐴\n via\n\n\t\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝑚\n𝐴\n)\n\t\n≔\n𝔽\n𝑝\n𝑚\nand\n𝐸\n(\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝑚\n𝐴\n)\n≔\n{\n{\n𝑢\n,\n𝑣\n}\n:\n𝑢\n,\n𝑣\n∈\n𝔽\n𝑝\n𝑚\n,\n(\n𝑢\n−\n𝑣\n)\n∈\n𝐴\n∪\n(\n−\n𝐴\n)\n}\n,\n\t\n\nwhere \n−\n𝐴\n=\n{\n−\n𝑥\n:\n𝑥\n∈\n𝐴\n}\n. Observe that \n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝑚\n𝐴\n=\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑚\n whenever \n𝐴\n=\n𝔽\n𝑝\n𝑚\n+\n.\n\nThe level of a difference graph \n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝑚\n𝐴\n is the cardinality of \n𝐴\n; we write \nℓ\n⁡\n(\nC\np\nm\nA\n)\n for the level of \n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝑚\n𝐴\n.\n\nNote that for all odd prime numbers \n𝑝\n the set \n𝔽\n𝑝\n+\n is equal to \n{\n1\n,\n…\n,\n(\n𝑝\n−\n1\n)\n/\n2\n}\n and that \n𝔽\n2\n+\n is equal to \n{\n1\n}\n.\n\n{lemmaq}\n\n[See [DMW23, Lemma 7.11]] For any prime \n𝑝\n and any positive integer \n𝑚\n, we have\n\n\t\nFP\n(\nSyl\n𝑝\n𝑚\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑚\n)\n=\n{\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n𝑚\n:\n𝐴\n𝑖\n⊆\n𝔽\n𝑝\n+\n}\n.\n\t\t\n\nAdditionally, it is useful to know that the level of a fixed point \n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n𝑚\n in \nFP\n⁡\n(\nSyl\n𝑝\n𝑚\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑚\n)\n (that is, the number of orbits) is simply \n∑\n𝑖\n=\n1\n𝑚\n|\n𝐴\n𝑖\n|\n.\n\n{lemmaq}\n\n[See [DMW23, Lemma 7.12]] For any prime \n𝑝\n and any positive integer \n𝑚\n, the level of \n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n𝑚\n∈\nFP\n⁡\n(\nSyl\n𝑝\n𝑚\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑚\n)\n is\n\n\t\nℓ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n𝑚\n)\n=\n∑\n𝑖\n=\n1\n𝑚\n|\n𝐴\n𝑖\n|\n.\n\t\nA.3Fixed Points of Sylow Groups Contain Large Bicliques\n\nThe goal of this section is to understand the fixed points structure of \n𝑝\n-Sylow groups. We start by defining the empty-prefix of a fixed point in \nFP\n⁡\n(\nSyl\n𝑝\n𝑚\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑚\n)\n.\n\nDefinition A.8 (See [DMW23, Definition 7.14]).\n\nWrite \n𝐻\n≔\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n𝑚\n for a fixed point of \nFP\n⁡\n(\nSyl\n𝑝\n𝑚\n,\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑚\n)\n. The empty-prefix of \n𝐻\n is the smallest index \n𝑖\n with \n𝐴\n𝑖\n≠\n∅\n, minus one; we write \n𝜀\n⁡\n(\nA\n1\n,\n…\n,\nA\nm\n)\n≔\ni\n−\n1\n.\n\nOur first observation is that each fixed point with a low empty-prefix contains a large biclique as an edge-subgraph. This allows us to use Section 1.2 which proves #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hardness and tight bounds. The proof itself is a simple computation.\n\n{lemmaq}\n\n[See [DMW23, Lemma 7.15]] Let \n𝑝\n denote a prime number and let \n𝑚\n denote a positive integer. For each \n𝑖\n∈\n[\n𝑚\n]\n, let \n𝐴\n𝑖\n⊆\n𝔽\n𝑝\n+\n denote a subset and set \n𝐴\n≔\n(\n𝐴\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐴\n𝑚\n)\n. Then, \n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n𝑚\n contains \n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑚\n−\n1\n−\n𝜀\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n,\n𝑝\n𝑚\n−\n1\n−\n𝜀\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n)\n as a subgraph.\n\nOur second observation is that fixed points with a high empty-prefix are always isomorphic to edge-subgraphs of fixed points with a low empty-prefix. This allows us to consider only fixed points with low empty-prefix (see Theorem 4.1).\n\nLemma A.9 (See [DMW23, Lemma 7.17]).\n\nLet \n𝑝\n denote a prime number and let \n𝑚\n denote a positive integer. For each \n𝑖\n∈\n[\n𝑚\n]\n, let \n𝐴\n𝑖\n⊆\n𝔽\n𝑝\n+\n denote a subset. Then, for all \n𝑗\n∈\n[\n𝑚\n]\n, the graph \n𝐶\n𝑝\n∅\n∘\n⋯\n𝐶\n𝑝\n∅\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n𝑚\n−\n𝑗\n is isomorphic to an edge-subgraph of \n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n𝑚\n.\n\nProof A.10.\n\nThe intuition behind this proof is that we can use the following bijection\n\n\t\n𝜑\n→\n𝑝\n𝑚\n:\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑚\n)\n→\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑝\n𝑚\n)\n;\n(\n𝑎\n1\n​\n…\n,\n𝑎\n𝑚\n)\n↦\n(\n𝑎\n𝑚\n,\n𝑎\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑎\n𝑚\n−\n1\n)\n\t\n\nto show that \n𝜑\n→\n𝑝\n𝑚\n(\n𝐶\n𝑝\n∅\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n𝑚\n−\n1\n)\n is an edge-subgraph of \n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n1\n∘\n⋯\n∘\n𝐶\n𝑝\n𝐴\n𝑚\n. Now, the iteration of this result yields the claim.\n\nA.4Product Groups, Graphs Unions, and Graph Joins\n\nIn the last two sections, we consider fixed points of \n𝐾\n𝑚\n whenever \n𝑚\n is a \n𝑝\n power. However, if \n𝑚\n is not a \n𝑝\n-power then we can use the product of multiple \n𝑝\n-groups to get a \n𝑝\n-subgroup of \nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐾\n𝑚\n)\n. For (not necessarily disjoint) sets \n𝑋\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑋\n𝑚\n, the disjoint union \n𝑋\n1\n⊎\n⋯\n⊎\n𝑋\n𝑚\n is the set \n{\n(\n𝑖\n,\n𝑥\n)\n:\n𝑖\n∈\n[\n𝑚\n]\n,\n𝑥\n∈\n𝑋\n𝑖\n}\n.\n\nDefinition A.11 (See [DMW23, Definition 6.3]).\n\nFor permutation groups \nΓ\n1\n=\n(\n𝐺\n1\n,\n∘\n)\n,\n…\n,\nΓ\n𝑚\n=\n(\n𝐺\n𝑚\n,\n∘\n)\n with \nΓ\n𝑖\n⊆\n𝔖\n𝑋\n𝑖\n, their product group \nΓ\n≔\nΓ\n1\n×\n⋯\n×\nΓ\n𝑚\n is the set \n𝐺\n≔\n𝐺\n1\n×\n⋯\n×\n𝐺\n𝑚\n together with the component-wise function composition, that is, for \n(\n𝑔\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑔\n𝑚\n)\n,\n(\n𝑔\n1\n′\n,\n…\n,\n𝑔\n𝑚\n′\n)\n∈\nΓ\n, we set\n\n\t\n(\n𝑔\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑔\n𝑚\n)\n∘\n(\n𝑔\n1\n′\n,\n…\n,\n𝑔\n𝑚\n′\n)\n≔\n(\n𝑔\n1\n∘\n𝑔\n1\n′\n,\n…\n,\n𝑔\n𝑚\n∘\n𝑔\n𝑚\n′\n)\n.\n\t\n\nWe let \nΓ\n act on \n𝑋\n≔\n𝑋\n1\n⊎\n⋯\n⊎\n𝑋\n𝑚\n via\n\n\t\n𝑔\n⁡\n(\n𝑗\n,\n𝑥\n𝑗\n)\n≔\n(\n𝑗\n,\n𝑔\n𝑗\n​\n(\n𝑥\n𝑗\n)\n)\n,\n for all \n​\n𝑔\n=\n(\n𝑔\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑔\n𝑚\n)\n∈\n𝐺\n​\n and \n​\n𝑥\n=\n(\n𝑗\n,\n𝑥\n𝑗\n)\n∈\n𝑋\n.\n\t\n\nLet \nΓ\n1\n⊆\nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n1\n)\n,\n…\n,\nΓ\n𝑠\n⊆\nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑚\n)\n denote \n𝑝\n-groups. Now, it is easy to see that \nΓ\n≔\nΓ\n1\n×\n⋯\n×\nΓ\n𝑚\n is also a \n𝑝\n-group, and that it is a subgroup of \nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n for \n𝐺\n≔\n𝐺\n1\n×\n⋯\n×\n𝐺\n𝑚\n.\n\nNext, we try to understand the fixed points structure of \nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐺\n)\n. For this, we need some way to merge multiple graphs together.\n\n\\definhab\n\nNow, our observation is that the fixed points of \nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n,\n𝐺\n)\n are inhabited graphs. In particular, each fixed point \n𝐴\n≔\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐴\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐴\n𝑚\n⟩\n consists of \n𝑚\n blocks, where the \n𝑖\n-th block is a fixed point \n𝐴\n𝑖\n∈\nFP\n⁡\n(\nΓ\n𝑖\n,\n𝐺\n𝑖\n)\n and we fully connect two blocks \n𝑖\n and \n𝑗\n with each other if \n{\n𝑖\n,\n𝑗\n}\n∈\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n)\n.\n\n{corollaryq}\n\n[See [DMW23, Corollary 6.8]] For \n𝑖\n∈\n[\n𝑚\n]\n, let \n𝐺\n𝑖\n denote a graph and let \nΓ\n𝑖\n⊆\nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑖\n)\n denote a transitive permutation group. Then, we have\n\n\t\nFP\n(\n×\n𝑖\n=\n1\n𝑚\nΓ\n𝑖\n,\n▽\n𝑖\n=\n1\n𝑚\n𝐺\n𝑖\n)\n=\n{\n𝐶\n⟨\n𝐴\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐴\n𝑚\n⟩\n:\n𝐶\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑚\n,\n𝐴\n𝑖\n∈\nFP\n(\nΓ\n𝑖\n,\n𝐺\n𝑖\n)\n}\n.\n\t\t\n\nThe structure of these fixed points is very useful whenever we use large graphs. For example, consider a fixed point \n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐴\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐴\n𝑚\n⟩\n∈\nFP\n⁡\n(\n×\n𝑖\n=\n1\n𝑚\n⁡\nΓ\n𝑖\n,\n▽\n𝑖\n=\n1\n𝑚\n⁡\n𝐺\n𝑖\n)\n. Now, if \n{\n𝑖\n,\n𝑗\n}\n∈\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n)\n, then \n𝐾\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑖\n)\n|\n,\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑗\n)\n|\n is a subgraph of \n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐴\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐴\n𝑚\n⟩\n. This implies that for large graphs \n𝐺\n𝑖\n and \n𝐺\n𝑗\n, we can use Section 1.2 to show #\n𝖶\n⁡\n[\n𝟣\n]\n-hardness and tight bounds. Lastly, the following remark is quite useful.\n\nRemark A.12.\n\nFor \n𝑖\n∈\n[\n𝑚\n]\n, let \n𝐺\n𝑖\n denote a graph, let \nΓ\n𝑖\n⊆\nAut\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝑖\n)\n denote a transitive permutation group, and let \n𝐶\n∈\n𝒢\n𝑚\n be a graph. The sub-points of a fixed point \n𝐶\n​\n⟨\nIS\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n⟩\n are precisely the fixed points \n𝐶\n′\n​\n⟨\nIS\n,\n…\n,\nIS\n⟩\n, where \n𝐶\n′\n is an edge-subgraph of \n𝐶\n.\n\nUsing [DMW23, Lemma 6.5], we obtain that the level of the fixed point\n\n\t\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐴\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐴\n𝑚\n⟩\n∈\nFP\n⁡\n(\n×\n𝑖\n=\n1\n𝑚\n⁡\nΓ\n𝑖\n,\n▽\n𝑖\n=\n1\n𝑚\n⁡\n𝐺\n𝑖\n)\n\t\n\nis equal to\n\n\t\nℓ\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n⁡\n⟨\n𝐴\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐴\n𝑚\n⟩\n)\n=\n|\n𝐸\n⁡\n(\n𝐶\n)\n|\n+\n∑\n𝑖\n=\n1\n𝑚\nℓ\n⁡\n(\n𝐴\n𝑖\n)\n.\n\t\nAppendix BThe Parameterized Complexity of \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nClique\n\nFor our modular counting results from Section 6, we rely on the hardness \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nClique\n. However, to show that \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nClique\n is hard, we introduce the following two problems.\n\nDefinition B.1.\n\nWe define the following two decision problems\n\n• \n\nThe problem \nUnique\n-\n​\n𝑘\n​\n-\nSAT\n gets as input a \n𝑘\n​\n-\nSAT\n instance with the guarantee that this instance either has 0 or 1 solutions. The problem is to decide whether a satisfying assignment exists.\n\n• \n\nThe problem \nUnique\n-\n​\n𝑘\n​\n-\nClique\n gets as input a graph \n𝐺\n with the guarantee that this instance either has 0 or 1 many \n𝑘\n-cliques. The problem is to decide whether a \n𝑘\n-clique exists.\n\nTo show that there is a constant \n𝛼\n>\n0\n such that no algorithm solves \nUnique\n-\n​\n𝑘\n​\n-\nClique\n in times \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛼\n​\n𝑘\n)\n, we use a reduction from \nUnique\n-\n​\n3\n​\n-\nSAT\n. According to [CIKP08] there is a constant \n𝛾\n>\n0\n such that no algorithm solves \nUnique\n-\n​\n3\n​\n-\nSAT\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n2\n𝛾\n​\n𝑛\n)\n unless the randomized exponential time hypothesis fails. The randomized exponential time hypothesis (rETH) states that there is a constant \n𝛿\n3\n such that no randomized algorithm solves \n3\n​\n-\nSAT\n in expected time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n2\n𝛿\n3\n​\n𝑛\n)\n.\n\n{theoremq}\n\n[[CIKP08]] Assuming rETH, there is a constant \n𝛾\n>\n0\n such that no algorithm solves \nUnique\n-\n​\n3\n​\n-\nSAT\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n2\n𝛾\n​\n𝑛\n)\n, where \n𝑛\n is the number of variables.\n\nNext, we combine Appendix B with the sparsification lemma to obtain the following statement.\n\nCorollary B.2.\n\nAssuming rETH, there is a constant \n𝛿\n>\n0\n such that no algorithm solves \nUnique\n-\n​\n3\n​\n-\nSAT\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n2\n𝛿\n⁡\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n)\n, where \n𝑛\n is the number of variables and \n𝑚\n is the number of clauses.\n\nProof B.3.\n\nSuppose that there is a randomized algorithm that solves the \nUnique\n-\n​\n3\n​\n-\nSAT\n problem in expected time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n2\n𝛿\n⁡\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n)\n. Then, we can use the Sparsification Lemma (see [CFK+15, Theorem 14.4]) to get an algorithm that solves \nUnique\n-\n​\n3\n​\n-\nSAT\n in expected time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n2\n𝛿\n′\n​\n𝑛\n)\n for some \n𝛿\n′\n>\n0\n.\n\nUsing Corollary B.2, we can show tight bounds for \nUnique\n-\n​\n𝑘\n​\n-\nClique\n assuming rETH. The proof constructs a parsimonious reduction from \n3\n​\n-\nSAT\n to \n𝑘\n-Clique that is fast enough to keep the exponent.\n\nTheorem B.4.\n\nAssuming rETH, there is a constant \n𝛾\n>\n0\n such that, no algorithm (that reads the whole input) solves \nUnique\n-\n​\n𝑘\n​\n-\nClique\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛼\n​\n𝑘\n)\n.\n\nProof B.5.\n\nThe proof is a modification of the proof of Theorem 14.21 in [CFK+15] and [DMW23, Lemma B.2]. Originally, the proof works by first using a reduction from \n3\n​\n-\nSAT\n to \n3\n-Coloring and later a reduction from \n3\n-Coloring to \n𝑘\n-Clique. The problem with this approach is that this reduction is not parsimonious since each valid satisfying assignment generates multiple valid 3 colorings. We circumvent this problem by combining both reductions into a single one. This gives us more freedom in defining the colorings, which ensures that each satisfying assignment only yields a single valid coloring that later leads to exactly one \n𝑘\n-clique. This parsimonious reduction proves the claim.\n\nGive a \n3\n​\n-\nSAT\n formula \n𝜙\n with \n𝑛\n variables and \n𝑚\n clauses, we construct a graph \n𝐺\n𝜙\n using the standard reduction [GJS76, Theorem 2.1] from \n3\n​\n-\nSAT\n to \n3\n-Coloring.\n\nLet \n𝑥\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑥\n𝑛\n be the variables of \n𝜙\n and \n𝐶\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐶\n𝑚\n be the clauses of \n𝜙\n. The \n𝑖\n-th clause is equal to \n𝐶\n𝑖\n≔\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n1\n∨\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n2\n∨\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n3\n, where \nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n1\n,\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n2\n,\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n3\n are literals. The vertex set of \n𝐺\n𝜙\n is defined as\n\n\t\n𝑉\n(\n𝐺\n𝜙\n)\n=\n{\n𝑇\n,\n𝐹\n,\n𝐵\n}\n∪\n{\n𝑥\n𝑖\n,\n𝑥\n¯\n𝑖\n:\n𝑖\n∈\n[\n𝑛\n]\n}\n∪\n{\n𝑦\n𝑖\n,\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑦\n𝑖\n,\n6\n:\n𝑖\n∈\n[\n𝑚\n]\n}\n\t\n\nthis is, we introduce two vertices \n𝑥\n𝑖\n and \n𝑥\n¯\n𝑖\n per variable and six vertices per clause. We call the vertices \n𝑥\n𝑖\n and \n𝑥\n¯\n𝑖\n literal vertices since they represent the literals of \nΦ\n. Further, we introduce three auxiliary vertices \n𝑇\n,\n𝐹\n,\n𝐵\n.\n\nNext, we describe the edge set of \n𝐺\n𝜙\n. First, we connect the vertices \n𝑇\n,\n𝐹\n,\n𝐵\n with each other to form a triangle. Next, we connect \n𝑥\n𝑖\n with \n𝑥\n¯\n𝑖\n and connect both \n𝑥\n𝑖\n and \n𝑥\n¯\n𝑖\n with \n𝐵\n. Further, we connect each vertex \n𝑦\n𝑖\n,\n6\n to both \n𝐹\n and \n𝐵\n. Lastly, for each clause \n𝐶\n𝑖\n, we add the clause gadget from Figure 2(b) to the graph \n𝐺\nΦ\n.\n\n(a)The clause gadget of the clause \n𝐶\n𝑖\n(b)The graph \n𝐺\n𝜙\n for \n𝜙\n=\n(\n𝑥\n¯\n1\n∨\n𝑥\n2\n∨\n𝑥\n3\n)\n∧\n(\n𝑥\n¯\n4\n∨\n𝑥\n3\n∨\n𝑥\n¯\n2\n)\n.\nFigure 2:How to construct the graph \n𝐺\n𝜙\n.\n\nObserve, that the vertex set of each clause gadget \n𝐶\n𝑖\n contains three literal vertices and six additional vertices \n𝑦\n𝑖\n,\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑦\n𝑖\n,\n6\n. This means that a literal vertex \n𝑥\n𝑖\n or \n𝑥\n𝑖\n¯\n might occur in multiple clause gadgets.\n\nWe introduce three colors \n𝑇\n,\n𝐹\n,\n𝐵\n. Let \n𝑓\n:\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝜙\n)\n→\n{\n𝑇\n,\n𝐹\n,\n𝐵\n}\n be a coloring of \n𝐺\n𝜙\n. Assume that \n𝑓\n is a proper coloring with \n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑧\n)\n=\n𝑧\n, for all \n𝑧\n∈\n{\n𝑇\n,\n𝐹\n,\n𝐵\n}\n, then \n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑥\n𝑖\n)\n,\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑥\n¯\n𝑖\n)\n∈\n{\n𝑇\n,\n𝐹\n}\n and \n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑥\n𝑖\n)\n≠\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑥\n¯\n𝑖\n)\n. Intuitively, this allows us to interpret the color \n𝑇\n as true and the color \n𝐹\n as false. The color \n𝐵\n stands for base.\n\nNext, we define what it means to be a valid coloring. We say that \n𝑓\n is a valid coloring if\n\n• \n\n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑧\n)\n=\n𝑧\n, for all \n𝑧\n∈\n{\n𝑇\n,\n𝐹\n,\n𝐵\n}\n,\n\n• \n\nand the coloring of each clause gadget \n𝐶\n𝑖\n is valid.\n\nWe define what it means that a clause gadget \n𝐶\n𝑖\n has a valid color. By [GJS76, Theorem 2.1], if each literal \nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n1\n,\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n2\n,\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n3\n is colored using the colors \n𝑇\n and \n𝐹\n, then each proper coloring \n𝑓\n of a clause gadget of \n𝐶\n𝑖\n has the property that \n𝑦\n𝑖\n,\n6\n can only be colored with the color \n𝑇\n if at least one literal is colored with \n𝑇\n. Intuitively this means that the gadget simulates a disjunction of the literals \nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n1\n,\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n2\n,\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n3\n. However, if we have an assignment of variables such that \nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n1\n∨\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n2\n∨\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n3\n is true, then they might exist multiple proper colorings \n𝑓\n of the clause gadget \n𝐶\n𝑖\n with \n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑦\n𝑖\n,\n6\n)\n=\n𝑇\n. We avoid this ambiguity by using the following approach.\n\nFor each assignment of the literals \nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n1\n,\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n2\n,\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n3\n (i.e. colorings using \n𝑇\n or \n𝐹\n) such that \nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n1\n∨\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n2\n∨\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n3\n is true, we consider all proper colors \n𝑓\n of the clause gadget with \n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑦\n𝑖\n,\n6\n)\n=\n𝑇\n. Now, we choose one of those proper colors and define it to be a valid coloring. This way, we get exactly 7 valid colors for clause gadgets.\n\nNext, we show that there is a one-to-one mapping between satisfying assignments and colorings that are both proper and valid.\n\nClaim 8.\n\nThe number of satisfying assignments in \n𝜙\n is equal to the number of proper and valid colorings \n𝑓\n:\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝜙\n)\n→\n{\n𝑇\n,\n𝐹\n,\n𝐵\n}\n.\n\n{claimproof}\n\nLet \n𝑓\n be a proper and valid coloring of \n𝐺\n𝜙\n then \n𝑓\n defines a satisfying assignment \n𝜓\n⁡\n(\n𝑓\n)\n of \n𝜙\n by setting the \n𝑖\nth variable to true if and only if \n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑥\n𝑖\n)\n=\n𝑇\n. Since \n𝑓\n is a valid coloring we get that \n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑧\n)\n=\n𝑧\n for \n𝑧\n∈\n{\n𝑇\n,\n𝐹\n,\n𝐵\n}\n. However, this implies \n𝑓\n⁡\n(\n𝑦\n𝑖\n,\n6\n)\n=\n𝑇\n for all clause gadgets \n𝐶\n𝑖\n because \n𝑦\n𝑖\n,\n6\n is connected to the vertices \n𝑇\n and \n𝐹\n. This is only possible if at least one of the literals \nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n1\n,\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n2\n,\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n3\n is colored using \n𝑇\n which implies that \n𝜓\n⁡\n(\n𝑓\n)\n is an assignment that satisfies all clauses \n𝐶\n𝑖\n of \n𝜙\n.\n\nOn the other side, let \n𝐴\n≔\n{\n𝑧\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑧\n𝑛\n}\n be a satisfying assignment of \n𝑓\n𝐴\n. We show that this assignment corresponds to exactly one valid and proper coloring \n𝑓\n𝐴\n. First, we define \n𝑓\n𝐴\n​\n(\n𝑧\n)\n=\n𝑧\n for \n𝑧\n∈\n{\n𝑇\n,\n𝐹\n,\n𝐵\n}\n. Next, we define \n𝑓\n𝐴\n​\n(\n𝑥\n𝑖\n)\n=\n𝑇\n and \n𝑓\n𝐴\n​\n(\n𝑥\n¯\n𝑖\n)\n=\n𝐹\n if \n𝑧\n𝑖\n is true, and \n𝑓\n𝐴\n​\n(\n𝑥\n𝑖\n)\n=\n𝐹\n and \n𝑓\n𝐴\n​\n(\n𝑥\n¯\n𝑖\n)\n=\n𝑇\n if \n𝑧\n𝑖\n is false. Lastly, let \n𝐶\n𝑖\n be a literal. Since \n𝐴\n is satisfying, we get that \nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n1\n∨\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n2\n∨\nℓ\n𝑖\n,\n3\n is true. This means that there exists exactly one valid coloring of \n𝐶\n𝑖\n with the property that \n𝑦\n𝑖\n,\n6\n gets the color \n𝑇\n. We use this coloring to color the vertices \n𝑦\n𝑖\n,\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑦\n𝑖\n,\n6\n. This defines our coloring \n𝑓\n𝐴\n. It is now easy to check that \n𝑓\n𝐴\n is a valid and proper coloring.\n\nObserve that for all colorings \n𝑓\n we get \n𝑓\n𝜓\n⁡\n(\n𝑓\n)\n=\n𝑓\n and for all satisfying assignments \n𝐴\n, we obtain \n𝜓\n⁡\n(\n𝑓\n𝐴\n)\n=\n𝐴\n. This shows that the set of proper and valid colorings is equal to the set of satisfying assignments.\n\nWe show how to compute the number of proper and valid \n3\n-colorings by counting cliques of size \n(\n2\n​\n𝑘\n+\n1\n)\n.\n\nClaim 9.\n\nIf we can solve \n𝑘\n-Clique in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑛\n𝛼\n​\n𝑘\n)\n, then we can solve \n3\n​\n-\nSAT\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑏\n2\n​\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n/\n𝑘\n⋅\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n2\n+\n(\n𝑏\n2\n​\n𝛼\n​\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n)\n)\n where \n𝑏\n=\n3\n9\n.\n\n{claimproof}\n\nFirst, we split the variables and clauses of the formula \n𝜙\n into different groups. For this, we split the variables \n𝑥\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑥\n𝑛\n into \n𝑘\n groups such that each group contains at most \n⌈\n𝑛\n/\n𝑘\n⌉\n many variables. Next, we do the same for the clauses and obtain \n𝑘\n groups of clauses such that each group contains at most \n⌈\n𝑚\n/\n𝑘\n⌉\n many clauses. We use this to define \n2\n​\n𝑘\n+\n1\n many groups on \n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝜙\n)\n:\n\n• \n\nWe define one group containing the vertices \n𝑇\n, \n𝐹\n, and \n𝐵\n.\n\n• \n\nEach group of variables \n𝑥\n𝑎\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑥\n𝑎\n𝑠\n defines a group of literal vertices \n𝑥\n𝑎\n1\n,\n𝑥\n¯\n𝑎\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑥\n𝑎\n𝑠\n,\n𝑥\n¯\n𝑎\n𝑠\n.\n\n• \n\nEach group of clauses \n𝐶\n𝑏\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝐶\n𝑏\n𝑠\n defines a group of clause gadget vertices. The vertices of this group are precisely those vertices that are part of some clause gadget \n𝐶\n𝑏\n𝑖\n (see Figure 2(b)).\n\nNote that a vertex \n𝑥\n𝑖\n∈\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n𝜙\n)\n might be contained in multiple groups. We use \n𝑉\n1\n,\n…\n,\n𝑉\n2\n​\n𝑘\n+\n1\n to enumerate the groups. Observe that \n|\n𝑉\n𝑖\n|\n≤\n9\n​\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n/\n𝑘\n. Next, we create a graph \n𝐺\n~\n𝜙\n in the following.\n\nThe idea is that the vertices of \n𝐺\n~\n𝜙\n represent valid colorings of subgraphs of \n𝐺\n𝜙\n, meaning that each vertex of \n𝐺\n~\n𝜙\n has the form \n(\n𝑉\n𝑖\n,\n𝑓\n𝑖\n)\n, where \n𝑓\n𝑖\n is a valid and proper coloring of \n𝑉\n𝑖\n. For the group \n{\n𝑇\n,\n𝐹\n,\n𝐵\n}\n, we create a single vertex with the coloring \n𝑓\n𝑖\n​\n(\n𝑧\n)\n=\n𝑧\n. Next, let \n𝑉\n𝑖\n be a group of literal vertices then we create a vertex \n(\n𝑉\n𝑖\n,\n𝑓\n)\n for each valid and proper coloring \n𝑓\n𝑖\n on \n𝑉\n𝑖\n meaning that \n𝑓\n𝑖\n can only use colorers in \n{\n𝑇\n,\n𝐹\n}\n. Lastly, let \n𝑉\n𝑖\n be a group of clause gadget vertices, then we create a vertex \n(\n𝑉\n𝑖\n,\n𝑓\n𝑖\n)\n for each valid and proper coloring \n𝑓\n𝑖\n, meaning that \n𝑓\n𝑖\n is a valid color of each clause gadget \n𝐶\n𝑗\n that is part of the group \n𝑉\n𝑖\n. Note that \n𝑉\n𝑖\n either contains all vertices of a clause gadget or no vertex of a clause gadget. Observe that \n𝐺\n~\n𝜙\n has at most \n(\n2\n​\n𝑘\n+\n1\n)\n⋅\n3\n9\n​\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n/\n𝑘\n=\n(\n2\n​\n𝑘\n+\n1\n)\n⋅\n𝑏\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n/\n𝑘\n many vertices, where \n𝑏\n≔\n3\n9\n.\n\nNext, we add an edge between \n(\n𝑉\n𝑖\n,\n𝑓\n𝑖\n)\n and \n(\n𝑉\n𝑗\n,\n𝑓\n𝑗\n)\n if \n𝑖\n≠\n𝑗\n, \n𝑓\n𝑖\n​\n(\n𝑥\n)\n=\n𝑓\n𝑗\n​\n(\n𝑥\n)\n for all \n𝑥\n∈\n𝑉\n𝑖\n∩\n𝑉\n𝑗\n, and \n𝑓\n is a proper \n3\n-coloring of \n𝐺\n𝜙\n​\n(\n𝑉\n𝑖\n∪\n𝑉\n𝑗\n)\n, where \n𝑓\n is defined as \n𝑓\n​\n(\n𝑥\n)\n=\n𝑓\n𝑖\n​\n(\n𝑥\n)\n if \n𝑥\n∈\n𝑉\n𝑖\n and \n𝑓\n​\n(\n𝑥\n)\n=\n𝑓\n𝑗\n​\n(\n𝑥\n)\n if \n𝑥\n∈\n𝑉\n𝑗\n. It is easy to see that can construct the graph \n𝐺\n~\n𝜙\n in time\n\n\t\n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n(\n(\n2\n​\n𝑘\n+\n1\n)\n⋅\n𝑏\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n/\n𝑘\n)\n2\n⋅\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n2\n)\n\t\n\nFurther, it is easy to see that each proper and valid coloring of \n𝐺\n𝜙\n corresponds to exactly one \n(\n2\n​\n𝑘\n+\n1\n)\n-clique in \n𝐺\n~\n𝜙\n. Assume next that we can solve \n𝑘\n-clique in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑛\n𝛼\n​\n𝑘\n)\n, then we can compute the number of satisfying assignments of \n𝜙\n in time\n\n\t\n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n(\n(\n2\n​\n𝑘\n+\n1\n)\n⋅\n𝑏\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n/\n𝑘\n)\n2\n⋅\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n2\n+\n(\n(\n2\n​\n𝑘\n+\n1\n)\n⋅\n𝑏\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n/\n𝑘\n)\n𝛼\n⁡\n(\n2\n​\n𝑘\n+\n1\n)\n)\n\t\n\nFor a fixed \n𝑘\n, this time is in \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑏\n2\n​\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n/\n𝑘\n⋅\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n2\n+\n(\n𝑏\n2\n​\n𝛼\n​\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n)\n)\n.\n\nLet \n𝛿\n>\n0\n be constant from Corollary B.2. We set \n𝛼\n≔\nlog\n𝑏\n⁡\n(\n2\n)\n​\n𝛿\n/\n3\n. If there is an \n𝑘\n≥\n1\n/\n𝛼\n such that we can solve \n𝑘\n-Clique in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑛\n𝛼\n​\n𝑘\n)\n, then we use Claim 9 to solve \n3\n​\n-\nSAT\n in time\n\n\t\n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑏\n2\n​\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n/\n𝑘\n⋅\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n2\n+\n(\n𝑏\n2\n​\n𝛼\n​\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n)\n)\n⊆\n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑏\n2\n​\n𝛼\n​\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n⋅\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n2\n)\n⊆\n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n2\n2\n​\n𝛿\n​\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n/\n3\n⋅\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n2\n)\n⊆\n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n2\n𝛿\n⁡\n(\n𝑛\n+\n𝑚\n)\n)\n.\n\t\n\nHowever, according to Corollary B.2 this is only possible if ETH fails. If \n𝑘\n<\n1\n/\n𝛼\n then \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n𝑛\n𝛼\n​\n𝑘\n)\n would be sublinear and there is no sublinear algorithm that reads the whole input.\n\nNow, Theorem B.4 gives us tight lower bounds for \nUnique\n-\n​\n𝑘\n​\n-\nClique\n, which immediately yields tight lower bounds for Section 6.\n\n\\corcliquemodeth\nProof B.6.\n\nLet \n𝛼\n be the constant from Theorem B.4. Assume that we have an algorithm that decides if the number of \n𝑘\n-cliques is divisiable by \n𝑝\n in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛼\n​\n𝑘\n)\n then this algorithm can be used to solve Unique-Clique in time \n𝑂\n⁡\n(\n|\n𝑉\n⁡\n(\n𝐺\n)\n|\n𝛼\n​\n𝑘\n)\n. However, this is not possible unless rETH fails.\n\nLastly, we show that \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nClique\n and \n#\n​\nClique\n𝑝\n are equivalent under parameterized Turing reductions\n\nLemma B.7.\n\nThe problems \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nClique\n and \n#\n𝑝\n-Clique are equivalent under parameterized Turing reductions.\n\nProof B.8.\n\nLet \n𝐴\n be an algorithm that computes \n#\n​\nClique\n𝑝\n. On input \n(\n𝐺\n,\n𝑘\n)\n, we use the algorithm to compute the number of \n𝑘\n-cliques modulo \n𝑝\n. If this number is zero, then we return true. Otherwise, we return false. This algorithm decides if the number \n𝑘\n-cliques is divisible by \n𝑘\n.\n\nOn the other side, let \n𝐴\n be an algorithm that decides \nMOD\n𝑝\n​\nClique\n. For a given input \n(\n𝐺\n,\n𝑘\n)\n, let \n𝐺\n𝑖\n be the graph that we get by adding \n𝑖\n many \n𝑘\n-cliques to \n𝐺\n. Let \n𝑥\n be the number of \n𝑘\n-cliques in \n𝐺\n, then observe that the number of \n𝑘\n-cliques in \n𝐺\n𝑖\n is equal to \n𝑥\n+\n1\n since we get \n𝑥\n many \n𝑘\n-cliques in \n𝐺\n plus one \n𝑘\n-clique for each \n𝑘\n-clique that we add to \n𝐺\n. Thus \n𝐺\n𝑖\n contains \n𝑥\n+\n𝑖\n many \n𝑘\n-cliques. We now simply run \n𝐴\n on input \n(\n𝐺\n0\n,\n𝑘\n)\n, \n(\n𝐺\n1\n,\n𝑘\n)\n, \n…\n \n(\n𝐺\n𝑝\n−\n1\n,\n𝑘\n)\n. Observe that \n𝐴\n returns true exactly once and let \n𝐺\n𝑖\n be this graph. This means that \n𝑥\n+\n𝑖\n≡\n𝑝\n0\n which implies that \n𝑥\n≡\n𝑝\n−\n𝑖\n. Thus we simply return \n−\n𝑖\n+\n𝑝\n which is equal to the number of \n𝑘\n-cliques in \n𝐺\n modulo \n𝑝\n.\n\nExperimental support, please view the build logs for errors.
